What you must not do is stay *unconscious* of your choice, because then the story will fight itself: a Fichtean engine in a three-act template feels rushed at the start and padded in the middle, which is the most common structural sickness this chapter exists to prevent.

A concrete orientation pair, before we move on. Weak opening work: *"It was a rainy Tuesday in Bellhaven, a town with a complicated history, when Margaret received a letter she didn't expect."* — no protagonist yet (Margaret is a name, not a person in a defining situation), no texture of *her* life, no disturbance that costs anything (an unexpected letter is a genre sticker). Same information, oriented: *"Margaret had opened the shop alone for eleven months, and she had just locked up when the letter slid under the door — her sister's handwriting, which meant their mother was dead or dying, and the shop's only delivery was due at six."* — who (a woman who runs a shop alone and counts on it), where (her shop, her solitary routine, specified by 'eleven months' and 'six'), what's wrong (a sister's handwriting that only carries two meanings). Three jobs, one sentence-cluster, no history of Bellhaven required. That ratio — specificity over coverage — is the whole orientation craft.

## The one-page story skeleton

Everything above now condenses into one page — a document you will fill by hand for every story, because the act of fitting your idea into it *is* the testing. Use this template verbatim (a printable copy lives with the companion materials; retype it if you prefer — retype it once and you'll never forget the fields):

```text
STORY SKELETON — [working title]                          v[__]

1. PROTAGONIST: name, age, situation, defining trait (one sentence).
2. WANT (external, checkable): what they are trying to GET or DO.
3. NEED (internal, usually opposite to the want): what would actually
   heal or teach them.  [Ch 3 develops this field.]
4. OBSTACLE: the force welded to the want — who or what says no,
   and why it must be THIS obstacle for THIS person.
5. STAKES SENTENCE: If they fail, [personal] — and worse, [relational/
   existential].
6. STRUCTURE CHOICE: three-act | Fichtean | kishōtenketsu (one line
   of why).
7. DISTURBANCE: the event that breaks the defining situation.
8. POINT OF NO RETURN: the choice (not accident) that locks them in.
9. CRISIS/MIDPOINT: the worst-pressure moment; what changes after it.
10. CLIMAX PREVIEW: the collision the ending must contain — one
    sentence, no choreography.
11. ENDING PREVIEW: the closing image or action, one sentence.
12. POV & TENSE: close third past | first present | … (Ch 4 locks this.)
```

Twelve fields, one page, no scene numbering — deliberately. The skeleton is a *premise-level* document: it proves the story can stand up before you spend months leaning on it. Beat-level outlining is Chapter 9's job, with the machine's help; doing it now would be like drawing floor plans for a house with no foundation.

**How long should drafting the page take?** Fifteen to forty minutes for a first pass, then a day of background simmering, then one revision pass. If a field refuses to fill — if you cannot write the stakes sentence or preview a climax — that refusal is diagnostic: the story is telling you where it is soft, before it costs you 40,000 words.

### The stakes sentence: a drill and its sub-drills

The stakes sentence gets its own practice block because it carries the outline's weight and *fails* in ways naming helps. **Drill 1 — the cost-of-failure ladder run, aloud:** take any draft stakes sentence and climb it three rungs by asking the *specific* follow-up each time (the pattern from the chapter's stakes section, compressed to a script): "The sale fails → she loses *what exactly?* — the house (rung 1: personal/property); → and *who else* is affected? — Theo gains a sibling who resents him for gaining (rung 2: relational — note it cuts *toward* the survivor too, the ladder's usual direction); → and what story about herself stops being tellable? — 'the one who kept things' dies with the keys (rung 3: existential — the *self-story as infrastructure* line from the ladder table)." **The drill's checkpoint:** if any rung arrives in *abstractions* ("her identity," "the family legacy") rather than *pictures* (the keys, the ledger, the notice on the door), the rung is fake — redo it until you could *photograph* the loss. **Drill 2 — the swap test:** paste your stakes sentence over a *different* story's premise-summary (any thriller's, any romance's) — if it still "works" (no edits needed), it's **template stakes**: the tell is generic loss-language ("everything she's worked for," "more than just a job") that any protagonist could wear; fix by re-running Drill 1 on *your* character's actual rungs until the sentence *can't* leave its own story (swap test fails = specific). **Drill 3 — the skeptic's one question** (the stake-sentence's PC-08 preview): read it to someone (or play skeptic yourself) and take *only this* question: "why doesn't she just…?" — every "just" is a **missing obstacle-justification** (she can't walk away because *what*, specifically? — the loan terms, the notary's letter, Theo's silence — whatever *binds* her is the stakes sentence's hidden fourth element surfacing under pressure); if the honest answer is "nothing, she could just," **you don't have stakes yet — you have a situation**, and the chapter's earlier reframe ("bad premise = situation without checkable goal AND/OR stakes without binding cost") just told you *which* half broke. **Drill 4 — the compression round** (runs only after Drills 1–3 pass): three sentences → two → one, *each cut tested for rung-loss* ("did that clause carry a rung? — if yes, restore it: the sentence is already as short as its *load-bearing* parts allow"). The four drills in sequence = the sentence's full quality-assurance: ladder (substance), swap (specificity), skeptic (binding), compression (craft) — and the whole sequence takes eleven minutes on a first draft of the sentence, which is eleven minutes against the outline's *most-quoted, least-inspected* line item: the line beta readers will later quote *back* to you as "the reason I cared" — worth the drill, every time.

### Where premises actually go to die (five funnels, five fixes)

A field guide to the chapter's promise ("premises die in specific ways") drawn from years of workshop outcomes — each funnel with its *signature* and the stage's built-in fix, so you recognize which cliff you're approaching *before* the view changes:

**Funnel 1 — the enthusiasm cliff.** Signature: premise written on a high (late-night energy, one great image), skeleton started next week, first gap between *vision* and *draft-reality* discovered — and the project quietly ends not with a rejection but with *no sessions booked*. Root cause: **the emotional high got recorded as a *decision* without a *capacity check*** (the four-part's "can this survive my actual life?" question skipped — high-energy sessions are *terrible* capacity judges; they over-promise on behalf of your tired self). Fix already in the machinery: **PC-08's capacity-check row + the no-exceptions entry conditions from Part III's opening** (a premise that survives the stress-test has *signed* for the tired version of you — the check-4-constraints work double duty: it protects the *outline* and protects *enthusiasm from its own amnesia*). Prevention habit: never let a night's excitement mint anything above `parked` — *parking is the enthusiasm-capacity handshake* (the file's itch-flag + re-rate rule is exactly this funnel's speed bump, timed for the month when memory has softened the high).

**Funnel 2 — the research swamp.** Signature: skeleton exists, but sessions convert *one-by-one* into "just looking things up" — the project becomes a reading list with a title page; deliverable count approaches zero while `research/` grows fat. Root cause: **research-as-avoidance wearing research-as-craft's clothes** (the writing-session's resistance gets *licensed* by genuinely-useful information-gathering — the swamp's cruelty: the swamp-feel good, *look* responsible, and the *draft file stays cold*; also the generator-raw-material trap in costume — the tool's eager research-assist makes the swamp *faster to enter and harder to notice*). Fix already in the machinery: **Ch 5's type-split at scheduling level** (research questions get *slots* — a ninety-minute weekly block, not *every* session's first tab) + **the why-me/why-now-research protocol's bounded form** (perishables get dated, *closed* questions — "the harbor authority's 2024 staffing rules: two primary sources, done Friday" — research ends when *the question* ends, not when curiosity retires — one *bounded* question per swamp-day, then the draft's FILL slots get fed from what arrived). Prevention signature: if `research/` added three files this week and `manuscript/` added zero paragraphs, the audit's *ratio* line fires — the same instrument logic as word-counts-without-adjudications: **motion without output in the *target* channel is the swamp's humidity reading.**

**Funnel 3 — the shiny-new-premise diffusion.** Signature: three projects' *ideation-stage* tabs open in rotation; each session's energy split three ways; *all three* progress at the pace of "almost started" — the parked-file's discipline violated *upward* (premises not parked but *kept warm* — the diffusion's vanity: "I'm working on three things" usually means "I'm *maintaining* three almost-things"); root cause: **the one-DEVELOP-verdict-at-a-time rule from Ch 8 flouted because *ideation sessions don't feel like work*** (the lab's serial-verdicts rule exists precisely because *wandering is parallel but finishing is serial* — two active skeletons in `manuscript/` = two voices in the sessions, each contaminating the other's constraint banks until both drafts sound like *neither*); fix already in the machinery: **Ch 8's five-questions answer #5** ("verdicts are serial; wandering is parallel" — the second premise asking for a stress-test *has asked its rival to park*) + **the itch re-rating ritual** (unwanted warmth for parked items dies at monthly review pace; kept-warm premises *never* get that review — they just *stay warm in the calendar's shadow*, consuming Sunday-audit attention that belongs to the one ACTIVE); prevention signature: **the folder test** — `manuscript/` contains ONE project's `draft-current` *or* the project in most-recent-mint position, full stop; a second skeleton in the same folder is the diffusion's *first symptom* — move it to `parked-ideas.md`'s care before the next session, not after the quarter's self-reproach.

**Funnel 4 — the never-quite-ready polish-loop.** Signature: *v8 outline*, *v14 character sheets*, beat-lists redrafted for weeks — **zero words of prose committed** — the project's revision-axis achievements mounting while its *time-to-first-page* metric sits at infinity; root cause: **perfectionism colonizing the pre-draft stages** (outline-worship's cousin — the *document* gets polished because documents can be polished *without risking the draft*, and the draft risks everything: the moment "discovery through drafting" dies, so does the fun; also the feedback-loop-absence problem: sheets have no *reader* to disappoint, and the ego's risk-aversion *smells* the safety); fix already in the machinery: **Part III's no-exceptions entry conditions + Ch 12's lesson previewed early** (the *entry* conditions' design: outline's stake-sentence checkable, sheets' fields 1/3/5/6 filled, level-3 beats for *session one* — three boxes, *then the gate closes*: entry conditions exist to be *passed*, not *polished* — a v8 outline with zero mints is a machine whose *only* output is self-assurance) + **the deadlines-hidden-in-reality reframe** (two-week sale clock in the *story* — build yourself one: a beta-reader's date, a class's submission window, a contest's — *external dates are the polish-loop's fire alarm* — schedule it the day the outline hits v2, before comfort negotiates it later). Prevention signature: **the no-prose-week alert** (session logs showing two consecutive weeks of bible-work only → the audit auto-flags it — instrumentation again: the *metric* isn't "outline quality," it's "words-at-draft-stage per week > 0" — a number your logs already track, waiting for you to *treat it as the health reading it always was*).

**Funnel 5 — the mid-draft collapse of belief.** Signature (the cruelest — it strikes *after* the entry conditions): Act II's sag arrives *experientially* — the writer stops believing *their own stakes sentence* mid-draft; sessions slow to bargaining ("I'll finish if it's good"); the outline's promises start reading like *lies you told yourself*; root cause: **the stakes' *initial* substance was real but *un-rehearsed under draft-pressure*** (the sentence's rungs were checked in *outline-mood*, not *draft-mood* — and Part III's middle is where every writer discovers which of their rungs were *authored* versus *assembled* — the gap the Ch 12 "belief-check: does the promise still feel true" line item exists to catch at pass-1, but *some writers meet it first* in week six of drafting, unbuffered); fix already in the machinery: **re-run the chapter's Drill 1 on the *draft's* current state** (the ladder works mid-draft — "the sale fails → what, now that I've written ch1–6? — what *specifically* have I promised the reader by page 140 that the failure would destroy? — THAT's the stakes sentence's current rung-set — usually *sharper* than the outline's original, because the draft made promises the pre-draft sentence couldn't know") + **the F-07 diagnosis's sister question** ("am I doubting the *premise* or doubting my *draft-rewards*? — if the former: the outline needs a v-next *repair* (legitimate, logged); if the latter: the doubt belongs to *altitude-confusion* — structure-pass now, line-pass later, one job at a time — belief in the *project* survives the *pass* schedule when the schedule's kept, and dies *inside* the schedule when each pass' jobs get *stacked onto every session*"). The chapter's closing line, earned twice now: **the premise that survives drafting is the one whose costs were *sized to your own capacity* — every funnel above is some variant of that sizing missing; the drills, checks, and entry conditions aren't bureaucracy — they're the sizing tools.**

## Worked example: Example A's skeleton, v1

Let's build the skeleton for the running example that will carry Part III. **Example A** is a literary-contemporary short story, "Low Tide," which you will watch move from premise (Ch 8) to outline (Ch 9) to drafted scenes (Ch 10–11) to revision (Ch 12) to beta feedback (Ch 13). Filling the skeleton now — unaided, before any AI enters the room — is the book practicing what it preaches: the aims exist first, so that later the machine proposes against them rather than instead of them.

```text
STORY SKELETON — "Low Tide"                               v1

1. PROTAGONIST: Mara Solano, 36, hydrographic surveyor who compiles
   tide tables for a maritime authority; treats every problem —
   grief included — as logistics to be managed.
2. WANT: sell the family house on the cliff in Porto Alvaro and
   return to her city job within two weeks.
3. NEED: stay long enough to grieve her mother WITH her brother —
   and admit why she stopped coming home.
4. OBSTACLE: her brother Theo refuses to sell and won't explain why;
   the buyer (Casi Duarte) needs the deal before the season;
   a February storm keeps everyone inside the same house.
5. STAKES SENTENCE: If Mara forces the sale, she gets her clean
   exit — and loses the last chance to be a sister to Theo; if she
   stalls too long, she loses the survey posting that is the life
   she chose instead.
6. STRUCTURE: three-act — the house and the storm give me a natural
   pressure chamber; Fichtean would rush the grief.
7. DISTURBANCE: the notary's letter — probate requires both signatures
   by month's end — arrives the same day Mara does.
8. POINT OF NO RETURN: Mara finds their mother's tide journal and
   realizes Theo has been reading it for months — she can't leave
   without knowing what's in it.
9. CRISIS/MIDPOINT: power dies in the storm; by flashlight the
   brothers' fight surfaces the real story: their mother set a third
   place at the table for years, waiting; Mara's unanswered calls.
10. CLIMAX PREVIEW: alone in the kitchen with the journal and Casi's
    contract, Mara must sign or not sign — with Theo watching from
    the doorway.
11. ENDING PREVIEW: Mara hands Theo the keys to keep — she's taking
    the survey posting, but she's coming back for the equinox; the
    tide table for March hangs unfinished on the wall.
12. POV/TENSE: close third past, Mara only.
```

**Annotated: where each element came from.**

- **Fields 1–3 arrived together** as one image: a woman whose *job* is predicting the sea's schedule, unable to schedule her own grief. The profession was chosen *because* it rhymes with the flaw (control over chaos) — when your protagonist's job and wound echo, half your symbolism is pre-installed. Field 3's NEED is already in tension with field 2's WANT; that tension is the story's engine (Chapter 3 builds it formally).
- **Field 4 stacks three obstacles** — human (Theo), clock (Casi's deadline), weather (the storm) — so pressure has redundancy. Note none is a mustache-twirler: Theo's refusal is love without vocabulary; Casi is business. Literary stories die when the obstacle is cosmically evil and survive when everyone is understandable and wrong.
- **Field 5 names both directions of failure.** The stakes sentence has a *both-ways-lose* shape, which is what makes the climax a decision rather than a tally. "The life she chose instead" quietly plants field 3: her city posting isn't neutral background, it's the counter-bet.
- **Field 8 is a discovery, not an accident** — Mara *chooses* to open the journal (curiosity she can't log away), so the point of no return preserves agency, as the pattern requires.
- **Fields 10–11 preview without choreography.** "Sign or not sign, with Theo watching" defines the collision; the unfinished tide table as closing image ties the profession-symbol to the resolution — she leaves the schedule open, the anti-logistics ending. Whether the short story earns that image is now a *testable question*, which is exactly what a skeleton is for.
- **What's deliberately missing:** scene order, subplots, dialogue, the mother's journal contents. Premise-level fields only. The machine never sees this document until Chapter 8, where it will stress-test it; if it survives *that*, Chapter 9 turns it into beats.

### Skeletons at short lengths

Everything above assumes room to move — a novel or a full short story. What about flash fiction, or the 1,200-word story a magazine will actually consider from a beginner? The fields do not disappear; they *compress into single decisions*. The four-part premise becomes one sentence that already contains its own stakes ("Mara has two weeks to sell the house her brother refuses to leave"). The structure choice collapses to a single turn: many flash stories are, in effect, a kishōtenketsu *ten* in miniature — a reframing so fast it feels like a cut — or a three-act shape with each act reduced to a paragraph. Fields 10 and 11 (climax preview, ending preview) become nearly the same sentence, because at short lengths the last line *is* the collision's residue.

The discipline that never compresses: **the cost-of-failure sentence** and **the choice at the climax**. If your 800-word story cannot name both, it is a vignette — a moment with a mood, which is a legitimate thing to write, but not yet a story with an ending you designed. Label it honestly in your notes ("vignette on purpose") rather than discovering the distinction from a rejection letter. And do fill *something*: even flash gets a five-field skeleton — protagonist, want, obstacle, turn, residue. The fifteen-minute habit scales down to four minutes; only the page count shrinks.

One more short-form caution: compressed structures rely on the reader *supplying* connective tissue — implied wants, assumed stakes, relationships entered mid-argument. At novel length you control that supply; at flash length you are betting on the reader's generosity. Choose generic situations carefully (a fight in a kitchen reads as "siblings" faster than a fight in a customs hall), because every field you cannot state explicitly must be instantly guessable from the situation itself. When the Stretch asks you to skeleton your story, write the length you are actually aiming at — the skeleton for a 2,000-word story and the skeleton for a 90,000-word novel share fields, not sizes.

> CRAFT NOTE — Skeletons are cheap; drafts are expensive. Writers resist fifteen-minute documents because the story feels more magical unfilled. The magic argument has it backwards: every experienced writer you admire is, underneath the mystique, someone who tested cheaply and often. Fill the page. If the page is boring, you have lost half an hour. If you skip it and the novel stalls at 30,000 words, you have lost a year.

### When a field refuses to fill

Every stalled skeleton has a diagnosis. The refusals cluster in three fields, and each one means something specific — read the failure as a message rather than a mood.

**Field 2 (want) stays vague.** "She wants a better life" will not fill because it is not a want; it is a *result* of wants. Force the checkable version: what would a camera record as success — the signed contract, the verdict, the ticket bought? If no camera-checkable want exists, your story may be a mood rather than a plot, and that is a legitimate literary choice *only on purpose*. Say so in field 6 ("character study; want is non-external by design") and stop fighting the field.

**Field 5 (stakes sentence) stays flat.** You are probably stuck on one rung. Writers in love with their plot often cannot name a relational loss because no relationship is loaded yet — fix by adding one (the daughter, the sister, the crew) *before* the outline stage, because retrofitting relationships in Chapter 12 is surgery with the patient awake. Writers in love with their themes often cannot name a personal loss because the protagonist is a symbol wearing a name — fix by giving them a body, a livelihood, and one embarrassing daily routine that failure would take away.

**Field 10 (climax preview) stays vague.** "They confront the truth" is a theme, not a collision. A climax preview needs parties and a choice: *who* is in the room, *what* binary decision stands before them, and *what* each choice costs. If you can fill field 10 only after inventing a new character or event, your middle was missing its engine all along — good to know now; Chapter 9 will rebuild Act II around the collision the climax actually requires.

There is no shame in a skeleton that takes three passes; there is only expensive shame in a skeleton never attempted. The right response to a refusal is ten more minutes, not twelve more weeks of hopeful drifting.

## Exercises

- **Try (15 min):** Ten premises in twenty minutes (start your timer; ugly is fine). Pick one seed word — a place, a job, a fear — and generate ten one-sentence premises that each contain all four parts. Force different genres across the ten. Do not stop to judge; judging happens after the timer. Then circle the two that produce the sharpest cost-of-failure sentences. Quantity first works because it demotes your first idea (usually the generic one) and forces the search past it — the same principle behind every ideation prompt in Chapter 8.

- **Stretch (1–2 h):** Take your best premise from the Try and build its full stakes ladder: write the personal rung, the relational rung, and the existential rung as three separate "If they fail…" sentences; choose the strongest pairing for your stakes sentence. Then fill all twelve skeleton fields for your story. Walk away for thirty minutes; return and revise only fields 4, 5, and 10 — the obstacle, the stakes, the climax preview. If any of the three still feels soft after the second pass, swap the premise now, while it costs a page instead of a book.

- **Project (ongoing):** Skeleton an old favorite film — from memory, no pausing, no looking up. One page, all twelve fields, ten minutes. Compare your filled fields against the actual film: where did your memory *invent* structure? This drill installs the shape in reflexes faster than any diagram; repeat it with a short story you love after Chapter 4, and with a friend's anecdote at a coffee shop. Within a month, you will hear premises differently at parties — the fields fill in automatically, and weak ones stop surviving your attention.

## Pitfalls

> PITFALL — **Mistaking plot for story.** *Symptom:* you can list what happens — the letters, the trial, the heist — but the summary bores you when said aloud; scenes accrete without the reader caring which way it goes; your stakes sentence comes out as events ("things fall apart") rather than losses ("he loses X, and worse, Y"). *Why it happens:* events are visible and therefore feel like progress; costs are invisible and therefore get deferred — often forever, into a sequel, into backstory, into "the reader will sense it." *Fix:* run the ladder on your existing events. For each major event, write who pays, in what currency (personal, relational, existential), and by how much; delete or merge events that pay nothing. Then rewrite the stakes sentence from the payments, not the happenings. *Prevention:* the skeleton's field 5 is required, never blank; in Chapter 9 every beat outline must state which rung each beat moves. A story is not what happens; it is what happens *to whom it matters*.

Related failure for Appendix C: scenes that feel busy but weightless — symptom feeds F-03 (draft-structure mismatches) when left unaddressed.

## Checklist

> CHECKLIST — You're ready to move on when:
> - [ ] You can state a premise in two sentences containing all four parts — protagonist, goal, obstacle, stakes.
> - [ ] Your stakes sentence has both clauses (failure costs *this*, and worse, *that*).
> - [ ] A one-page skeleton exists — for Example A as modeled, and for your own story after the Stretch.
> - [ ] You can name your protagonist's cost of failure without rereading your notes.
> - [ ] You chose a structure on purpose (three-act / Fichtean / kishōtenketsu), and can say in one line why.

## Key takeaways

- **Idea versus premise:** an idea names a possibility; a premise states a conflict with a specific protagonist, a checkable goal, a welded obstacle, and concrete stakes. The cost-of-failure sentence is the test.
- **Stakes are consequences, not volume.** Climb the ladder — personal → relational → existential — by asking "and then who else pays?" Specificity of loss beats pyrotechnics every time.
- **Three structures are proven designs, not laws:** three-act for pressure-chamber stories, Fichtean when crises must escalate from page one, kishōtenketsu when the engine is reframing rather than conflict. Train inside one; bend it later.
- **The one-page skeleton is the chapter's deliverable** and the workflow's foundation: twelve fields, premise-level only, with the obstacle, stakes, and climax preview carrying the most weight.
- **Test cheaply, test early.** A soft field discovered now costs minutes; the same soft field discovered in Chapter 12 costs months. Fill the page.


# Chapter 03 — Character Fundamentals: Desire, Flaw, Arc

## Opening hook

Here are two versions of the same character. Both have a flaw. Only one has a story.

**Version one:** Detective Ray Ferrara is *impatient* — he interrupts people, taps his pen, hates waiting. The flaw appears on every page: barked replies, a ticking watch. At the climax he... is still impatient. He solves the case with a burst of impatient brilliance. The reader closes the book unchanged, because a flaw that never *costs* the character anything is not a flaw; it is a personality condiment, shaken on for texture.

**Version two:** Ray cannot admit he needs anyone — the same energy as impatience, aimed somewhere lethal. Because he won't ask his old partner for the sealed file, the wrong man walks. Because he won't tell his daughter why the retirement dinner matters, she doesn't come. Because he won't admit the "Rook" years were his choice and not just the job, the killer he hunts is the identity he protects. At the climax, the flaw *is* the obstacle: the case is solvable only if Ray does the one thing his flaw forbids — say *I need this from you*, on tape, in front of everyone. The flaw has grown teeth, connected to plot like rebar to concrete, and the story ends differently because of it.

The difference between the versions is not depth of psychology; it is **causality**. Version two's flaw runs a chain: flaw → choices → consequences → climax. That chain is this chapter's first craft target. Around it we build the other two pillars of character fundamentals: the **want/need split** (what the character pursues versus what would actually save them) and the **arc** (how the ordeal changes them — or fails to). By the end you will fill a character sheet that already contains the skeleton of your plot, because with real characters, structure is what you get for free.

## Learning objectives

By the end of this chapter, you will be able to:

- [ ] Separate a character's want from their need.
- [ ] Give a character a flaw that causally connects to the climax (the chain test).
- [ ] Choose an arc type — positive, negative, or flat — deliberately.
- [ ] Fill a character sheet for your protagonist, with voice notes.

## Want vs. need

The want/need split is the most portable idea in modern character craft: most published formulations of it descend from the same mid-century screenwriting and writing-teaching lineage, and its core claim is stable across schools — **characters pursue what they think will fix them and require what would actually fix them, and the gap between the two generates plot.** Call it consensus-with-variations; you do not need the lineage, you need the working definitions.

**The want** is external, concrete, and verifiable on the page: the case closed, the house sold, the funding won, the ticket to leave town. Wants are what characters *do*. They give your story direction (Chapter 2's goal field) and they are how readers track progress: every scene can be checked against "is the protagonist closer to the want?"

**The need** is internal and almost always the want's opposite or its price: Ray needs to need people *as he* wants to close the case without needing anyone. Mara (Example A) wants to sell the house and leave — her need is to *stay long enough to be present* for her brother. The need is what the character cannot give themselves in Chapter 1 by declaration; it has to be extracted by events, usually at the climax's cost.

**Three tests to keep the split honest:**

1. *The one-line test.* Can you state want and need in one line each, without using the same verb? "Wants to sell the house; needs to keep it" passes only if you can say what the keeping *means* (presence, roots, repair of the abandonment). "Wants to sell; needs closure" fails — "closure" is fog.
2. *The plot test.* Does the want drive scenes (she calls the notary, she packs boxes) while the need is violated by her behavior in those very scenes (each packed box is another refusal to sit with Theo)? If want and need move together without friction, you have two wants.
3. *The climax test.* At the climax, does the character have to *choose* between want-satisfaction and need-satisfaction — or pay the want to gain the need? If the climax lets them have both at no cost, the split was decoration. (Bittersweet and costly endings fall straight out of this test; so do the great "wins the battle, loses the war" reversals.)

> CRAFT NOTE — **Without therapy-speak.** Character craft borrows psychology's vocabulary constantly, and beginner pages fill with characters who are "in denial," "processing trauma," "avoidant-attachment," "finally healing." Two cautions. First, clinical labels *summarize*; fiction must *dramatize*: we don't need Ray diagnosed as avoidant, we need him not making the phone call while the phone sits on the table. Second, labels invite symmetry — every flaw tidied into a category — and tidy categories are where originality goes to die. Let your characters be *specifically* odd: not "controlling" but the woman who alphabetizes the spice rack and then lies about small things to avoid arguments. Write the behavior; let the reader do the diagnosing. If your draft's character notes read like a intake form, delete three adjectives and add two actions.

**Wants and needs across types.** In genre engines, the want often *is* the plot (find the killer, win the tournament) while the need supplies the theme (learn to trust a partner again; decide what winning costs). In literary work, the inversion is common: the want is modest and plausible (sell the house; deliver the meal) while the need carries the weight (forgive, stay, confess). Neither arrangement is superior; what fails in both is *absence* — a protagonist with only a want is a machine for errands, and one with only a need is a mood waiting for a plot to happen to.

### Three splits, taken apart

Abstract tests stick better when run on live material, so here are three complete want/need splits — one genre, one literary, one from Example B's science-fiction register — each annotated for what makes the friction productive.

**Split 1 — Ray Ferrara (detective, negative-arc potential):** *Want:* close one clean case before forced retirement. *Need:* admit the "Rook" years were chosen, not imposed — ask for help without managing the ask. Friction analysis: the want's methods (solo legwork, controlled information, no debts) are precisely the need's violations; every scene that advances the case can therefore *simultaneously* deepen the wound — the plot and the character problem share one engine, which is why the structure will feel inevitable rather than bolted. Climax test preview: solvable only by the public admission the flaw forbids.

**Split 2 — Mara Solano (Example A, literary):** *Want:* sell the house in two weeks. *Need:* stay long enough to grieve alongside Theo. Friction analysis: her want's successful execution *is* the need's failure — every box packed is an absence made physical — so the story cannot merely stall her; it must make the logistics themselves suspect. The November schedules, the contract deadlines, the survey posting: her instruments become temptations. Note the inversion quality — the two pulls point opposite *in time* (leave versus remain), which is why the storm (a machine for trapping people in place) functions as theme, not just weather.

**Split 3 — Dr. Leila Haddad (Example B, science-fiction station story):** *Want:* keep the botany bay's failing oxygen loop online until the relief shuttle docks. *Need:* accept command responsibility — stop delegating the hard calls to protocol so her crew stops dying by the book's fine print. Friction analysis: the genre want is a countdown (excellent external clock); the need is an identity shift from *scientist who follows procedures* to *commander who amends them*. Each life-extension hack she approves cuts against her old self-image, so engineering decisions double as self-revisions — worldbuilding and character advancing on the same wire, which is the trick good SF does and padded SF forgets.

Common to all three: the need cannot be *declared* into satisfaction ("I've learned to trust!" over the credits); it gets paid at the climax in currency the want understood all along (the signature, the keys, the counter-protocol order). When you draft your own split, write these three columns — want, need, *what the want's success costs the need* — and if the third column comes out empty, the split is fake; go back to field 3 of your sheet.

> TRY IT — Pick three characters from stories you know cold (a film, a novel, a series). One line each: external want, internal need, and one scene where the two pull opposite. Fifteen minutes. The third column is the one that trains you — it is where you learn to *see* the split in finished work, which is the only way to reliably build it in your own.

## Flaw and wound

Now the chapter's load-bearing idea: the flaw chain. A **flaw** (also called a fault or weakness — the terms interconvert) is a persistent pattern of self-defeating behavior. A **wound** is the history that made the pattern adaptive once. The chain connects both to your climax:

**flaw → choices → consequences → climax**

Work it in order.

**1. Flaw as pattern, not label.** "Stubborn" is a label. The pattern is: *when contradicted, Mara doubles down on logistics — she responds to emotional appeals by producing schedules.* Patterns are writable because they predict behavior: you now know what she does at the notary's office, at the dinner table, at 11 p.m. when Theo won't answer. If your flaw cannot predict a specific behavior under pressure, it is still a label; keep drilling until it generates *doings*.

**2. Choices: give the flaw jobs.** The flaw must cause decisions a wiser character would not make — at least three across the story, escalating. Mara refuses Theo's vague counteroffer and hires the surveyor anyway (choice 1); she lets Casi accelerate the paperwork timeline instead of asking Theo what the house *means* (choice 2); she schedules her departure for the storm's peak because the ship won't wait (choice 3). Each choice is *defensible in the moment* — the flaw works by making the wrong move feel like the responsible one. That disguise is what separates character-driven from merely stupid.

**3. Consequences: the world answers back.** Every choice must cost something real: relationship equity, information, options, time. If consequences come only from *other* characters' flaws, your protagonist is a pinball. Mara's refusals cost: Theo stops telling her things (information loss); the buyer's timeline tightens (option loss); the storm finds her on the road (time made literal). By midpoint, the reader should be able to trace a clean causal line from who she is to how trapped she is — that line *is* the plot, which is why we promised at the chapter's start that good characters generate structure.

**4. Climax: the flaw faces its bill.** The climax puts the character where the flaw's methods definitively fail — where schedules cannot save her, where "manage it alone" leads to the worst outcome — and forces the choice between the old pattern and its alternative. Ray's climax demands the admission his flaw forbids. If your climax can be won *by* the flaw (patience solved by impatience, apparently), your chain has a short in it: go back to step 3 and sharpen the costs.

**Wound, carefully.** The wound explains the pattern's origin: Mara's years of managing their mother's dementia alone, then the last phone calls she stopped returning — the logistics shell *worked*, once; it got her through. Wounds supply sympathy and causality, and they should be **dosed, not dumped**: revealed under pressure, late enough to recontextualize behavior we've already seen, early enough to matter before the climax. Backload them emotionally, but schedule them structurally — put the wound beats in your outline (Chapter 9). Two dosing warnings: wounds do not *excuse* (a wound explains why the flaw exists; it does not void the consequences that make the story a story), and wounds do not *require* tragedy Olympics (a quieter wound — the promotion she took instead of the apprenticeship with her father — can run a whole novel if the pattern is sharp).

### The chain clinic: four drafts, diagnosed

Because the chain test is this chapter's pass/fail skill, run it on four specimen chains — two that fail in instructive ways, two that pass — before you build your own.

**Chain A (fails — label never became pattern):** *Flaw: jealous. Choices: she glares at the rival; she checks the rival's messages; she sulks at the party. Consequences: people notice. Climax: she wins anyway.* Diagnosis: the "choices" are symptoms, not decisions with arguments behind them; none costs *her* anything the plot requires; the climax doesn't need her to outgrow or surrender anything — the flaw could be present or absent with the same ending. Repair: make each choice *defensible* (checking the messages recovers evidence the plot needs — she is proven right once, which is how the pattern stays seductive), then design a climax where the jealous method destroys the very proof it sought.

**Chain B (fails — wound doing the flaw's job):** *Flaw: closed-off. Wound: father's abandonment. Choices: none listed — "he pushes people away" appears as narration between plot events.* Diagnosis: the flaw is reported in summaries and never *issues orders*; the wound is doing double duty as explanation *and* as excuse, so the character never actively chooses the wrong move. Repair: convert the narration into three staged refusals (he doesn't call the ally; he pays the ferry alone and misses the deadline; he lies about the package's contents "to keep it clean"), each with a bill. The wound then returns to its proper job: seasoning the refusals, not replacing them.

**Chain C (passes — Example A, compressed):** *Pattern: emotion → logistics. Choices: surveyor hired before telling Theo (cost: trust) → contract timeline accelerated (cost: honesty + leverage) → departure scheduled mid-storm (cost: safety, and the fight arrives early). Climax: forced to sign or refuse *with Theo watching*, where the pen-stroke — her signature instrument — can either betray him or finally serve them.* Passes every gate: pattern predicts behavior; choices disguise the flaw as responsibility; costs compound into the midpoint's trap; the climax weaponizes her own instrument. Note also the reparative symmetry — the same pen that logged her absences is what must now choose — chains earn their endings by *rhyming* with their beginnings.

**Chain D (passes — negative arc):** *Pattern: outsources moral calls to "the job." Choices: signs the raid warrant knowing the informant is unreliable (cost: a friendship) → withholds the exculpatory memo (cost: his own sleep, managed with whiskey) → testifies to the gapless version (cost: the last colleague's trust). Climax: the gapless version holds — he keeps the position — and the closing image shows him checking the door twice.* Diagnosis: each choice *upgrades* the surrender (that's the negative arc's signature: escalation by precedent, not by pressure alone); the climax's "win" is the fall's invoice. The twice-checked door is the arc's residue — change that isn't a speech.

Diagnose your own chain against C's rubric: defensible choices? compounding costs? climax that fails the *flaw's* methods? rhyming ending? Two failing links is a rewrite of the sheet — which is a rewrite of pages — which is exactly the cheap-now, expensive-later law from Chapter 2, now installed on the character side.

**Flat vs. round (working definitions).** From the long critical tradition (E. M. Forster's distinction is the famous ancestor, endlessly re-taught): a **flat** character is built around one or two consistent ideas and can be stated in a sentence ("the notary who will not deviate from procedure"); a **round** character contradicts themselves, contains conflict between traits, and resists one-sentence summary. The useful correction to the usual hierarchy: **flat characters are not failures.** Stories need both — flat characters are the load-bearing walls of clarity (Casi Duarte's single-mindedness makes Mara's complexity legible by contrast); round characters are where theme gets debated. Protagonists and their mirrors should be round; the cast *around* them can profitably stay flat. Beginners' common error is making every minor character "surprisingly deep," which is actually flatness by other means: the *same* depth, everywhere, always kind, always witty — a failure we will meet again, with AI's help, in Chapter 11.

## Arcs: positive, negative, flat

An **arc** is the story's answer to: *does the character change, and in which direction?* The taxonomy in most modern craft teaching is a triad, and knowing which one you are writing prevents the most expensive late-stage disappointment in fiction — finishing a draft that secretly wanted to be a different book.

**Positive arc (mastery first).** The character starts with a false belief or dysfunctional pattern (the flaw's rationalization: *handling everything alone is how I protect people*), suffers escalating costs from acting on it, and at the climax *chooses* the truth instead (*I stay, and staying is not losing*). The want usually gets renegotiated in the process: Mara's sale becomes a shared decision or a delayed one, and the survey posting — the symbol of escape — becomes something she can take *without* fleeing. The satisfying shape is not "flaw erased" but "flaw outvoted at the decisive moment, under pressure, at a price." Beliefs change slower than decisions; one climactic choice is the beginning of the change, not its completion — sequels start from there.

**Negative arc (the fall).** The character adopts or surrenders to the lie: Michael Corleone's consolidation of the family business; every tragedy's protagonist who trades the last ethical card for the want. The negative arc delivers coherence, not comfort — the ending *makes sense*, and the sense it makes is a warning. Beginner guidance: negative arcs need even tighter plotting than positive ones, because the reader must *want* the character to stop while watching them not stop; if your beta readers think the fall is glamorous, the costs are underpaid.

**Flat arc (the world changes).** The protagonist's core holds; the *situation or surrounding cast* transforms around them. Many parables, detective stories, and hard-SF stories run flat protagonists against evolving worlds; the drama comes from what the steadfast stance *extracts* from the environment. Flat is not static: a flat arc story still needs escalation, revelation, and a climax where the held value is tested at maximum — the test confirms rather than revises, and readers who came for that confirmation get paid.

**Choosing deliberately.** Match arc to engine: want/need friction with a costly climax → positive; theme of corruption or temptation → negative; theme of principle under erosion → flat. The beginner default — and this book's recommendation — is **master the positive arc first**: its machinery (false belief → costs → choice) is the triad's spine, and the other two are variations once the spine works. Whatever you choose, write it in the skeleton's field 3 area now: "Arc: positive; false belief: ___; climactic choice: ___." When Chapter 12 asks whether your ending delivered, that line is half the invoice (the stakes sentence from Chapter 2 is the other half).

> REALITY CHECK — Arc is not automatically *therapy*. Stories change people in stories faster than life changes people in life, and a novel's arc can compress years of inner movement into a weekend — that compression is legitimate dramatization, not a claim about psychology. What arc is *not* negotiable on is causality: no arc without the chain. A character who "learns a lesson" the plot never forced them to is reading a fortune cookie, not completing an arc.

### Antagonists, foils, and the cut-down cast sheet

Before the sheet, one cast-shaping rule that saves beginners whole drafts of symmetric over-building: **depth is budgeted, not moral.** Your protagonist gets the full twelve fields; the character who *opposes* or *mirrors* them meaningfully (Theo, Casi, the rival detective) gets eight; everyone else — the notary, the ferry attendant, the mother's neighbor — gets the cut-down four (identity, want, flaw-as-pattern or one governing trait, contrast role). Flatness in the supporting cast is not laziness; it is *contrast architecture*, the principle noted above now applied as staffing: roundness everywhere flattens the effect by spending it everywhere.

The **foil** deserves its own sentence because it does half of theme's work for free: a foil is a character who made a neighboring choice under similar pressures — Theo stayed, Mara left; Casi buys the houses his father lost; the partner who didn't take the bribe Ray took. Place foils so their *want/need splits invert* across from your protagonist's, and your scenes acquire argument structure: the same question ("what do you owe the living?") tried in two laboratories. The cut-down sheet's field 4 ("contrast role") is where you state that inversion in one line — if you cannot, the character is currently a stranger to the theme, and either give them a function or let them go nameless.

One casting warning that Part III will revisit with the machine in the room: **do not let the cast's job descriptions do the wanting.** "The notary wants the paperwork filed" is an institutional function, not a desire with a cost — institutions want things by *hiring people who want them*. Give your flat characters one private want visible only in a single beat (the notary who files everything correctly *and* keeps a snapshot of someone else's holiday in his drawer), and they will hum like a struck bell for one line of prose without demanding Chapter 11's full treatment.

## The character sheet

Time to fix everything above in a reusable document. The sheet is the character-side twin of Chapter 2's skeleton: premise-level, one to two pages, living in your project folder (Chapter 7's story bible absorbs it), and required for your protagonist before Part III. Supporting cast gets a cut-down version (four fields max).

```text
CHARACTER SHEET — [name]                                   v[__]

1. IDENTITY: name, age, occupation, one physical tell that shows
   under stress.
2. DEFINING SITUATION: what ordinary life looks like the week
   before the story.
3. WANT (external, checkable): one line.
4. NEED (internal): one line — must differ in verb from the want.
5. FLAW AS PATTERN: the self-defeating behavior *as it acts*
   ("responds to emotion with schedules"), not a label.
6. WOUND (dosed, not dumped): the history that made the pattern
   adaptive. One sentence now; reveal beats go in the outline.
7. FALSE BELIEF (rationalization the flaw tells itself): one line.
8. ARC TYPE: positive | negative | flat — plus the climactic
   choice in one line.
9. CHAIN: three choices the flaw will cause (beginning / middle /
   late), each with its cost.
10. VOICE: diction level (formal ↔ crude), typical sentence
    length, one verbal habit (interruptions? full stops? weather
    metaphors?), one thing they never say.
11. RELATIONSHIPS: the load-bearing relationship and what both
    sides want from it.
12. CONTRAST ROLE: which cast member they mirror or foil, one line.
```

**Voice, in brief** — field 10 deserves a paragraph because dialogue differentiation is where sheets pay off fastest (Chapter 11 runs the full program). Start with three levers anyone can control: **diction** (does this character say "insufficient" or "not enough"?), **syntax** (long缠 clauses or clipped stops?), and **rhythm under stress** (talk faster in short sentences, or go quiet and long?). Add one *habit* — interrupts, qualifies ("sort of"), answers questions with questions — and one *never* (no apologies, no first names, no swearing). That is enough to make two characters distinguishable in a blind test, which is exactly the test Chapter 11 will run.

### Why-me files and character proxies: the fuel spec

Two instruments share a spec — *material to check against, not mood to perform* — so they get one section:

**The why-me file** (`research/why-me.md`, opened Ch 8, maintained forever) is a *plain-text* list of **specific personal facts, obsessions, wounds, crafts, and fixations** you'd bring to any project — not themes ("loss") but *items* (the smell of a particular hospital corridor; the spreadsheet-argument from your first office; the exact way your grandmother pronounced bureaucratic words; the year you drove the coast road at night with the heater broken). Its format is deliberately boring: **dated bullet entries, one fact per line, no analysis** — analysis is what the *premise test* does with them; the file's job is to *exist when the test asks* ("why-me: what in YOU makes this yours?" → you paste eight bullets, not a paragraph of self-justification — bullets can be *true* under pressure; paragraphs negotiate). Maintenance rule: **append after every emotionally-specific day** (the observation-from-life habit of every fiction writer since the notebooks existed, now *filed* instead of scattered — Ch 1's model-failure anecdote's counter-weapon: the machine can't supply this content *because it never had a grandmother* — the file is the fuel depot the "never-had" problem *can't touch*, and the Chapter-8 test's check-3 reads from it directly). Growth note: entries that *earn their keep* (appear in multiple tests' check-3 answers) get starred — the star-column is your *actual* thematic gravity's map, discovered by *usage* rather than introspection's flattering guesses.

**Character proxies** are the *same fuel aimed differently*: each **named protagonist/foil in a project gets 3–5 why-me bullets *allocated to them*** (the file is general; proxies are *per-character routing* — "Mara gets: the harbor-commissioner's letter-voice you grew up hearing; the 'inventory-of-the-dead-books' habit from your father's garage; the specific cowardice of not-opening-mail-for-weeks"). The allocation does double duty: **check 3 can cite per-character fuel** (the stress-test's why-me row asks *whose* fuel — if a *named* character has zero bullets allocated, you've found your flattening risk in advance — Ch 11's helpful-intern problem wearing its *pre-draft* face), and **drafting sessions can paste the proxy directly into FILL slots** ("context: character-proxy bullets: […] + sheet fields 1/5/6" — the machine's generative range now *aimed at your specific garage* — the model's limitation structurally compensated: it completes *shapes*; you've given it *your* shapes to complete *around*). Privacy rule binds here too (the file lives in `research/`, never pasted *en masse* to any tool — only the *per-character allocations* travel, and only when they're fiction-safe: the grandmother's *pronunciation habit* is a gift; her *medical history* isn't — Ch 5's fictionalize-or-don't line drawn at the file's edge, same as always).

The two instruments' shared spec, stated once: **specific, dated, boring-format, append-mostly, allocated-on-assignment, privacy-filtered at travel time** — and their shared failure mode, also one: *letting them go stale* (a why-me file untouched since the last project's stress-test is a fuel depot for a *different* fire — the bullets rot toward abstraction, check-3 starts getting "I care about people"-grade answers, and the test's honest output becomes "mark: fuel *sounds* generic" — which is data, but sad data). The maintenance join is trivial and already scheduled: **the monthly audit gains one line** ("why-me file: entries added this month? Y/N — if N, the month's *observations* went somewhere other than the file — where?") — instruments either join the rhythm or join the graveyard; there's no third option for tools this load-bearing. One craft-note before the worked example, about *proxies and kindness*: allocated fuel tends to make proxies *sympathetic by default* (you're pasting *your* garage, after yourself) — resist at assignment time by giving each proxy **one bullet they'd *hate* to have read aloud** (the cowardice, the spreadsheet-argument, the *unflattering* corridor — fuel includes *your shame, not just your nostalgia* — proxies built only from nostalgic bullets produce characters who are *wounded in photogenic ways only*; the third field of the sheet — the flaw that *costs* — needs fuel with *teeth*, and the why-me file's append-rule ("after every emotionally-specific day") *includes the bad ones*, logged with the same bullet-format as the rest: the depot stocks light and dark because **the characters worth writing run on both.**

### Cast architecture: sizing a cast by function

The outline's cast budget needs a sizing method, so here it is — **function-first casting** (the reverse of the "who would be interesting?" daydream that bloats rosters past a draft's carrying capacity):

**Step 1 — list the spine's *demands*, not your favorite concepts:** read the stakes sentence and write down every *job* the plot needs done on-page: "someone must *deliver the binding news* (notary/letter-carrier function), someone must *embody the alternative use of the protagonist* (foil function), someone must *hold the before-state* (the pre-change world's voice — often a friend who *benefits* from the old arrangement), someone must *price the stakes* (the person who makes the cost *visible* — here: whoever receives the loss if the goal fails — for 'Low Tide': the buyer's *own* life-plan must have *witnesses*), someone must *withhold until the crisis* (the keeper of the withheld information — the December-funeral function), someone must *tempt the wrong-solution* (the shortcut-provider — the notary's *friendly* pressure toward signing-without-reading)." **Six jobs, often five people** (jobs *combine*: the foil often *also* holds-the-before-state; the temptation-role often *shares a body* with the news-delivery — *combining jobs is the cast-budget's arithmetic*: each merged job frees a *name* the reader must track — Ch 4's cognitive-load rule aimed at casting, one level up).

**Step 2 — allocate jobs to *bodies*, preferring existing characters:** every new name costs the reader a *filing-slot* (first appearance + re-recognition + relation-to-protagonist — three memory-acts per minor, *every* time they return); the default answer to "who should deliver this news?" is therefore *someone already cast* whose *presence in the scene* has its own reason (Theo delivering the notary-letter *compounds* the silence-wound; a *new* courier delivering it spends a memory-act on an envelope) — the audit question for any casting proposal: **"what does this person do *when the scene doesn't need them for their job*?"** — if the answer is "leave," they're a *function in a coat* (legitimate for one-scene jobs — the notary's *fine* as a coat — but identify them *as such* in the cast list: `function-role (coat OK)` versus `living-scene (needs arc-capacity)`, the distinction preventing one-scene coats from *accidentally growing* into three-scene obligations nobody budgeted).

**Step 3 — check the roster against the three cast types' load:** every `living-scene` (round-candidate) gets: a want/need split *scheduled*, a flaw-pattern *with costs assigned*, and at least one scene where they *act without the protagonist present* (the presence-check from the chapter — if you can't *imagine* their off-page scene, they're currently a coat, whether or not you've admitted it); every `flat` gets: a *statable* governing idea (one sentence — "protects the procedure," "believes the sale will save her son" — flats *fail* when their sentence is vague, because vagueness-as-flatness just reads as *missing person*; Forster's flatness is *precision at low word-count*, the opposite of absence — cast architecture's cruellest confusion: **a flat character with no statable idea isn't flat, they're *unfinished***); and the *whole roster* passes the **name-collision check** (initials distinct, name-eras similar enough to *belong* to the same town/era — the bible's names-list keeps the *sorted-by-first-letter* view ready for exactly this audit — Ch 12's continuity battery will *also* check it, but casting-time is cheaper than pass-5-time: the same check, two altitudes, entry-cost versus repair-cost).

**Step 4 — write the one-line cast charter** (the roster's summary the outline carries forward): "Mara (spine) · Theo (foil + before-state + *withholds-until-crisis* — three jobs, load flagged) · Aurelia (alternative-use + prices-stakes-at-distance) · Casi (temptation-coat → grows-to-living-scene iff contract-arc activates — trigger: ch8's refusal-beat) · notary (news-delivery coat) · town (chorus-flat, collective voice, *no individual lines past ch6*)." The charter's *value in five words*: **jobs-per-name made visible** — Theo's triple-load *stands out on the page* (the flagged line invites the follow-up question the section exists to ask: *can one person credibly carry three structural jobs without feeling like plot-furniture in a human suit?* — and the answer's *usually* yes when the jobs *rhyme* (his silences feed the *same* wound at every job — coherence, not coincidence — versus a cast member carrying *unrhymed* triple-jobs, which *reads* as convenience every time they enter). The whole method in one sentence: **cast from the spine's job-list, merge jobs before making names, distinguish coats from living-scenes *in writing*, and let the charter's load-flags tell you where the *next* character-worthy human needs to be born** — which for our reader's outline means exactly one casting note remains open (the town-chorus line above *pre-resolves* Ch 11's warmth-audit problem for extras: **chorus-flat + no-individual-past-ch6 = the helpful-intern bug structurally prevented at casting-time** — the problems that look like *drafting* failures are so often *casting* failures with *dates on them* — this charter is where the date gets written).

## Worked example: Example A's protagonist sheet

Here is Mara Solano's sheet, filled — the document Chapters 8 through 13 will carry like a passport, and the reference every AI-assisted character prompt in Part III must respect.

```text
CHARACTER SHEET — Mara Solano                              v1

1. IDENTITY: 36; hydrographic surveyor (compiles tide tables for a
   maritime authority); tell: straightens whatever's in reach —
   cutlery, charts, other people's schedules.
2. DEFINING SITUATION: successful city posting; promotion shortlist;
   tidily emptied apartment; nine months since her mother died,
   eleven since she last spoke to Theo.
3. WANT: sell the cliff house in Porto Alvaro in two weeks and make
   the survey posting that starts in six.
4. NEED: stay long enough to grieve WITH Theo — presence, not
   logistics.
5. FLAW AS PATTERN: answers emotional emergencies with operational
   plans; converts feelings into tasks that can be completed.
6. WOUND: she managed their mother's dementia from afar, then
   stopped answering calls; the last voicemail she didn't return
   came four days before the stroke. (Dose: journal beat, ch. ~;
   fight beat, midpoint.)
7. FALSE BELIEF: "Handling it alone is how I keep people from
   drowning — including me."
8. ARC: positive. Climactic choice: hands Theo the keys to KEEP —
   takes the posting WITH a return date — instead of forcing the
   sale and leaving the past administratively closed.
9. CHAIN: (a) hires the surveyor before telling Theo — cost:
   trust; (b) lets Casi accelerate the contract — cost: leverage
   and honesty; (c) schedules departure mid-storm — cost: safety
   + the fight she's been dodging arrives early.
10. VOICE: precise diction, meteorological metaphors under stress,
    medium sentences going clause-heavy when anxious; habit:
    converts questions into checklists; never: says "I'm scared."
11. RELATIONSHIPS: Theo — she wants his signature, he wants his
    sister; the house is both sides' proxy.
12. CONTRAST: Theo (feels first, acts slow) mirrors her (acts
    first, feels later); Casi foils her escape plan with a
    better-organized version of the same impulse.
```

**Annotated: which choices drive plot.** Field 9 *is* a three-act structure in disguise — the choices give Act I its trust fracture, Act II its acceleration, and the storm-noon deadline its Act III clock; from this sheet alone, Chapter 9 can hang beats without inventing anything. Field 5's pattern makes field 11's central conflict automatic: every scene with Theo is a scheduling-versus-feeling collision, so the *relationship* generates friction on demand — the payoff promised at the chapter's open. Field 6's dose plan (two beats, placed in the outline, not improvised in the draft) implements the wounding-as-schedule rule. And note what is absent: no horoscope, no childhood best-friend subplot, no inventory of favorites. The sheet is causal machinery, not biography — every field either causes choices or explains them. When you fill yours, cut any field you cannot connect to the chain with a one-line arrow. Archaeology for its own sake belongs in a different notebook.

> CRAFT NOTE — Fill sheets *before* Part III but expect them to change. A sheet that survives the draft untouched probably under-described the character; a sheet rewritten weekly probably isn't being read before scenes. The discipline is bidirectional: outline from the sheet, and feed discoveries back into it — one session per draft stage, changelog noted (Chapter 7's format).

## Exercises

- **Try (15 min):** Want/need for three characters you know well — one from a story, two from real life (yours included, handled gently). One line each for want and need, different verbs mandatory. Then circle any pair where you had to stretch to invent the need: in real people, needs are often invisible to everyone but the person — your instinct that it's *harder to see* is why fiction must build need into structure rather than trust it to leak.

- **Stretch (1–2 h):** Build the flaw-to-climax chain for your protagonist: write the flaw as a pattern sentence, then three choices it will cause (early / middle / late), each with a concrete cost, then the climax line where the flaw's methods fail and the alternative is chosen. Pressure-test each link with "and therefore?" — if two links connect with "and then" instead, the chain is an event list wearing a chain's clothes. Finish by filling fields 1–10 of your full character sheet (skip 11–12 if your cast doesn't exist yet; Chapter 11 supplies them).

- **Project (ongoing):** Complete the full sheet for your protagonist *and* the cut-down four-field version (identity, want, flaw-as-pattern, contrast role) for your antagonist or central opposition. Revisit both after your first draft chapter exists — the draft will have opinions. Track changes in the sheet with version numbers, the same discipline as your manuscript; the sheet is a living document, not a monument.

## Pitfalls

> PITFALL — **Decorative flaw.** *Symptom:* your notes describe a flaw impressively — proud, secretive, reckless — but no scene changes because of it; the climax could be won by anyone; beta readers shrug at the character's "complexity." *Why it happens:* flaws get written as profile features (they make people *interesting* in the abstract) instead of engines (they make people *wrong* in the specific). Also sneaks in via the wound: a tragic backstory that never issues orders in the present. *Fix:* run the chain test. List the flaw's three costed choices and the climax failure; if any link is missing, wire it now — a decorative flaw repaired is an outline rewritten, which is cheaper than a draft rewritten. *Prevention:* the character sheet's fields 5–9 are all chain fields; never mark the sheet complete with an empty climax line.

> PITFALL — **Therapy-speak substitution.** *Symptom:* characters are labels with dialogue ("my avoidant tendencies make me push people away" — spoken aloud, in the voice, by a fisherman); notes read like intake forms; change arrives by insight-declaration rather than event. *Fix:* delete the label, add an action and a cost — the same beat re-staged as behavior: Theo stops answering because every call becomes a logistics call. *Prevention:* the Craft Note's rule — write the *specifically odd* doing, let the reader diagnose; ban the eleven diagnostic words from your draft search (Chapter 12's line pass runs the search for you).

Related failures for Appendix C: flat-ensemble syndrome, every character kind and reasonable (feeds F-02); arc-plot mismatch discovered at revision (F-03's character-side face).

## Checklist

> CHECKLIST — You're ready to move on when:
> - [ ] Want and need are stated in one line each, with different verbs.
> - [ ] The flaw is written as a pattern (predictable behavior), not a label.
> - [ ] The chain test passes: three costed choices reach a climax where the flaw's methods fail.
> - [ ] Arc type is chosen on purpose, with the climactic choice named.
> - [ ] A completed character sheet exists for your protagonist — and its field 9 looks suspiciously like a plot.

## Key takeaways

- **Want versus need is friction, not trivia:** the want drives scenes; the need is violated by how the character pursues it; the climax forces the trade. Different verbs, or you have two wants.
- **The flaw chain is the chapter's spine:** flaw → costed choices → consequences → climax. A flaw that costs nothing is a condiment; decorate nothing — wire everything.
- **Dose the wound; don't dump it.** Backstory explains the pattern's origin; the present-tense pattern does the dramatizing. Schedule reveals in the outline, not by ambush in the draft.
- **Arcs are positive, negative, or flat — chosen deliberately.** Master the positive arc first; whatever you choose, write the false belief and climactic choice into the sheet now so Chapter 12 has something to invoice.
- **Sheets are causal machinery, not biography.** Every field must cause or explain choices — cut the rest. The payoff arrives immediately: field 9's chain gives Chapter 9 an outline and gives every scene its friction on demand.


# Chapter 04 — Setting, Scene & Point of View

## Opening hook

Here is a paragraph about a woman arriving at her dead mother's house:

> Mara drove up the coast in February, thinking about everything she had to do. When she reached the house, she went inside and found that Theo had already started packing up their mother's things. They argued about the sale, as she had expected, and she realized that nothing between them would be easy.

Nothing is *wrong* with it, exactly. Every fact is present: arrival, season, motive, discovery, conflict, emotional weather. It is also dead on the page — a stage manager's report of a play no one will attend. Now the same content, staged:

> The key stuck twice before the door gave, and the smell hit her first — cedar and the cold-sweet rot of a drain nobody had run in nine months. Half the kitchen was already in boxes. Theo had done them in the wrong order, lids on the bottom shelf, and their mother's good soup pot sitting on top of a carton of towels, as if it were the treasure of a shipwreck.
>
> "You started," Mara said.
>
> "Somebody had to." He didn't turn from the window. "You'd have made a spreadsheet."
>
> "A spreadsheet is how things get —"
>
> "Done. Yeah." Now he did turn, and his hands were the wrong kind of clean. "Everything gets done. She asked for you in December, by the way. Thought you should know that before you inventory the pots."

Same facts. But now there is a *scene*: a goal (assess the house, get the conversation onto the sale), a conflict that arrives through behavior instead of narration ("the wrong order" of boxes; "the wrong kind of clean"), and an outcome (the December information lands — and changes what the next scene must be about). The transformation between those two paragraphs is the skill this chapter installs, in four parts: **scene anatomy** (goal, conflict, outcome), **show and tell as tools** (not religions), **point of view as camera and consciousness** (chosen and sustained), and **setting as pressure** (places that limit choices). This is the last chapter of Part I, and by its end your Project tier has you doing what the whole part promised: finishing a short story draft *without AI*.

## Learning objectives

By the end of this chapter, you will be able to:

- [ ] Build a scene as goal + conflict + outcome.
- [ ] Apply show-vs-tell as a tool, not a dogma ("tell to orient, show to matter").
- [ ] Choose and sustain a POV (first, close third, omniscient) on the distance ladder.
- [ ] Use setting as pressure on the character.
- [ ] Name the sequel/reaction beat and basic dialogue mechanics well enough to draft Part I's milestone story.

## Scene = goal + conflict + outcome

The **scene** is fiction's unit of experience. Chapters are filing tabs; paragraphs are breath; the scene is where a reader either leans in or starts shopping. The good news is that scenes have a skeleton as reliable as Chapter 2's — and most beginner scenes fail for one of three missing bones.

**Goal.** Someone in the scene wants something *checkable* before it ends: a signature, an answer, a borrowed tool, a confession, a moment of peace that the scene's other occupant refuses to grant. The goal can be tiny (get through dinner without the subject coming up) but it must exist, and it must sit in a specific character — usually the POV character, sometimes the one pressing against them. Test: *could a director stage "scene works" as a visible action?* "They reconnect emotionally" fails; "she signs the paper; she doesn't" passes.

**Conflict.** Something opposes the goal *before the scene ends* — another want, a physical limit, a rule, the weather, the self. Opposition must be *active*, not atmospheric: a sad room does not conflict; a brother packing boxes in the wrong order does. Notice the hook's revision — the conflict arrived as detail *plus* dialogue turn, never as authorial warning ("they argued, as expected" was the dead version's sin: announcing the weather instead of letting it rain).

**Outcome.** The scene ends with *something changed*: goal achieved, refused, redirected, or superseded by worse news. The change can be a whispered reversal or a slammed door; what it cannot be is *nothing* — no-resolution scenes are the leading cause of mush in middle drafts. The outcome should also *load the next scene*: December's revelation in the hook demands a response scene, which is momentum built from scene furniture instead of plot contrivance.

Three bones, then a fourth concept that beginners use without naming: **sequel beats.** Real scenes breathe as *scene → reaction → dilemma → decision*, where the reaction beat (the character alone with what just happened: stunned, reordering the day) and the decision (which launches the next scene) are where interiority lives. You will not always write all four parts — flash compresses them into a sentence of stunned silence; thrillers truncate reaction to a single line of action — but knowing the full cycle lets you choose *where* you're cutting, instead of leaving your protagonist reacting nowhere on the page.

### Scene endings: how to turn off the light

If outcomes are the scene's third bone, the *last line* is where the bone meets the reader's nervous system. A scene ending is a contract for the next thirty seconds of attention, and the catalog of honest ways to honor it is shorter than you think:

- **The door-slam turn:** a line or action that physically or socially ends the beat while opening pressure on the next (Theo's "Does 'when' go in the list?"). Your workhorse; strongest when the line is *about the scene's subject* while *refusing to resolve it*.
- **The loaded decision:** the POV character chooses — even microscopically ("she did not type"). Interiority as cliff: the reader leans toward the action the decision implies.
- **The reversal of information:** something is learned that re-prices everything previously said. Ends must land *after* the scene's largest change; ending before it is how scenes die in the middle of their own event.
- **The image close:** a concrete object absorbs the emotion (the soup pot on the shipwreck carton). Literary fiction's default; works only if the image was *loaded* earlier — unloaded images at scene ends are decoration with a bow on it.
- **The callback rhyme:** an echo of an earlier line, now changed in meaning ("Everything gets done." → "Yeah."). Cheap in words, expensive in planning — put the setup beat in your outline.
- **The fade on decision pending:** rare, deliberate: scene ends *inside* the dilemma, mid-breath, when the sequel beat itself is the point. Overused by anxious writers; if your scenes all end mid-breath, you are postponing outcomes, not pacing them.

The failure modes to hunt in revision: **the double-ending** (strong last line followed by a summary sentence that explains it — delete the explanation; the line already did the job), **the trailing-adverb exit** ("...angrily."), and **the authorial fade-out** ("And so the evening passed, with much left unsaid" — narration stepping in to collect a scene it didn't stage). Test every scene's last line by covering everything above it: does the line alone make a curious person want the next line of the *next* scene? If it needs its own paragraph of context to land, either move the landing earlier or cut the context.

**Dialogue mechanics, the working subset.** Dialogue in scenes does three jobs simultaneously: *pursues the goal* (every line is either advance, defense, or feint), *reveals voice* (Chapter 3's field 10 — diction, syntax, habits), and *manages information* (who knows what, who learns what, who is lying). The mechanics that serve those jobs: attribute with action beats more than "he said/she said" tags when the cast exceeds two; let subtext do the heavy lifting (characters argue about the soup pot when they are arguing about their mother); use interruption and non-answer as pressure tools; and — the test that saves every draft — read the speeches aloud with the tags covered: if two characters' lines swap without changing meaning, their voices are not differentiated yet (Chapter 11 fixes this systematically with AI's help; here you fix it by ear).

> TRY IT — Take your favorite scene from any story and label its bones in the margin: underline the goal (yours or theirs), bracket the opposition, box the outcome. Five minutes, no writing required. You will find the goal sometimes hides *inside* a line of dialogue and the outcome sometimes is a silence — that's fine; you're training recognition, not enforcing a straitjacket.

## Showing vs. telling, practically

Every beginner meets the commandment "show, don't tell" as absolute, follows it into purple monstrosities — three paragraphs describing a handshake — and then swings to pure summary and writes a synopsis with dialogue. The mature version is unexciting and unstoppable: **tell to orient, show to matter.**

**Tell (summary, statement)** is efficient and honest: it covers time, sets context, delivers facts the scene's pressure doesn't need to stage. "They argued about the sale, as she had expected" is perfectly good *summary* — it belongs in transition, in chapter openings, anywhere the reader needs the map before the terrain. Telling also controls *pacing*: fast periods of telling (weeks pass) make the shown moments (the kitchen, the December line) feel like slow-motion — which is exactly what slow-motion is for.

**Show (scene, dramatization)** is expensive and therefore should be spent where the story's value concentrates: on decisions, turns, wounds activated, relationships bending. The rule of thumb — if the reader would shrug at the outcome, don't stage it; if the reader's pulse should change, stage it to the bone.
