When a mood word matters, convert it: "tense" → structural consequences; "funny" → specific comic mechanism (understatement? escalation? category slip-ups?); "lyrical" → a rhythm rule you can state (clauses of a certain length, image source restricted to one domain — water, say). Constraints are adjectives with their homework done.

> TRY IT — Take your weakest existing prompt (everyone has one they keep reusing) and rewrite it with all five slots, converting at least two adjectives into checkable constraints. Run both versions in fresh sessions. Diff the outputs *against your constraints*, line by line, before judging which you prefer — you are grading compliance first and quality second, because compliance is what you can make repeatable.

### The version line

Every prompt card in this book carries a header comment:

```text

```

Three fields, all load-bearing: the **ID** (stable identity across the book and your file), the **version** (v1, v2… — bump when you edit the prompt's behavior), and the **tested** date against a mainstream assistant (freshness evidence for the Chapter 5 discipline — capability-dependent cards age). "Generic, no model-specific syntax" is a promise: no secret slash-commands, no brand dialect, nothing that dies with a product update. When *your* cards graduate to your file (the Project exercise), keep the same three fields; six months later they are the difference between a prompt library and a prompt graveyard.

## Iteration: draft → critique → refine

One-shot prompting — the prayer model — assumes a perfect first result, which Chapter 5 already told you to stop expecting. The working loop has three legs, each a distinct *task* with a distinct *role*:

1. **Draft leg:** generate material under constraints (the anatomy above).
2. **Critique leg:** in a *fresh* framing (or fresh session), evaluate the output against your stated goals — "as a skeptical editor: where does this scene fail its constraints? Miss its goal? Cliché? Be specific; no praise." (PC-02 is this leg in card form.)
3. **Refine leg:** apply *your* adjudicated fixes — sometimes by asking the model to execute *specific* changes ("tighten paragraphs 2 and 4; keep every constraint from the original prompt; do not touch the dialogue"), often by editing yourself and returning the result as new context.

Why legs instead of one mega-instruction ("draft it perfectly"): instruction stacks conflict (Chapter 5's field guide, force 2), critiques of a model's own fresh draft are shallow unless *grounded in your criteria*, and — decisively — **the loop's adjudication step is where your taste does its reps.** A loop you adjudicate is practice; a loop that self-feeds is a circle (failure F-07's little sister, previewing Chapter 12's non-converging revision loops). Two loop-hygiene rules: (a) **max two refine passes per leg before you intervene by hand** — if the output isn't converging toward your constraint list after two, the prompt is wrong, not the output (see the debugger below); (b) **log the loop's outcome in one decisions-file line** when anything substantive was accepted — the loop is only as honest as its ledger.

**Prompt chains** extend the loop across operations: extract → transform → verify, each step's output the next step's context. Example chain for description work: (1) *extract* every concrete detail from my scene as a list; (2) *flag* which details are owned-by-this-story versus generic (Chapter 4's ladder); (3) *rewrite flagged lines* to rung 3+, keeping count fixed. Chains beat mega-prompts for the same reason recipes beat prayers: each step is checkable, failures localize (you can see *which* step lied), and you can re-run just the broken link. The cost is bookkeeping — hence Chapter 7's prompt file, where proven chains get card-ified.

**Negative constraints** are the "must not" half of the space, and fiction needs them more than most domains because the model's positive priors are so strong (it *knows* what stories usually do — Chapter 5's prediction frame — so "surprise me" underspecifies against a gale). Practical set: banned clichés ("no *heart pounded*, no *silence stretched*"), banned summary ("no backstory paragraphs"), banned resolutions ("do not reconcile the sisters in this scene"), banned closings ("no final-sentence realization"). Pair every negative with a positive substitute where possible ("no weather indoors; use interior objects instead") — pure prohibition leaves a vacuum the prior rushes to fill; prohibition-plus-replacement gives the vacuum a shape.

**The prompt debugger (PC-06 habit).** When output misbehaves: *don't re-roll and hope* — re-rolling samples the same distribution. Instead run Chapter 5's four-force card in order: (1) buried or vague instruction → move and sharpen; (2) contradictions inside the prompt → hunt them (examples beat instructions — check your few-shot); (3) desk pressure → shrink or re-enclose context, restate locks *after* the paste; (4) task stack → decompose into a chain. Still failing? Run the prompt **three times in fresh sessions** to see whether you have a *systematic* miss (prompt problem) or a *draw* (randomness — now fix by locks, not panic). Then change **one slot only** and retest — single-variable debugging, or you'll never know which edit worked. This habit, institutionalized as PC-06, is the difference between owning prompting and renting it.

## Few-shot with your own prose

**Few-shot prompting** = including examples of the behavior you want ("few" = anywhere from one to a handful). The examples do more teaching than your instructions (Chapter 5, force 2: *examples always outrank instructions*), which cuts both ways: a bad example poisons faster than a good rule can save, and — the opportunity — **your own prose as example gives the model something no instruction can: your voice's actual statistics.**

Technique, in order:

1. **Select the anchor carefully.** One to three paragraphs *you* stand by — voice-consistent, scene-typical, no experimental failures. This is the voice-anchor idea at prompt level (the paragraph-first discipline of Chapter 10 grows from here): the example must be *yours* or the model will faithfully imitate someone else's you.
2. **Frame the example as data, not as answer key.** "Below is my prose; match voice, sentence rhythm, and image density — do not continue or improve it" — otherwise the model starts *writing toward* the example's ending, and you get fan fiction of your own paragraph.
3. **State what to abstract** — name the dimensions: contraction habits, clause length, how you handle interiority (free indirect? quoted thought?), image source range. Naming dimensions prevents shallow mimicry (swapping in your vocabulary while keeping the internet's rhythm).
4. **Keep the example adjacent to the task**, after the instructions, right before generation — the desk's recency bias is now your ally (Chapter 5's "near" rule).
5. **Test the pair:** if the model's output would pass as yours when you're not looking, the few-shot worked; if it passes only because you're *tired*, tighten the frame with one negative constraint (PC-04 is this recipe in card form).

Few-shot's limits, honestly: it shifts *surface statistics* impressively and shifts *judgment* not at all — the model can sound like you while choosing things you never would (Chapter 14's whole diagnostics exist for this residue). And there's a privacy rhyme with Chapter 5: examples are *your* text going onto the shared desk — paste from files you own, never third-party prose ("match this famous author's voice" is both an IP tar pit and a worse teacher: published famous voices are overrepresented in the model's center already).

## Prompt cards PC-01 … PC-06

The chapter's six graduates, introduced here in teaching order, collected in full (with knobs) in Appendix A. Read them now as *patterns of anatomy made flesh* — each card's five slots marked in miniature:

> PROMPT CARD PC-01 — Scene drafter (voice-anchor)
>
> ```text
> 
> Role: you draft fiction scenes for me to revise; your text is raw
> material, not final prose.
> Context: [story situation in 2–3 lines: who wants what here, what
> opposes]. Anchor prose (my voice — match rhythm and diction, do not
> continue it):
> [1–3 paragraphs of my prose]
> Task: draft the next scene from these beats: [beats as short lines].
> Constraints: POV [X], tense [past/present], 400–550 words; conflict
> unresolved at the end; no backstory paragraphs; no words from my
> banned list: [list].
> Output: scene only after a line "DRAFT:" — no preamble, no titles.
> ```

> PROMPT CARD PC-02 — Critique my draft (structured)
>
> ```text
> 
> Role: skeptical fiction editor; specific, unsentimental, no praise
> unless a line earns it with a reason.
> Task: critique the passage below against these five questions only:
> 1) What does the POV character want in this scene, and is it visible
> by the midpoint? 2) Where does telling occur that should be staged?
> 3) Which sentences are generic (could appear in anyone's story)?
> 4) Where does POV/distance slip? 5) Does the ending turn or fade?
> Format: numbered findings, each as [location quote] → [issue] →
> [concrete fix]. Max 12 findings, ranked by damage.
> Passage: [text]
> ```

> PROMPT CARD PC-03 — Explain-my-options brainstormer
>
> ```text
> 
> Role: options-broker, not co-writer; you generate choices; I choose.
> Context: [story situation / decision point, 3–5 lines].
> Task: give me 10 options for [decision], each labeled with:
> (a) what it costs the protagonist, (b) which story shape it makes
> most likely (choice A’s kind of ending), (c) one risk it creates
> for pacing.
> Rules: no option that requires new characters unless I mark
> "cast open"; at least 3 options must be uncomfortable; no ranking,
> no recommendation — I decide.
> ```

> PROMPT CARD PC-04 — Few-shot style transfer (my prose)
>
> ```text
> 
> Role: stylistic apprentice to the anchor prose below.
> Task: rewrite the TARGET passage to match the ANCHOR's voice.
> Abstract and carry: sentence-length rhythm, contraction habits,
> interiority method, image-source range, punctuation tics.
> Do not carry: the anchor's content, names, or plot.
> Do not improve ideas — only manner.
> ANCHOR (my prose): [1–3 paragraphs]
> TARGET: [passage to re-voice]
> Output: rewritten target only.
> ```

> PROMPT CARD PC-05 — Constraint imposer ("write with…")
>
> ```text
> 
> Role: constraint executor for a drafting exercise.
> Task: rewrite the passage under ALL of these simultaneously:
> - no sentences longer than 18 words
> - no abstract nouns from this list: [list]
> - every paragraph must contain one concrete object handled by a hand
> - dialogue lines ≤ 8 words
> - keep: POV, tense, proper nouns, and the sequence of events
> If a constraint makes the passage impossible, keep the closest
> lawful version and list which lines you strained at, after the text.
> Passage: [text]
> ```

> PROMPT CARD PC-06 — Prompt debugger
>
> ```text
> 
> Role: prompt engineer for fiction workflows.
> Below: MY PROMPT and THE OUTPUT I got, plus WHAT I WANTED.
> Diagnose in this order, stopping at the first confirmed cause:
> 1) vague/buried instruction  2) internal contradiction (check my
> examples vs. my rules)  3) context pressure (what in my pasted
> material pulls against my instruction)  4) stacked tasks.
> For each confirmed cause: quote the offending line, then give ONE
> revised prompt (full text) fixing only that cause. No praise, no
> general advice. End with: what to test first and what result
> would prove the fix worked.
> MY PROMPT: [...] OUTPUT: [...] WANTED: [...]
> ```

Read PC-02's five questions against Chapter 4's anatomy — the card *is* the chapter's craft made executable. PC-06 is Chapter 5's four forces, prompt-level. Nothing in the six cards is brand-specific or clever; their power is entirely anatomical — and watching *which* slot produced which output effect, across your own tests, is the real curriculum of the card library.

### Five failures, mapped to slots

A compact pathology table — each row a symptom you will actually see, the slot that caused it, and the repair:

1. **Symptom:** output is a wall of purple description before anything happens. **Slot fault:** *task* was "set the scene" shaped (or implied by a bare "write a scene about…"), and no *constraint* marked what counts as progress. **Repair:** name the operation's spine — "the scene must move from her decision to open the letter to her decision not to answer it" — and add "no description longer than two sentences before the first line of dialogue or decision."
2. **Symptom:** characters talk like the same well-educated stranger. **Slot fault:** missing *context* voice data (no anchor, no voice notes from Chapter 3's field 10) plus *constraints* silent on diction. **Repair:** paste one speech per major participant as voice data with "each speaker keeps their diction/syntax/habit"; add "if two consecutive lines could swap speakers without meaning changing, rewrite line two."
3. **Symptom:** the ending ties off what you wanted left open. **Slot fault:** *constraint* left the ending's shape unspecified, so the model's default (literary default: resolution; genre default: climax-payoff) filled the slot. **Repair:** state the ending as a required shape — "end on the unanswered question; no reconciliation, no summary of feelings" — in the *last* constraint line, near generation.
4. **Symptom:** output answers a *different, easier question* than yours (gives definitions when you wanted options; gives one polished option when you wanted ten). **Slot fault:** *task* verb mismatched your actual need, often buried mid-prompt. **Repair:** lead with the verb and quantify — "Give me TEN options, then STOP" — and move any secondary asks ("and briefly note risks") behind an explicit "after the ten, in a separate section."
5. **Symptom:** format keeps collapsing (preamble essays, markdown tables where you wanted prose, bullet fragments of a scene). **Slot fault:** *output shape* absent or specified once, early, away from generation. **Repair:** restate the shape adjacent to the task AND at the end ("first line of your reply: DRAFT: — nothing before it"), and give a miniature shape example (three lines of the format you want, placeholder content) — shape examples teach faster than shape rules.

Rows 4 and 5 are the everyday annoyances; rows 1–3 are where stories quietly go wrong, which is why your compliance grading (pen on constraints, output beside them) checks *ending shape* and *progress* with the same seriousness as word count. A card that has never been graded on its story-critical constraints has only ever been graded on its formatting.

### Parameterized cards: prompt templates with fills

As your file grows, you will notice yourself maintaining *families*: the same skeleton with swappable parts. Treat serious cards like tiny programs — **fixed logic, marked fills**:

```text
[fixed]  Role + loop rules + output shape + banned list logic
[FILL-A] story situation (2–3 lines, from the bible)
[FILL-B] beats (3–7 short lines, hand-written)
[FILL-C] anchor prose (1–3 paragraphs, my best voice)
[FILL-D] constraint variant (comedy / dread / intimacy pack)
```

Two disciplines make parameterization pay: **fills come from your documents** (FILL-A from the bible, FILL-B from the outline — never improvised under the chat's influence, or the desk starts drafting your beats too); and **constraint packs are named and versioned separately** (the "dread pack" — no interior comfort, weather permitted only as pressure, endings forbid relief — can be tested once and reused across stories, since it encodes *your* theory of dread, not this plot's). Promoted cards in Appendix A are shown *de-parameterized* for teaching; your working copies should look like the marked template above. When a fill type keeps growing past its slot (context balloons), that's Chapter 5's desk pressure — summarize to the bones, keep the bible's relevant three rules verbatim, and restate locks after the paste.

### Register, audience, and task-mode controls

Three smaller slot-refinements that beginners reach for constantly:

**Register** ("how the prose is dressed"): state it as *readers and restraint*, not vibes — "register: literary-contemporary workshop prose; restraint over ornament; contractions in dialogue; zero rhetorical questions in narration." If register matters, ban one opposite failure explicitly ("no inspirational-poster cadence, no closing aphorisms").

**Audience** ("who is imagined to read this"): "draft for a discerning adult literary audience; assume intelligence; no YA sensibilities; no explaining the metaphor." Audience lines quietly fix tone where mood adjectives fail — they are constraint-conversions of taste.

**Task-mode** ("which hat you wear now"): critique mode vs. drafting mode vs. extraction mode must never share a prompt (stacked tasks force one). Signs you mixed modes: a "draft" that keeps editorializing ("here you might consider…"), or a "critique" that silently rewrites everything. Split it: critique in session one with PC-02; drafts in session two with PC-01 and your adjudicated notes as FILL material. The mode boundary *is* the loop leg boundary — the anatomy and the iteration are the same system seen twice.

### Chain patterns worth stealing

Prompt chains (Chapter 5's decomposition discipline, Chapter 6's loops made explicit) recur across this book in proven shapes — five worth pre-installing in your file, each with its failure localization stated (the whole argument for chaining is knowing *which* link lied):

1. **Extract → judge → transform** (the workhorse): extract elements from your prose (details, claims, beats) as a *list* → judge the list against a rubric (owned/generic, in/outline, pass/fail) → transform only the judged rows. *Localizes at:* the judge step (rubrics are checkable; transformation of a clean list can't smuggle in un-judged material). Used by PC-16's diagnostic-first design and Chapter 12's sweeps.
2. **Generate → blind-test → repair**: generate candidates (premises, dialogue, names) *unlabeled* → test them against your criteria *without* revealing which you favor (blindness prevents the model from pattern-completing toward your visible preference) → repair only survivors. *Localizes at:* the test (your criteria, applied without knowledge of provenance — Ch 11's blind test is this chain at character scale).
3. **Draft → critique → adjudicate → revise** (the loop of this chapter, now fully explicit): the *adjudication step is a human turn in the chain* — the machine never sees its own critique unfiltered; it sees *your kept-findings list* as the next draft's context. *Localizes at:* adjudication quality (row-logged — Ch 12's verdict vocabulary is this link's eventual form).
4. **Summarize → restate → verify-against-source**: for any long context (your own chapters, research packets): summarize to bones → *restate the summary in your words* (the restate step is the honesty gate — a summary you can't restate in your own phrasing is a summary you don't own) → verify load-bearing lines against the original document. *Localizes at:* the restatement (misunderstandings surface when *you* become the next link — the chain's built-in tripwire).
5. **Constraint-pack → generate → schema-check**: assemble constraint pack (bible §5 + style bans + locks) → generate → check output against the pack *as a schema* (machine-checkable: banned strings absent? length in band? required beats present?). *Localizes at:* schema (the check is code-like — zero taste required, which is why it runs reliably at 1 a.m.). PC-14's locks + marking discipline is this chain, operationalized.

Common to all five: **every link has one verb, an inspectable output, and a named failure mode** — chains without inspectable intermediate outputs are just mega-prompts with delusions of structure (if you can't show me the list after link 1, you don't have a chain, you have a prayer with steps). Build new chains by starting from *whichever link you currently do by hand painfully* — the chain's purpose is making *your* judgment-scale work machine-assisted, not making the machine's work larger. And retire chains that fail twice at the same link (the failure is the link's fault or the card's, not the chain-idea's — version the link, re-test, or archive with a failure note; your file's retirement section teaches future-you as much as its active cards do).

### The card lifecycle: save, version, retire

Saving and reusing prompts — the habit the whole card format exists to serve — runs as a small lifecycle with four states, and *knowing which state a card is in* prevents the prompt file's two classic diseases (the scrapbook of untested genius-ideas, and the graveyard of cards that worked exactly once):

**Draft (no ID yet):** a prompt that worked in the wild and got copied into `prompts/_drafts/` with one line of context ("the thing that fixed ch7's compress — 2026-09-XX"). Drafts are allowed to be ugly and context-dependent — they're *evidence*, not tools. The lifecycle gate: **a draft promoted to a card only after a second successful run in a *different session and context*** — one-run success is usually luck-of-the-desk (the shared context was doing invisible work — Ch 5 force 3); two-run success with changed context is *reproducibility*, the minimum bar for toolhood. Most prompts you love should *stay drafts* — the file's draft folder is where context-limited helpers live happily forever, labeled with their one good use.

**Card (ID + version line + knobs):** promoted drafts get the full treatment — PC-style header, blanks marked `[...]`, knobs listed (which lines vary per use: length band, POV, constraint-pack, persona), a *failure note* ("goes generic on action scenes — beats need physical causality"), and a `tested` date. The knobs list is the difference between owning and merely possessing: a card whose variable lines aren't *marked* gets hacked inline each use (version drift without version numbers — you're three undocumented variants deep by October and can't reproduce May's result). Editing a card's *behavior* = bump version, note the delta in the card's changelog line (`v2 · added no-emotion-weather ban` — PC-14's bump in Ch 10 is the live example); editing only *fills* (the `[...]` slots) = no bump — fills are inputs, not card-identity.

**Active / Dormant:** active cards get *re-tested* on their `tested` anniversary-ish (Appendix F's habit: capability-dependent cards re-validated before publication and after any tool switch — a two-minute run to confirm the failure note hasn't *widened*); dormant cards (project finished, technique superseded) move to `_archive/` *with their last-known-good version intact* — archived ≠ deleted (Ch 16's next project will mine them — the parked-ideas discipline for prompts). The disease this state prevents: *the file of cards you're vaguely ashamed of because you don't remember if they work* — dormancy is a honest label; shame is not a filing system.

**Retired (failure-noted):** cards that failed twice at the same link, or violated a constraint you've since banned, get retired *with the autopsy on top* (`retired 2026-09 · cause: outputs drifted to house-register after provider update — pattern taught: style-imitation cards are the most update-fragile class`). Retired cards are the file's *institutional memory of what prompt classes break how* — read the retirements section annually and you'll re-discover why your favorite-thing-to-blame ("the model got worse") is usually a *card class* that was always fragile (style-transfer yes; schema-checks almost never — the fragility gradient itself is worth knowing, and you can only know it from autopsies you bothered to write down).

The whole lifecycle fits on an index card (meta-card, naturally): *draft on second-run evidence → promote with knobs + failure note → version on behavior-change, re-test on schedule → archive honestly, retire with autopsy.* Run it, and the file grows into what Appendix A was built to be — not a scrapbook of aspirations, but a drawer of *tested instruments with known edges*, which is the only kind of prompt library that survives contact with a real manuscript and a real writing life.

### The five prompt patterns, annotated live

The chapter promises five reusable patterns; each gets its *pattern statement* (the transferable shape), one *micro-example* (so the shape is visible at eight lines), and its *dominant failure* (the way it typically breaks — each failure mapping back to Ch 5's four forces):

**Pattern 1 — the three-part brief** (context → task → output shape). *Statement:* every reliable prompt's skeleton — *situation* (what the machine must know to aim), *job* (the single verb-action requested), *shape* (how the answer must arrive: format, length, what follows what) — failures almost always trace to a *missing* member (context-less tasks produce confident genericism; shape-less tasks produce novels when you wanted lists). *Micro-example:* "Context: two sisters dividing their mother's house contents; the older has lived there 20 years. Task: draft the scene where the younger names the first item she wants. Shape: 200–300 words, close third on the older sister, end on her *not* answering." *Dominant failure:* **shape-scope creep** — the draft quietly overshoots length/end-spec because the shape's constraints sat *after* 800 tokens of context (instruction *position* matters: constraints last, adjacent to the task — the context-pressure force wearing its positional hat; fix = move shape after task, re-run, one variable).

**Pattern 2 — the critic frame** (adversarial role + verdict vocabulary). *Statement:* critiques need *assignment-shaped* motivation — a role that *must find things* (not "be helpful"), a *finding format* (quote → issue → fix), and *vocabulary that ends in decisions* (damage ratings, verdicts — outputs that stop the machine from *closing with praise* like a report card). *Micro-example:* the PC-02 anatomy in eight lines: "You are a skeptical editor. Critique the passage below: 5 questions max, each as [quote → issue → concrete fix], ranked by damage. No praise unless a line earns it *with a reason*." *Dominant failure:* **collapsed adversarialism** — after two rounds the frame *softens* (the conversation's history pulls toward agreeableness — context pressure over *several turns*: your "great point" replies in earlier rounds *taught* the frame what its job was — the sycophancy force demonstrated in your own chat history); fix = *fresh sessions per critique round* (the single most important operational rule for critic frames: the frame lives in turn one; by turn twelve it's *your reflections arguing with you* — hence every critic-card's "fresh session" note, and F-05's zero-row-session alarm).

**Pattern 3 — the structured extraction** (schema-first, evidence-or-null). *Statement:* any "pull information out of text" job needs a *schema* declared up front (fields, order, units) plus an **evidence-or-null** clause (every row cites the source span *or* says "not found" — no *filling the schema to look complete* — the type-split's extraction-mode applied structurally). *Micro-example:* "Extract from the pasted contract: clause #, subject, deadline (date or NONE), obligations (verbatim quote each). If the clause type isn't present, output 'not found' — never approximate." *Dominant failure:* **schema-satisfaction fabrication** — empty fields get *invented* completions because the shape exerts pull (a blank REQUIRED cell reads to the machine as unfinished work — hallucination's *helpful* species); fix = evidence-or-null *as a hard rule in every extraction prompt* + spot-check three citations by hand (the quotes-or-nothing verification pass — the pattern's non-negotiable quality gate, five minutes, catching the fabrication class *before* it enters documents).

**Pattern 4 — the transform under locks** (rewrite with preserve-list and change-spec). *Statement:* rewrites succeed when the *invariants* are enumerated as loudly as the *changes* (preserve: names, tense, POV, locked constructions — change: *one specified dimension* — length, register, distance) — the locks existing because the machine's default transform *helpfully updates everything it notices* (including the things you didn't want touched). *Micro-example:* "Rewrite for 30% shorter: PRESERVE proper nouns, tense, the metaphor in paragraph 2, and all [!mine] markers. CHANGE only: sentence count, redundancy. No new images." *Dominant failure:* **silent-lock-violation** — the output *reads* right while a preserve-item drifted (the misspelled name, the smoothed odd-syntax — locks violated in *low-salience* positions where your reading-eye is weakest); fix = lock-audit *after* every transform (a thirty-second grep for proper nouns + [!mine] markers before accepting — PC-21's preserve-list doing its job as a *checklist*, not a wish — the same altitudes lesson: generation's excitement is worst at *noticing* details, so the detail-check happens in the adjudication pass, not the receipt).

**Pattern 5 — the chain** (extract → judge → transform, links inspectable). *Statement:* complex jobs decompose into *single-purpose links* whose outputs can each be *read and rejected* — the chain earning its keep not by *quality of any link* but by **fault localization** (when the final answer's wrong, you *know* which link to re-run — versus one mega-prompt's global failure mode where everything's plausible and nothing's attributable). *Micro-example:* "Link 1: extract the passage's claims as a numbered list. [I check the extraction.] Link 2: for each claim, classify internal/external-type. [I check.] Link 3: route-verify: for external claims, list what a primary source must confirm." *Dominant failure:* **chain-of-plausible-garbage** — links *individually* fluent, *collectively* laundering an error (bad extraction in link 1 *compounds* through perfect downstream execution — each link's fluency *certifying* the previous link's mistake — the shared-desk effect at pipeline scale); fix = **the human check-gate between links** (the pattern's design *includes* the stops — "I check the extraction" isn't a suggestion, it's a *chain element* — automation's oldest lesson, restated for prompts: a chain with no gates is a single point of failure wearing five boxes; every link *you* don't check is a link *nobody* checked).

The five patterns' shared skeleton, now that they've all been seen: **aim (context/proxy) → job (one clear verb) → shape (format+limits) → arms (locks/evidence-rules) → stops (fresh-session discipline / human gates)** — and their shared *anti*-skeleton: any prompt missing two of the five is a *wish* (Ch 5's four forces will find it; the debug loop will name which piece; the version line will stop you re-losing the fix). Patterns are taught as *shapes* precisely because shapes transfer across model-generations — when the tool changes next year, your five patterns survive intact and only the `tested` dates expire: **the durable asset was never the prompts; it's the *discipline structure* the cards taught your hands to reach for.**

## Worked example: building your scene drafter live

Let's assemble PC-01 from scratch — the chapter's Project exercise, run once in full so you can imitate the *process*, not just copy the card.

**Step 1 — Decide before you type (all five slots, in your words).** Suppose your project is a family comedy of manners at a wedding: POV close third on the bride's sister; present tense (the genre's jittery heartbeat); the scene you need: the seating-chart confrontation. Decisions to transfer: *role* (drafter-for-revision — you'll rewrite everything anyway), *context* (three lines: the fight's stake is the mother's public verdict; the sister knows the chart is wrong on purpose), *task* (draft from your five hand-written beats), *constraints* (800–1,000 words; comedy register via understatement, *no* farce escalation; no one cries; unresolved; present, close third, no head-hopping), *output shape* (DRAFT: block only).

**Step 2 — Add your anchor.** Two paragraphs of your prose from Chapter 1's baseline or the milestone story — the ones that still sound like you when you read them cold. (Anchor placement: *after* instructions, *before* beats — near generation.)

**Step 3 — First run, grade compliance first.** Paste, run fresh, and check against constraints with a pen: present tense? (…) close third? (…) no tears? (…) unresolved? (…) Assume anything ungraded failed until shown otherwise. Typical first-run misses: the comedy slides to farce (your "no farce" line was vague — convert to "no pratfalls, no one locked in rooms, misdirection only through dialogue"), or the ending resolves with a hug (restate "unresolved" *as the last line of constraints* — nearness wins).

**Step 4 — Single-variable edits, version to v2.** Change the farce constraint to its concrete form; rerun; grade again. One variable at a time (debugger discipline). Log: `PC-01-yourname v1 → v2 · farce→concrete-ban · 2026-09-XX`.

**Step 5 — Critique leg (PC-02) on the best run.** Fresh session: the five questions against your output; adjudicate — typically you'll keep structure, rewrite 40% of sentences (voice-anchor doing its job — Chapter 14 will name and systematize that 40% instinct).

**Step 6 — Freeze the card, keep knobs visible.** Save `scene-drafter-[yours]` with version line, blanks marked `[...]`, and a two-line "when this fails" note (e.g., "fails on action scenes — beats need physical causality, not dialogue beats"). That failure note is what makes a prompt file a *tool library* instead of a scrapbook: every card carries its own edge-wear.

Commentary on the whole build: nothing clever happened — decisions → constraints → anchor → compliance grading → single-variable versioning → critique. The machine's contribution was *throughput* (a draft to grade in seconds); your contribution was *every line that made grading possible*. Run this build once properly and you've learned the chapter; the six cards and every card you write for the rest of the book are variations.

## Exercises

- **Try (15 min):** Fix five broken prompts (provided in Appendix B's mirror — attempt first): (1) "Write something sad about the sea."; (2) "Make this paragraph better."; (3) "Continue my story in the same style." [no story attached]; (4) "Write a cool dialogue between two spies, very realistic and very witty."; (5) "Critique my chapter." [no chapter, no criteria]. For each: name the missing slot(s), rewrite with all five, convert at least one adjective to a checkable constraint. Five rewrites in fifteen minutes is the right pace — anatomy becomes reflex through reps, not re-reading.

- **Stretch (1–2 h):** Build and test your scene-drafter, following the worked example's six steps on a real scene from your milestone story or current project: two runs minimum, compliance graded both times, one single-variable fix logged as v2, one PC-02 critique pass, your adjudication written in five lines. Then *deliberately break it once*: feed it a beat list with an internal contradiction (e.g., "quiet scene, no raised voices" + a beat "she screams") and watch which constraint wins — that experiment teaches more about constraint priority than any theory paragraph.

- **Project (ongoing):** Start your personal prompt file (`prompts/` folder — Chapter 7's anatomy), seeded with your tested scene-drafter and copies of PC-01…PC-06 you've *actually run at least once* (cards you've run are tools; cards you've收藏ed are shopping). Habit: every prompt you reuse twice gets an ID, a version line, and a one-line failure note. By Chapter 16 this file is your private prompt library — the book's Appendix A was built exactly this way, and yours should eventually embarrass the book's with your own cases.

## Pitfalls

> PITFALL — **One-shot hoping.** *Symptom:* you re-roll the same prompt five, eight, twelve times waiting for magic; drafts accumulate in the chat like lottery tickets, none graded, nothing learned; the vocabulary of luck enters your notes ("this one *feels* right"). *Why it happens:* re-rolling is cheaper-feeling than diagnosing (no self-confrontation required), and the first-run lottery genuinely pays off often enough in brainstorming to wire the habit where it doesn't belong — by drafting time, you're sampling distribution shape without knowing it (Chapter 5's randomness), so the failures you hit are *predictable properties of the prompt*, and no number of draws fixes a broken slot. *Fix:* the debugger's order — three fresh runs only to classify *systematic vs. draw*; then four forces; then one-variable edits with version bumps; compliance grading with a pen before taste. Two refine passes cap, then hands-on intervention (loop hygiene). *Prevention:* version lines and decisions-log entries make hoping *visible* — a prompt with v1 and no tests is allowed to be lucky once; anything you reach for twice must be a card with compliance history.

Related failures for Appendix C: F-10 (prompt works once, never again — randomness + unversioned prompts), F-05 (agreeable brainstorming — previewed: your critique legs need adversarial roles or they'll flatter; full treatment Chapter 8).

## Checklist

> CHECKLIST — You're ready to move on when:
> - [ ] Every prompt you write now contains — or consciously omits — all five slots, and your constraints read as checkable rules, not mood adjectives.
> - [ ] You own a tested scene-drafter card with a version line, an anchor, and a failure note.
> - [ ] You've run a full draft → critique → refine loop and adjudicated it in writing (Stretch log).
> - [ ] You debug prompts by the four forces + single-variable edits instead of re-rolling until luck arrives.
> - [ ] Your prompt file exists and holds at least PC-01…PC-06 *plus* one card that is yours alone.

## Key takeaways

- **Prompt craft is pre-writing in machine-readable form:** role, context, task, constraints, output shape — five slots, each a story decision transferred before generation.
- **Constraints beat adjectives:** convert every mood word into a checkable structural rule; prohibition pairs with replacement; examples outrank instructions, so police your few-shots.
- **Iterate in legs (draft → critique → refine), chain for multi-step work, cap refine passes, adjudicate everything** — the loop's value is your taste's reps, not the model's throughput.
- **Few-shot with your *own* prose is the voice lever** — anchor carefully, frame as data, name what to abstract; it transfers surface statistics, not judgment (Chapter 14 finishes the job).
- **Debug, don't gamble:** four forces, three-run classification, one variable per edit, version lines on everything — a prompt you can't debug is a prompt you don't own yet.


# Chapter 07 — Your Writing Environment & Project Setup

## Opening hook

The horror story every writer eventually lives: it is 1 a.m., three weeks after the best chapter you've ever written — the chapter that finally *earns* the ending you've promised since Chapter 1 — and the document in front of you says the last save was sometime before you experimented with "the version where the sister stays." You remember dragging files around. You remember a prompt window where the assistant returned a rewritten chapter you accepted on the spot. You remember, distantly, closing something that asked "Save changes?" You open the backup folder: same timestamp. The cloud sync: same timestamp. The chat history: forty pages of prompts, and somewhere in there the chapter — but which paragraph was v1 and which was the machine's take you meant to rewrite?

You did not lose the chapter because you lack talent. You lost it because *project structure was optional until it was fatal* — and AI-assisted writing multiplies the risk: every accepted generation is a fork you might want back, every chat session is fragmentary memory pretending to be the real thing. The fix is unglamorous and completely reliable: **a folder anatomy, a versioning rule, a story bible, and a session rhythm** — fifteen minutes of setup that turns months of AI-assisted drafting from archaeological dig into clean commit history. This chapter installs all four, live, using Example A's project as the specimen, and by the end your Project folder will exist with a real Draft 1 inside it.

## Learning objectives

By the end of this chapter, you will be able to:

- [ ] Set up the book's project folder anatomy for your story.
- [ ] Version drafts with numbering plus changelogs.
- [ ] Maintain a story bible and a session log.
- [ ] Run sustainable writing sessions with AI chat history as project memory — and its limits.

## Project folder anatomy

One folder per story. Inside it, five subfolders and two loose files — the complete anatomy used everywhere in this book:

```text
my-story/
├── manuscript/        # the book itself — numbered drafts only
│   ├── draft-01.md
│   ├── draft-02.md
│   └── ...
├── bible/             # story bible + character sheets (Ch 3, below)
├── research/          # fact sources, policy notes, period detail
├── prompts/           # your prompt cards + tested variants (Ch 6)
├── cuts/              # everything you delete from the manuscript
├── changelog.md       # what changed between drafts, one line each
└── session-log.md     # what you did when, for how long, next step
```

The rules that make the anatomy work are simpler than the tree:

- **`manuscript/` holds numbered drafts only — and the current number is always complete enough to compile.** `draft-03.md` is a *whole* manuscript (however rough), not a scratch file. You never edit an old number in place; a new draft number is minted when a meaningful revision completes (the pass system of Chapter 12 defines "meaningful" precisely). Working fragments — scenes in progress — live inside the current draft or in clearly named work files (`wip-ch09-kitchen.md`) that get *folded in* before any new number is minted. The invariant: at any moment, you can hand someone `draft-current` and they can read your story start to finish.
- **`cuts/` is non-negotiable, and it changes your psychology.** Every paragraph, scene, and subplot removed from the manuscript gets pasted here with a date line — because the real reason writers fail to cut ("but what if I need it later") is that "later" has nowhere to live. A cut with an address stops being a loss. AI-assisted drafting *increases* cuts (faster generation → more surplus → more deletion), so the folder starts on day one, not when you first feel brave. Naming convention: `2026-09-24-ch03-mothers-letter-scene.md` — date, origin, content; the origin tag matters when you're mining cuts in Chapter 12.
- **`prompts/` and `research/` are where trustable references accumulate.** Prompt cards from Chapter 6 (yours *and* the book's copies you've tested); research notes with their sources and dates (Appendix E's protocol at personal scale: source, date accessed, one line of what it supports). When Chapter 15 asks you to re-verify a policy, the folder is the checklist.
- **Loose files live loose on purpose:** `changelog.md` and `session-log.md` sit at root because they apply to *every* draft and *every* session — filing them would hide them, and hidden logs die.

**Tool-agnostic by design.** This anatomy is folders and markdown; any editor can open it; any sync tool can carry it; any model can be pasted from it. If you live in a word processor instead, mirror the structure (documents named `draft-03`, a `Cuts` document, a `Changelog` document) — the *invariants* are what matter: numbered complete states, addressed cuts, one-line change history. Named products come and go (Chapter 5's dial #5); your project should outlive any of them.

> TRY IT — Create the seven items above right now, with your real story's name, even if the story is one paragraph long. If the story doesn't exist yet, create it from your Chapter 2 skeleton — the skeleton *is* draft material, and a project folder with one page already beats a brilliant idea in a notes app. Three minutes; do it before finishing this page's section headings.

## Versioning without tears

Versioning sounds bureaucratic and behaves like insurance you're *grateful* for the first time a revision goes sideways. The whole system is two habits:

**Habit 1 — Draft numbering.** `draft-01`, `draft-02`, forward only, integers never overwritten. When is a new number minted? When a *coherent state change* completes: all scenes of a pass applied (Chapter 12's structure pass done → `draft-04`), a milestone redraft of Part Two folded in, beta feedback from a full round integrated. Not minted: mid-sentence saves, every session, or the machine's raw output (that's FILL material, not a draft). Inside a number, your editor's autosave does what it likes — the *number boundary* is the granularity of memory. Typical novella rhythm: `draft-01` (first end-to-end draft) → `02` (self-edit structure) → `03` (character pass) → `04` (first beta round) → `05` (line pass) → `06` (final proof state). Six states for a whole novel is normal; twelve means passes are being split into slivers (resist — see Chapter 12's non-convergence pitfall); one means you're flying without instruments.

**Habit 2 — The changelog, one line per mint.** Format: `- draft-03 → 04 · 2026-09-24 · structure pass: cut subplot B, moved storm arrival from ch6 to ch4; notes: [link/decisions-file range]`. The line answers *what changed and why* in under thirty seconds of future reading — and the `notes` hook points at the adjudication trail (decisions file, pass log) where the *full* why lives. Three changelog payoffs compound across a project: **reversibility** (a bad revision direction is discovered at draft-05? You can see exactly what draft-04→05 did and selectively restore — no archaeology); **rhythm awareness** (skim the log; if every entry says "polish," you're Chapter 12's F-07 case); and **authorship evidence** (dates + descriptions of your human adjudications; Chapter 15 will point back here as the kind of process record that documents *your* authorship — keep it factual and unhyped).

One AI-specific guard: **machine outputs never mint drafts.** The assistant's chapter rewrite is a proposal; it lives in the chat and then (if worth keeping) as a file in `wip-` space or a cut — the number advances only when *you* have integrated, edited, and accepted the state as a readable manuscript. This single rule prevents the worst AI-versioning pathology: a manuscript folder where you can no longer distinguish your prose from generations you never actually reviewed.

> CRAFT NOTE — "But git does this better." Version control tools *do* exist and writers do use them; they are also a hobbyist tax for this audience — the mental model (numbered states + one-line logs + never overwrite) is the transferable skill, and the simple system is the one you'll still run at 11 p.m. after a full day of work. If you already speak git fluently, use it, and keep this book's changelog anyway: your future self reads lines, not diffs. The failure mode to avoid isn't choosing plain files — it's *choosing nothing*.

## The story bible

The **story bible** is the project's long-term memory: a single reference document (or small folder of documents — `bible/` can hold `characters.md`, `setting.md`, `timeline.md` once it grows) containing the facts every later stage must *not* re-invent. For AI-assisted work specifically, the bible has a doubled role: it's what *you* consult for continuity, and it's what gets **restated onto the model's desk** at the start of fact-dependent tasks (Chapter 5's shared-desk rule — the bible is the antidote to macro-amnesia). Bible-first prompting — leading continuity, critique, and drafting tasks with the relevant bible extract — is the practice Chapters 9–12 will lean on constantly; it starts with having a bible worth pasting.

**The template** (starter scale — grows by section, not by guilt):

```text
STORY BIBLE — [title]                          v[__]  updated: [date]

1. PREMISE & STAKES: the Chapter 2 skeleton, kept current (paste it).
2. CHARACTERS: for each — the Ch 3 sheet (or cut-down cast version).
   One line each for walk-ons: name · function · one trait.
3. TIMELINE: story-internal order of events with day markers;
   real-world anchors only where needed (seasons, dates, ages).
4. SETTING FACTS: place rules that must never contradict — geography,
   institutions, technology/magic limits (Ch 4's constraints), names
   and spellings (THE canonical spellings list — the single most
   consulted section you will ever maintain).
5. STORY RULES: what this story promises and forbids (no one lies
   off-page? POV never leaves Mara? the storm is never comic?) —
   the constraint packs from Ch 6 hang here.
6. OPEN THREADS: unresolved seeds with the chapter planted and the
   chapter intended to pay off — the cheapest late-book insurance
   there is.
7. DECISIONS LEDGER: pointer to the decisions file + the five
   decisions most likely to be second-guessed, with their one-line
   why.
```

**Maintenance rhythm beats maintenance zeal.** Update the bible *at draft-number minting* (new number → ten-minute bible sync) and whenever a *fact* changes mid-session (the storm moved → timeline line updated *before* you close the editor). The failure mode isn't a bible that's briefly stale; it's a bible that's *selectively* stale — half-current facts are worse than none, because you and the model will both trust them (F-06's internal cousin: confident contradictions against a document that used to be right). If a fact appears in a draft but not in the bible, the bible is wrong *by definition* until synced — the manuscript is canon; the bible is the index; the loop closes at every mint.

## Sessions that compound

Structure supports the folder; **rhythm** supports the writer. Three session mechanics, tuned for AI-assisted work specifically:

**Session skeleton (the twenty-second version).** Open → *reread the last 500 words* (orientation, always — even when you wrote them an hour ago) → *state today's one outcome in one line at the top of your notes* ("kitchen scene drafted; bible synced") → *work* → *close with the session log filled and the next step written*. That last line is the whole trick of sustainable practice: **end every session mid-sentence or with the next sentence specified**, so tomorrow's start has zero decision cost. AI sessions add one step: before closing a productive chat, *harvest it* — copy the accepted output to `wip-`, paste the prompt that worked into `prompts/` (if reusable), one decisions line if substantive. Chats are workshops, not warehouses; harvest or lose.

**Session log format** (`session-log.md`, one block per session — this exact shape feeds Appendix F habits too):

```text
## 2026-09-24 · 07:10–07:55 · words: +840 (draft-03 @ 41,200)
Goal: draft kitchen confrontation from beats 4–6.
Mode: voice-anchor co-draft (PC-01 v2) → hand rewrite ~50%.
Adjudications: 2 accepted (deadline fact, doorway image), 5 rejected
  (generic summaries — see decisions 014–018).
Bible: timeline synced (storm = day 2); no new names.
Next: Theo's reaction scene — start from "the wrong kind of clean".
Blockers: none | fog: mild — resolved by beat list.
```

Counting words: **total at session end minus session start**, pasted at the left — thirty seconds, no app. Word counts are *instrumentation, not targets* (Chapter 16's 90-day plan converts them into targets deliberately; during learning stages they only answer "am I moving?"). Log them anyway, because the log's real payload is the *Mode* and *Adjudications* lines: those entries are how you'll later answer Chapter 14's comparison question ("what does my accepted-AI prose actually cost me?") and how you'll notice drift patterns ("last six sessions: mostly rejected expansions — I'm over-generating").

**Chat history as project memory — and its limits.** It's tempting to treat your chat threads as the project's second brain: they contain decisions, experiments, tone calibration. They are a *supplementary* memory with three hard limits: (1) **searchability is poor** — threads don't query well, and platform retention can change under you (Chapter 5's policy hygiene); (2) **it's raw** — chats mix accepted work, rejected experiments, and machine fluff with no adjudication markers; (3) **it's not portable** — your project must be reconstructable from *your folders alone* if the tool disappears tomorrow. The working policy: **hubs and spokes** — folders are the hub (canon: drafts, bible, logs, harvested prompts); chats are spokes (live workshops). Anything that matters gets *promoted* to the hub at session end (the harvest step). If you catch yourself about to ask a chat "what did we decide about Theo's scar?" — that's the bible failing its job; answer from the hub, then fix the hub.

> REALITY CHECK — No setup survives contact with enthusiasm *or* avoidance: the two setup failures are the perfectionist (seven days tuning folders, zero words — the pitfall below) and the romantically disorganized (brilliant sessions, no memory — the hook's 1 a.m. self). Both fail the same invariant: *could a stranger (or future you) reconstruct the story's state in ten minutes?* Everything above — numbers, logs, bible, harvest — serves only that question. If your current setup can't pass it this week, retrofit the invariants; don't redesign the aesthetics.

### Session architectures that survive real life

The session skeleton fits *inside* a session; these three patterns arrange whole weeks — pick by your actual life, not your fantasy one, and re-check at Chapter 16's 90-day plan:

**Pattern A — Maker's morning (best for drafts-in-motion).** Writing block first, before email and chat: orientation read (5 min), one outcome stated (1 min), drafting or co-drafting with anchors (40–90 min), harvest + log + next-sentence line (10 min), *then* the rest of the world. The AI chats open only after the day's outcome sentence is written — chats are tools serving today's line, not morning entertainment that pre-loads the desk with everyone else's priorities (Chapter 5's shared desk applies to *your* attention too).

**Pattern B — Split-shift (jobs, caregiving, fragmented days).** Two labeled halves: a *maker* half (≥35 uninterrupted minutes — drafts, anchors, hand rewrites only) and a *manager* half (prompts prepared, continuity checks, bible sync, prompt-file filing, harvest cleanup). The iron rule: **manager work never happens inside maker time**, and maker work never starts with a chat — you may open a session *mid-scene*, but the first keystroke is yours. On days with only a manager half: prep beats (write tomorrow's FILL-B beat list), sync the bible, mine cuts — project-forward motion without pretending fragmented minutes are drafting minutes.

**Pattern C — Weekend batch (the one-weekend-a-week life).** Saturday = generation day (bible review, prompt prep, 2–3 co-drafted scenes with full anchors); Sunday = adjudication day (rewrite passes, compliance grading, changelog/bible sync, next weekend's beat prep). Generation and adjudication on different days is the secret structure: it guarantees the *human pass* always has fresh eyes, and it makes drift visible (Sunday's rewrites accumulate into a pattern you can actually see across one week's output).

All three share the invariants: one outcome line per session, harvest before close, next-step always written, drafts minted only at coherent boundaries. What varies is *when* chats open and *when* adjudication happens — decide that once, write the pattern name in your session log's header, and let the log's consistency do the coaching. If two weeks of blocks show the chosen pattern never actually firing (zero maker blocks logged), the pattern was cosplay: downgrade honestly (three 35-minute maker blocks beat two fantasy Saturdays) rather than keep aspirational architecture that trains you to distrust your own logs.

### The three audits your logs unlock

Logs are only romantic in the abstract; concretely they exist to run three cheap audits — schedule them or they won't happen:

1. **Weekly rhythm audit (10 minutes, end of week).** Skim session blocks: maker blocks vs. manager ratio; average words *per maker block* (not per calendar day — honest denominators); most common blocker; one log line of adjustment ("manager work crept into mornings — move chats after the outcome line"). The audit's output is *one* change for next week. Multiple simultaneous fixes are how systems die.
2. **Adjudication audit (at every draft mint, ~15 minutes).** Tally decisions-file rows since last mint: accepted/rejected ratio, and the *categories* of acceptances (structure? deadline facts? description expansions?). Two failure signatures to hunt: acceptances clustering in a category you never intended to delegate (usually description — the machine's easiest sell), or a rejection rate so high you're acting as a full-time copy desk for a generator you don't need (trim the generation mode — Chapter 10's mode table will help you choose better modes per scene type).
3. **Voice drift spot-check (monthly, 20 minutes).** Pull three AI-assisted paragraphs and three hand-written ones from the current draft; read them cold, unlabeled: can you *reliably* tell which is which? If not — especially if the machine's are the ones you'd sign — Chapter 14's diagnostic and Chapter 10's anchor discipline need tightening *now*, while the sample is small. This audit is the early-warning system for the book's central risk (F-09 voice erosion); it takes twenty minutes a month because the logs already point at where the samples live.

None of these audits is a report card — they're instrumentation readings. The session log, changelog, and decisions file are the project's instruments; the audits are simply *scheduled glances at the dials*. Un-glanced instruments drift into decoration, and decoration is how the 1 a.m. hook happens twice.

### Backups: one paragraph, no hobby

The versioning system above protects you from *your* revisions; backups protect you from *everything else* — disk death, sync bugs, the coffee, the account lockout. You need exactly one paragraph of policy, not a homelab:

- **3 copies, 2 different places, 1 of them off-site** (the classic 3-2-1, compressed): your project folder lives on your machine (copy 1) and in *two* independent targets — a synced drive or cloud folder (copy 2, also your off-site via provider redundancy) and a periodic plain zip dropped on *any* second service or an external drive (copy 3, different technology). Automate the zip if you can (weekly, named `my-story-backup-YYYY-MM-DD.zip`), manual if you can't — a calendar reminder Friday at 17:00 is automation enough.
- **When:** backups run *at every draft mint* (numbered states compress perfectly) plus weekly during active drafting. Mint-and-backup become one ritual: change log line → zip → sync; three minutes, end of session.
- **Test once a quarter:** open one random old backup and *read a file*. A backup you've never restored is a rumor.

That's the whole topic. Provider-side sync is *not* a backup (it faithfully mirrors deletions and ransomware alike), which is why the periodic zip exists separately — and why `cuts/` gets backed up with everything else: your deletions are history too.

### Naming everything, once

Ambiguity in filenames compounds into archaeology; five conventions end the category:

| Thing | Convention | Example |
|---|---|---|
| Drafts | `draft-NN` forward-only, zero-padded | `draft-03.md` |
| Work-in-progress files | `wip-chNN-topic` | `wip-ch09-kitchen.md` |
| Cuts | `YYYY-MM-DD-chNN-topic` | `2026-09-24-ch03-mothers-letter.md` |
| Prompt cards | `PC-NN-slug` + version line inside | `PC-01-scene-drafter.md` |
| Backups | `my-story-backup-YYYY-MM-DD.zip` | `…-2026-09-26.zip` |

Plus two spelling decisions that pay for themselves the day a continuity check runs: **canonical spellings live in the bible's section 4** (the list is the single most-pasted bible section during AI sessions — every drafting prompt that names a person or place pastes from it), and **file names use ASCII hyphens and lowercase-with-hyphens** (portable across every tool you'll ever sync through). Write the table at the top of your changelog if you like; five lines consulted twice a week is cheap insurance against the "which file is current?" species of the lost-draft story.

### FAQ from the workshop floor

*What if I already have a messy project mid-story?* Retrofit, don't restart: compile the current state as `draft-01`, dump everything ambiguous into `cuts/` or `wip-`, write a changelog line "baseline import from disorganized state," paste what's decided into a starter bible, and adopt conventions going forward. Retroactive perfection is tooling procrastination in a trench coat; *forward* hygiene is the whole requirement.

*Should the bible be one file or many?* Start one file until it exceeds comfortable scrolling (roughly a novella's worth of sections filled), then split by section groups (`characters.md`, `setting-timeline.md`, `rules-threads.md`) with the premise/skeleton staying in the root file you paste most. Split when retrieval hurts, not before — three files to find one name is its own black hole.

*Where do worldbuilding *experiments* live — the 4,000-word history?* `research/` as `world-lore-draft.md`, clearly labeled *non-canonical until promoted*. The bible holds what the *story* has decided (Ch 4's worldbuilding-on-need discipline); the research folder holds the museum; promotion to bible = the lore earned a scene.

*Do I log AI sessions in the chat tool's own history features (projects, pinned threads)?* As *convenience*, never as structure — pins break, exports change, retention policies move (Ch 5). The hub's `prompts/`, harvests, and session-log pointers are the durable layer; treat every in-tool organization as a temporary desk arrangement you'd be fine losing tonight.

### What goes in which log (the three-column discipline)

Three logs now exist — session-log, changelog, decisions file — and their boundaries blur exactly when you're tired, which is when the blurring costs most. One table ends the ambiguity:

| Question you're answering… | Log | Entry shape |
|---|---|---|
| *What did I **do** and how long did it take?* | `session-log` | time-boxed block: goal, mode, word delta, next-step, blockers |
| *What did the **manuscript's state** change to?* | `changelog` | one line per mint: draft-N → N+1, reason, row pointers |
| *What did I **decide** and why (accept/reject/override)?* | `decisions.md` | one row per substantive judgment: suggestion, verdict, one-line reason |

The test-case trio, run through once so the boundaries stick: *"PC-21 suggested cutting paragraph 3; I rejected it"* → **decisions row** (a judgment). *"That session's edits completed, minting draft-06"* → **changelog line** (a state change). *"7:10–8:05, +840 words, co-draft mode, felt foggy until beat list"* → **session block** (an activity record). Cross-linking is encouraged and cheap (changelog lines cite decisions ranges; session blocks cite mint numbers) — *merging* is the failure (a "log" that holds all three questions holds none: you can't grep decisions out of prose-blocks at Ch 12's adjudication-audit speed, and the changelog's clean history drowns). The deep reason for the split beyond retrieval: **the three logs answer three different accountability audiences** — session-log = *your future motivation* (capacity truth), changelog = *the project's structural memory* (and, per Ch 15, potential authorship evidence — which is precisely why its lines stay factual and state-focused, never argumentative), decisions = *your taste on trial* (the taste reps' scorecard since Chapter 1). Instruments aimed at different questions must not share formats; the moment you feel clever combining them, read the hook of this chapter again — clever structure is what the lost-draft story is made of.

**A worked two-week review** (the Project exercise's payoff, shown at full resolution so "review the pattern" has a concrete shape): pull fourteen days of session blocks. *Sample findings from a realistic record:* maker blocks: 9 (target was 12) — but words-per-maker-block: 780 (capacity *underestimated*: original fantasy assumed 1,200 — the 90-day plan later will use 780×0.85 ≈ 660 as the honest planning number); manager-drift: 4 blocks opened with chat-before-outcome-line (the morning-rules violation — all four on Mon/Wed, meeting-days: *calendar correlation found*); mode column: 7 co-draft / 2 dictate / 0 expand — "expand" unused *because the beat lists were being written too late to feed it* (blockers column says "beat list rushed" ×3 — the mode table's *pipeline* upstream of drafting is starving); adjudications: 23 rows, accept-rate 34% (healthy skepticism — Ch 1's gate holding; if this had read 80%+, red flag); blockers vocabulary: "fog before beat list" ×5 — one *process* word, not five moods. *The ten-line findings written at the log's header* become: three schedule changes (beat-list written *night before* — kills the fog + unstars expand; Mon/Wed sessions start unaided-outcome-only; capacity planning number set to 660), one instrument check (accept-rate target band 30–60% adopted into the weekly audit), one celebration with evidence (gate holding at 34%), and one open question for next review (Sunday-batch pattern untested — try one). Note what the review *didn't* contain: feelings about talent, comparisons to anyone's output, resolution to "write more" without a *mechanism* (the beat-list-night-before fix is a mechanism; "try harder Monday" isn't). **Ten lines, two minutes a day's data, one month's trajectory bent** — the log system's entire ROI stated in one worked example: instrumentation you'll actually do beats analytics you admire.

### The honest word count

A paragraph on the project's most-abused number, because the session-log keeps asking you for one. **What to count:** words *in the current draft file* at session end minus session start — the delta, counted only what *entered draft-current* (your wip-to-draft promotions count; your *deletions* don't subtract from the delta — record cuts in the changelog's reason instead, or the number lies by making good editing look like failure; some days you'll net *negative* and that's a *mint-worthy* pass day, flagged "(−310: line pass)" in the block so the capacity review doesn't misread collapse); **what not to count:** prompt drafts, bible edits, decisions rows (they're *time*-logged, not word-logged — the block's minutes column already values them), chat outputs before adjudication (machine words are *proposals*, not manuscript — counting them is the inflation trap this book's premise-scorn has been about since Chapter 1: text volume celebrates exactly the thing the tool gives free; the session-log's word column exists to track *your manuscript's growth rate*, nothing else — which is why the weekly review reads it *beside* accept-rates, never alone: words up + accept-rate down = generating faster than judging = the next week's adjustment *writes itself*). **Tooling:** word processor's status bar, copied into the block — deliberately *no counting app* (apps that count *every* keystroke across *every* file produce numbers with no manuscript attached — the ambiguity that breeds 1 a.m. archaeology; delta-per-draft-file is the only variant whose failure mode is "mildly wrong," the standard worth holding in a project built on logs you'll actually maintain).

### Session-log formats: the four lines that must never bend

The session-log's field set was introduced as a template; this section hardens it into *format law*, because logs die by field-creep (fields added in enthusiasm, abandoned in fatigue — the graveyard's most common occupant):

**Line 1 — `goal:`** one sentence, *closed-form* ("draft the kitchen scene to beats" — not "work on the book" — the field's test: *at session end, could a stranger check this box?* — open goals can't be checked, unchecked goals can't teach capacity, and capacity numbers built on uncheckable goals are *astrology with timestamps*; the Monday-decision version of this law: goals get written *the night before* wherever possible (the beat-list-night-before fix's log-side twin: decision made fresh, executed tired — goal-drift collapses when the goal pre-exists the session's mood)); **Line 2 — `modes:`** the drafting-mode column *filled* (hand-first / co-draft / dictate / expand / dialogue-first — the column Ch 10's mode table and Ch 14's audits *read*: an empty modes cell isn't missing data, it's a *missed session-decision* — the pre-choice rule's enforcement point lives here: **you cannot log a mode you never chose**, which is precisely the feedback loop — three empty cells in a row and the pattern's self-evident: sessions are *starting* rather than *beginning*); **Line 3 — `delta:`** the word-count or pass-page *delta into draft-current only* (the honest-counting section's law, applied: prompt drafts and bible edits get `notes:` mentions, never delta-inflation — and negative deltas allowed with their reason-tag, "(−310: line pass)" — because a *log that can't record good-bad-days* gets abandoned on bad days, which is when you need it most: **the field's survivability beats its precision** — a log you'll actually keep at 11 p.m. is worth three logs you'd design for a better self); **Line 4 — `next:`** the *specific* next-step, written to be *grab-able* ("open at beat 3's door-line; mode already chosen: dialogue-first" — the field answering tomorrow-session's first two minutes, which is where most sessions *actually* die (not mid-work: *pre*-work, in the blank minute where re-orientation happens — `next:` deletes the blank minute: the session-log's smallest field with the largest effect on *tomorrow's row existing at all*); plus the **`adjudications:`** line (row-count-or-zero — the explicit one: "0 rows" written *honestly* after a genuine critique is a finding; "0 rows" *unwritten* is F-05's signature — the field exists to make the zero *visible*, because invisible zeros accumulate into a year of sessions where the machine talked and *nobody wrote down what they decided*).

**The format law, then:** these four-and-a-half lines are *fixed schema* — new fields may be *added* (blockers, energy, caffeine notes — instrument your own weather), but **the core lines never get dropped for brevity** (the failure mode's shape: tired-writer skips `goal:` "just this once" → end-of-session can't check capacity → weekly review's block-kept metric goes fuzzy → the audit's trust in its own data erodes → the *whole instrument* deprecated by slow attrition — field-law exists because *every* abandoned log in this book's workshop history died by a thousand "just this once" cuts, never by one dramatic abandonment — hence the corollary: **when a core line genuinely can't be filled (the session ended mid-catastrophe), write `INTERRUPTED` + the partial lines anyway** — an interrupted row preserves the day's *fragments* for pattern-analysis (interruptions *are* a pattern: three INTERRUPTEDs in a month = a *calendar* finding, not a character-flaw) — the log records *what happened to the writing*, and what happened includes the plumber, the fever, and the email that couldn't wait; **format law serving instrument-integrity: schema fixed, content compassionate, zeros visible, interruptions data** — four rules, the field set's entire constitution, printed once so the template's little `goal:` box never again looks optional.

### The folder anatomy, annotated to the shelf-life of each file

The `low-tide/` walkthrough showed *where* things go; the missing half is *how long each thing lives* — the **shelf-life column** the anatomy table deserves, because temporary-feeling files left unmanaged are how folders return to archaeology-digs:

| File/dir | What it is | Shelf-life & fate |
|---|---|---|
| `00-inbox/` | unsorted arrivals: research finds, quotes, screenshots | **≤30 days old by rule** — monthly audit's first filter: sort into research/ or cuts/ or delete; an inbox item aged >30 days is a *decision you're postponing*, flagged in the audit row |
| `bible/` | skeleton, sheets, timeline, settings, rules, open threads | **living — versioned with the manuscript** (sheet edits mint changelog rows like prose edits; at project close: *freeze* — the bible's last state archived beside draft-final, never edited post-publication except for *series* continuity, at which point it's a *new* version with a new project's name) |
| `manuscript/` | `draft-current` + numbered mints + pass-logs | **mints are permanent** (the reversibility chain — *never* pruned, even at 40 drafts: disk is cheap, rollback is priceless; at project close: mint history *shipped to archive intact* — the authorship-evidence trail's final form); pass-logs permanent too (Appendix F's data) |
| `prompts/` | card instances with versions + failure notes | **instances: life of project**; **cards: the durable asset** — promoted instances graduate to your personal library file (project-independent), retired ones keep their autopsies (Ch 6's lifecycle: the *instance* dies, the *learning* migrates) |
| `research/` | why-me file, venue dossier, source-harvests, claim rows' evidence | **living across projects** (why-me and venues-file are *career*-institutions — they outlive any single manuscript by design); per-project source-harvests: archived with the project, *re-checked* not copied, when a sequel reuses perishables |
| `cuts/` | every removed passage, dated, one-line reason | **permanent** (the graveyard's whole value is *retrievability* — F-07 and the parking-ritual both mine it; deletion from cuts/ requires the same reversibility respect as draft-deletes: nothing leaves the graveyard except by *conscious resurrection*, logged as a changelog row) |
| `log/` | session-log, changelog, decisions, audits, adr/ | **permanent, append-mostly** (the three append-only disciplines: decisions, ADRs, changelog — corrections add rows; audit.md *sections* may be summarized annually but raw rows stay — this directory *is* the professional record: capacity data for planning, taste data for Ch 14's audits, provenance data for Ch 15's disclosures, and — the word this book earned the right to use — *authorship evidence* for anyone who ever asks what you did) |
| backups | the 3-2-1 copies | **continuous by automation** (the one shelf-life that's *measured in hours*: no backup younger than 24h for active projects — the Sunday-audit's first health-check when the folder-audit's flag fires) |

The shelf-life column's revealed logic: **files split into three permanence-classes — *living* (bible, research's career-files, current logs), *permanent-record* (mints, pass-logs, decisions, ADRs, cuts, all log rows), and *composted* (inbox items, session debris, prompt instances post-graduation)** — and the archaeology-problem this book opened with was never *mess*; it was **everything treated as compost-class** (undifferentiated *stuff*, no retention rules, so preservation-panic either hoarded all of it or lost half of it — the two failure extremes the permanence-classes now *route around*: what must be kept has a *named home with append-only law*, what should flow has a *scheduled exit*, and the question "should I delete this?" — the question that stalled whole projects pre-system — *answers itself at filing time by class* (the inbox item's 30-day rule deciding for you every month-end: the folder's organization was always going to come from *retention decisions made in advance* or *guilt performed in retrospect*; this table chooses the first, once, in writing).

## Worked example: Example A's project, set up live

Watch the anatomy fill with real material — this is the exact folder the rest of Part III will grow inside, shown at the moment Chapter 8 begins (project birth) rather than at some idealized end state:

```text
low-tide/
├── manuscript/
│   └── draft-01.md          # the Ch 4 milestone story, retitled
│                            # as the story's opening state (2,410 w)
├── bible/
│   └── story-bible.md       # v1, filled below (excerpt)
├── research/
│   └── sources.md           # empty but present; seeds in Ch 8–9:
│                            # tide tables (primary: maritime
│                            # authority PDFs), cannery closure
│                            # dates (town archive), storm categories
├── prompts/
│   ├── PC-01-scene-drafter.md      # tested copy, v2
│   ├── PC-02-critique.md           # as printed, unedited
│   └── scene-drafter-mara-anchor.md # anchors: Mara's Ch4 paragraph
├── cuts/
│   └── 2026-09-23-ch01-ferry-ride-opening.md   # dated, addressed
├── changelog.md             # - draft-01 · 2026-09-23 · baseline:
│                            #   unaided milestone draft imported
└── session-log.md           # first block filled (format above)
```

And the bible's first page, excerpted — notice it is *the book's own earlier documents, transcribed*, not new invention:

```text
STORY BIBLE — "Low Tide"                        v1   updated: 2026-09-23

1. PREMISE & STAKES: [Ch 2 skeleton pasted verbatim — all 12 fields]
2. CHARACTERS: Mara Solano [Ch 3 sheet v1]; Theo Solano [cut-down:
   29, mechanic, stayed; want: keep house as-is; flaw-pattern:
   substitutes silent labor for conversation; foil to Mara];
   Dona Aurelia [walk-on +: neighbor, 78, mother's friend — one
   line in Ch 4 scene, function: delivers the December fact
   secondhand later]; Casi Duarte [cut-down: buyer, 37, wants cliff
   property for seasonal rental project; foil: better-organized
   version of Mara's escape impulse]
3. TIMELINE: D-14 notary letter (disturbance, post-Ch4 draft) ·
   D0 Mara arrives (Ch 4 scene — storm warning posted) · D+1 storm
   landfall (power outage = midpoint target) · …
4. SETTING FACTS: Porto Alvaro: one main street, harbor + cliff
   path; house = former cannery factor's residence; February =
   storm season, ferry unreliable; NAMES LIST: Solano, Porto
   Alvaro, cannery "Conservera Alvor" — canonical spellings locked.
5. STORY RULES: POV never leaves Mara (scene-break = time skip,
   not anchor change); storm never comic; no one says "closure";
   the journal's contents stay ambiguous except two quoted entries.
6. OPEN THREADS: mother's December request (planted Ch 4 — pay Ch
   ~midpoint); scar-hand laterality (bible: LEFT — Ch 4 para has
   it; consistency check loaded); tide journal (plant Ch 8 outline,
   reveal Ch 10 draft); third place at table (pay: fight scene).
7. DECISIONS LEDGER: see decisions.md; top-5 pinned: (a) keep
   Mara's POV exclusive — rejected omniscient option 2026-09-22
   (distance control); (b) buyer is human, not villain — cost
   noted: needs the organized-escape foil beat; …
```

Read section 5 twice: **the story rules are Chapter 6's constraint packs lifted to project altitude** — "no one says closure" graduated from a prompt line to a canonical promise, because rules that only live in one prompt die with that prompt. Section 6's scar entry shows the loop closing *both ways*: Ch 4's prose and the bible agree today; Chapter 12's PC-22 continuity pass will machine-check that they still agree at draft-04 — the bible as shared source of truth for you *and* the assistant. And note what's deliberately empty (`research/sources.md`: present, seeded, waiting): the anatomy values *slots existing* over slots being full — an empty named shelf still tells you where tomorrow's fact belongs, which is half of why structure beats memory.
