The setup took under an hour including transcribing the skeleton and sheet. The changelog has one line; the log has one block; Draft 1 exists. Everything in Part III — every prompt that says "according to the bible," every continuity check, every changelog mint — runs on *this*, which is why this chapter came before the workflow chapters: you cannot bible-first prompt a bible that isn't there.

## Exercises

- **Try (15 min):** Create your project's seven items (folder tree, empty `changelog.md` with a header, `session-log.md` with one template block) and name your Draft 1 — even if Draft 1 is your Chapter 4 milestone story, your skeleton plus three scenes, or a single compiled opening. If nothing exists yet, draft-01.md contains the skeleton and a title page: *it exists*. The invariant to pass: a stranger opening `manuscript/draft-01.md` can start reading your story today.

- **Stretch (1–2 h):** Fill a starter story bible for your real project: paste your Chapter 2 skeleton into section 1; add cut-down sheets for your two central characters (Chapter 3's fields); start sections 3–6 with everything currently *decided* (timeline may be partial; names list is mandatory — canonical spellings today save three continuity fixes later); section 7 points at your decisions file with five pinned calls. Time-box: ninety minutes max — a starter bible that exists beats a complete bible that doesn't; it syncs at every future mint. Then run one **mock AI session** against it: paste the bible's relevant extract + Chapter 4's POV decision into a fresh chat and ask for a 150-word continuation of any open beat — not to keep the output, but to feel what bible-first context *does* to response quality compared with your old bare prompts.

- **Project (ongoing):** Log every session for two weeks, no exceptions — the full block format, including Mode and Adjudications (three words suffice on dead days: "edited by hand, no AI"). At day fourteen, review the pattern, not the prose: Which modes recur? What's your real words-per-session distribution (your *actual* capacity, not your fantasy — Chapter 16 builds the 90-day plan on this number)? Where do sessions habitually end (blockers column)? Write ten lines of findings in the log's header. Two weeks of honest instrumentation is worth more than any productivity system you could buy, because it's *yours* — and if the findings scare you slightly, good: the 90-day plan needs truth, not aspiration.

## Pitfalls

> PITFALL — **Tooling procrastination.** *Symptom:* the folder system grows furniture while the manuscript doesn't — six color-coded subcategories per folder, a template for the templates, an evening spent comparing editors instead of drafting; the changelog's most active document is itself; genuinely useful setup work becomes the procrastination it was meant to enable. *Why it happens:* setup feels like progress (it is progress — visible, finite, low-risk) while drafting feels like exposure; when fear of the draft rises, the organism reaches for organizational nesting. Also feeds on AI-era novelty: a new tool promising to "manage your AI writing workflow" arrives, migration becomes the week's project. *Fix:* the **"setup done in one session" rule** — anatomy created, Draft 1 named, bible starter pasted, log seeded, *closed*: total ninety minutes, timer enforced; after that, any folder-shaped impulse gets written as a one-line to-do in the session log ("organize prompts subfolder") and executed *only* at session end, max ten minutes, never at session start. *Prevention:* the invariant question (can a stranger read draft-01 today?) is the *ceiling* of required structure, not the floor — anything beyond the ceiling that doesn't serve that question is furniture. Migration policy: new tools import the existing folder tree *as-is* or not at all; your project is the folders, and tools are tenants.

Related failure for Appendix C: adjacent to F-07's family — setup-shaped non-convergence (systems that keep revising themselves instead of the book); prevention is the same done-criteria instinct, applied to infrastructure.

## Checklist

> CHECKLIST — You're ready to move on when:
> - [ ] Your project folder exists with all seven anatomy items, and `manuscript/draft-01.*` opens as readable story material (skeleton counts).
> - [ ] `changelog.md` has its first real line, and you can say in one sentence when a new draft number gets minted (and that machine outputs never mint one).
> - [ ] A starter story bible exists — sections 1–2 filled from skeleton + sheets; names list canonical; story rules started.
> - [ ] You've logged at least three sessions in the full block format, including Mode and Adjudications.
> - [ ] Your harvest habit ran once: a chat's accepted output promoted to the hub, a reusable prompt filed, decisions line written.

## Key takeaways

- **The folder anatomy is the project's skeleton:** numbered complete drafts, addressed cuts, bible, research, prompts, changelog, session log — tool-agnostic, reconstructable by a stranger in ten minutes, built so Part III's bible-first prompting has a bible to paste.
- **Two versioning habits, total:** forward-only draft numbers minted at coherent state changes (never by machine output), and a one-line changelog entry per mint with a pointer to adjudication notes — together they buy reversibility, rhythm awareness, and authorship evidence.
- **The bible is canon's index and the model's desk:** sync it at every mint and every fact-change; half-current facts are more dangerous than none; story rules are constraint packs at project altitude.
- **Sessions compound through closing rituals:** orientation read, one-line goal, harvest step, session log block, *next sentence specified* — with chat history as workshop spokes, never the hub.
- **Structure has two failure modes: the perfectionist's empty nest and the romantics' black hole.** The invariant question — *could a stranger reconstruct the state in ten minutes?* — is both ceiling and test; the "setup done in one session" rule keeps furniture from becoming the job.


# Chapter 08 — Premise & Concept Development with AI

> Scope note: this is the first workflow chapter — the machine enters the room. Running examples begin in earnest: **Example A** ("Low Tide," literary-contemporary) develops below from seed to selected premise; **Example B**'s genre scenes appear where a technique needs a different register. Everything assumes Chapters 2, 3, 7: a decisions file, a project folder, a skeleton you can compare candidates against.

## Opening hook

Ask a general assistant for story ideas and you will receive, with enthusiasm, fifty ideas. They will be: a time traveler who regrets one meeting; a detective who discovers the killer is himself; a child who befriends the monster under the bed; a woman who inherits a mysterious house; a robot who learns to love. Ask again tomorrow and you will receive them again — slightly recombined, equally polite, equally dead. Not because the machine is stupid, but because *unconstrained ideation is a vote for the statistical center*, and the center of the distribution of "story ideas ever proposed" is where stories go to be nobody's.

Now the other experiment. Five prompts, each carrying *your* constraints — a place you actually know, a job you actually understand, a fear you actually have, a formal restriction you chose (one location; told in receipts; no villain), a combination of two domains that don't belong together (tide tables + custody law). Five attempts at the same act of narrowing. You will get some clunkers; you will also get one idea that makes your hands want to type — not because it's "better written" than the fifty, but because it couldn't have existed without *you* in the prompt.

This chapter is the art and discipline of that difference. **Constrained ideation** (generating premises as a narrowing game, not a wishing well), **stress-testing** (killing darlings with adversarial prompts before they cost you a year), and **loglines** (compressing the survivor into a sentence you can steer by). These are the first cards of your prompt library — PC-07 through PC-10 — and by the end of the chapter you will have selected *one* premise, with a stress-tested logline, and parked the rest. That selection is the seed every later chapter grows: outline (9), draft (10), characters (11), revision (12). Skip it and the rest of the book becomes an elaborate way to avoid choosing.

## Learning objectives

By the end of this chapter, you will be able to:

- [ ] Generate premises with constrained ideation (not "give me ideas").
- [ ] Stress-test a premise for stakes, novelty, and fit.
- [ ] Write and refine a logline.
- [ ] Select one premise worth months of work — and park the rest.

## Constrained ideation

The governing insight from Chapter 6: the model's default output *is* the average of what's been asked before; your constraints are the only lever that pulls it away from that average — and simultaneously the only thing that makes an idea *yours*. Three techniques, escalating in force.

### What-if ladders (PC-07)

A **what-if ladder** takes one seed and forces it through successive rewrites, each rung adding a constraint that *costs* something. The anti-pattern it replaces is "brainstorm 20 premises" (quantity of neighbors); the ladder produces *lineages* (quality of descent). Run it:

> PROMPT CARD PC-07 — What-if ladder
>
> ```text
> 
> Role: ideation partner executing a ladder, not a list.
> Seed: [one sentence situation].
> Produce 10 successive WHAT-IF rewrites. Rules for the ladder:
> - Rungs 1–3: change ONE external condition each (place, era,
>   profession, constraint of the situation) — keep characters intact.
> - Rungs 4–6: each rung must give the protagonist a harder WANT
>   (something they can fail at visibly).
> - Rungs 7–8: each rung must add a cost that lands on a specific
>   other person.
> - Rungs 9–10: each rung must make the obvious genre version
>   impossible (state which genre you just banned and why).
> Format: numbered rungs, one line each, ending with: which rung you
> would develop and one sentence of why — do not choose for me; flag
> the choice as a suggestion only.
> ```

Read the rules *as constraints doing Chapter 6's work*: "one external condition," "harder want," "cost lands on a specific person," "ban the obvious genre" — each checkable, each converting vague ideation into structural pressure. The ladder's real product is often **rung 9–10**: banning the obvious version forces the model (and you) off the road you'd both default to, which is where the original rung either dies usefully or reveals it had a second, stranger life.

> TRY IT — Ladder your worst stored idea — the one you're embarrassed still interests you. Ten rungs, ten minutes if you type fast. Most writers discover the embarrassment was the *generic* wrapper around a decent core; the ladder's job is to strip the wrapper. If rungs 7–10 feel impossible ("this seed can't carry costs"), that's the seed telling you its weight class — file it and ladder something with more mass.

### Combination and constraint stacking (PC-10)

**Idea combiners** take two parked premises (or a premise + a formal restriction) and fuse them under a rule that prevents mush:

> PROMPT CARD PC-10 — Idea combiner
>
> ```text
> 
> Role: synthesis engineer; I provide elements; you fuse, you don't
> average.
> Elements: A) [parked premise one-liner]  B) [parked premise
> one-liner]  C) [optional formal constraint: one location / told in
> documents / no direct dialogue / etc.]
> Task: 5 fusion attempts. For each, state: (1) the NEW conflict that
> exists only in the fusion (not A + B side by side); (2) which
> element you had to distort to make room, and how; (3) the first
> scene's goal in one line.
> Anti-rules: no title-generation; no "best of both worlds" language;
> if an element refuses to fuse, say so and give the nearest honest
> version.
> Output: 5 attempts, numbered, then stop.
> ```

The load-bearing line is (1): *the new conflict that exists only in the fusion* — the test distinguishing synthesis from collage. (2) forces honesty about distortion — fusions always damage an element; knowing *which* one bent tells you what the story now privileges. Fusions that pass tend to produce premises with built-in thematic pressure (two worldviews colliding in one plot); fusions that fail teach you your parked ideas were parked for reasons.

**Constraint stacking** is the freest form: gather your personal constraint bank — *places you know cold, jobs you've done or watched up close, one fear, one formal toy, one "not this genre"* — and require each ideation run to spend a minimum: "every premise must include one real place + one non-negotiable cost + one formal restriction from my list below." The bank is a *you*-artifact, accumulated in the margins of your reading (Chapter 14's style sheet doubles as a bank source); the model contributes recombination labor only. Ideation help you can't get anywhere else looks like this — *not* "write me five premises," but "recombine *these* mine under *these* rules."

### The ideation loop, honestly

The full working loop for this chapter's stage: ladder or combine (PC-07/10) → *you* select 2–3 rungs/attempts that produce hand-itch → stress-test survivors (PC-08, below) → logline the winner (PC-09) → **file everything else** in the parked-ideas doc with one-line loglines (the Parked file is Chapter 16's sequel-writing mine and your anti-sunk-cost insurance). Two loop truths before we move to testing: *generate in bulk, select in silence* — don't adjudicate mid-ladder; selection during generation contaminates both (you'll get safer rungs, dumber judgments). And *one seed per session* — ideation sessions that mix five seeds produce five averages; sessions that stay on one seed drill to the strange strata where the good stuff hides.

## Stress-testing premises

Selection without testing is vibes; testing is where the machine's adversarial value (Chapter 1's improvement #2, systematized) earns its keep. Three tests, each a prompt, each answered *in writing by you* before anything gets "developed."

> PROMPT CARD PC-08 — Premise stress-test
>
> ```text
> 
> Role: ruthless development editor with no stake in your feelings;
> you are testing a premise for a multi-month commitment.
> Premise: [four-part premise from Ch 2 — protagonist, goal,
> obstacle, stakes].
> Run these checks, IN THIS ORDER, in this format — for each: FINDING
> (one line), DAMAGE (high/med/low), REPAIR (one concrete change, or
> "none available — flag for parking"):
> 1. STAKES CHECK: is the cost-of-failure concrete, climbable on the
>    Ch 2 ladder, and *worse* than inconvenience at every rung?
> 2. NOVELTY AUDIT: state the three most-predictable versions of this
>    premise; then state what makes MINE not those — if you cannot
>    tell them apart, say "center-of-distribution" loudly.
> 3. WHY-ME / WHY-NOW: what in ME (the author-proxy below) makes this
>    mine to write — and what in the story's present moment makes it
>    urgent? If either is empty, mark the premise.
> 4. CAPACITY CHECK: against my declared constraints (length target,
>    POV exclusivity, single-timeline), name the structural cost this
>    premise will charge.
> 5. KILLER QUESTION: the one objection a skeptical first reader will
>    raise by page 10 — phrase it as the reader's exact words.
> No praise. End with: verdict — DEVELOP / PARK / PARK-AND-STEAL-
> [element] (name the element worth cannibalizing later).
> Author-proxy (why-me fuel): [3–5 lines: your obsessions, the
> specific memories/knowledge you'd spend, what you'd refuse to
> sentimentalize]
> ```

**Why the author-proxy is inside the prompt:** check 3 would otherwise be answered with horoscope-grade flattery ("this premise suits a writer with your evident depth!") — Chapter 5's sycophancy, fully deployed. Give the model *raw material about you* to work against ("I obsess over inheritance disputes; I refuse to sentimentalize small towns; I know tide tables from a former job") and the check becomes answerable with specifics: *the premise spends exactly your listed obsessions and respects your refusal* — a claim you can verify. **Why verdicts include PARK-AND-STEAL:** premises die easier when they leave a bequest; naming the organ to harvest (a character, a setting, one turn) converts killing darlings from funeral into inventory — the parked file's real wealth-building habit.

**Novelty-check honesty.** Check 2 is the one the machine is *worst* at judging for you and *best* at scaffolding: its "three predictable versions" are genuinely its center-of-mass samples (reliable — they're literally what the distribution suggests), and the differentiation sentence ("MINE is not those because…") must be *yours to assert* — if you can't complete it without the machine's help, you are standing in the center, however flattering the view. Expect this to happen; it's the test working. Park, ladder again, return.

**Why-me, why-now — the two halves aren't equal.** Why-now (urgency) is nice-to-have; why-me (authority + obsession + refusal) is load-bearing — stories survive outdated urgency; they rarely survive an author with nothing at stake. Taste note: "why-me" answers that are only *competence* ("I know that world") produce competent books; answers containing *compulsion* ("I can't let the inheritance question go") produce the ones people finish at 2 a.m. Mine for the compulsion line; competence is the floor, not the fuel.

### Killing darlings early

The stage's hardest discipline, and the one AI assistance makes *cheaper* — which is precisely why to use it now. Every parked premise gets: one-line logline, verdict date, and the bequest (if any) noted; the file never gets deleted from, only grown (Chapter 16 mines it). The economics that justify ruthlessness: a premise killed today cost a logline; the same premise discovered broken at outline cost a week; at draft, a month; at "why isn't this working," a year of sunk-cost bargaining. Your decisions file gets one row per kill — *premise X parked: predictable-version problem; stole the notary for Y* — because future-you's favorite proof of taste will be the graveyard's size. Writers who only ever *add* are not generous; they're afraid of the ledger.

## Loglines that work

The survivor gets compressed. A **logline** is one sentence (professionally, one to three — we'll practice the one-sentence discipline first) that carries: protagonist + want + obstacle/antagonism + stakes-flavored cost, in language whose *word choice* signals the tone. Anatomy, using a famous-shape example rebuilt generically: *[A specific protagonist] must [goal] or else [cost], despite [obstacle] — all in [world/tone signal]*. The tonal-signal clause is the underrated half: "…or the town's last well goes dry" says rural fable; "…before the merger closes" says office thriller; the logline *recruits* the right expectations before page one.

> PROMPT CARD PC-09 — Logline refiner
>
> ```text
> 
> Role: logline editor; compression specialist; no new plot elements.
> Input premise: [four-part premise]. Input draft logline (mine):
> [your sentence].
> Task: (1) DIAGNOSE my draft against the anatomy — name which slot
> is missing, vague, or overloaded (protagonist / goal / obstacle /
> cost / tonal signal). (2) Offer 6 REFINEMENTS of MY draft only —
> each must keep my plot's events and characters; vary which slot
> gets emphasis. (3) For each, note what it HIDES (every logline
> lies by omission — name the omission).
> Anti-rules: no words like "must confront"; no "battling
> [abstraction]"; if my premise can't support a concrete cost, say
> so instead of decorating it.
> Output: diagnosis, then 6 sentences with their hidden cards.
> ```

Part (3) — *name the omission* — turns refinement into training: every logline necessarily hides something (the subplots, the POV trick, the false lead), and knowing *what yours hides* is how you keep the document honest when agents or friends ask "what's it about?" — and how you notice when your logline's hidden thing turns out to be the *actual* story (surprise: the tide journal was the book; logline gets revised at Chapter 12's structure pass, not carved in stone now).

**Your draft first, always.** PC-09 refines *your* sentence; arriving with the machine's sentence as input trains you into a reviewer of its compression rather than a compressor — the reps are the point (same reason Chapter 1's improvement #3 was hand-written). Aim: twenty minutes of your own attempts before the card runs; six refinements adjudicated down to one line you'd say out loud in an elevator without checking your notes.

### Anatomy of an ideation session (the ninety-minute lab)

Sessions that produce selections follow a fixed arc; running it deliberately separates *lab time* (structured, clocked, mechanical) from *itch time* (the unstructured noticing that feeds the bank). The lab, in order:

1. **Frame (5 min).** One seed, one session (loop truth #2). State the session's output contract aloud in your notes: "By 90 minutes: ≥10 ladder rungs filed, 2–3 shortlisted, 1 stress-tested if a shortlist survives." Output contracts prevent the common drift where ideation *feels* productive and ends without a verdict.
2. **Bank check (5 min).** Open the constraint bank; star today's ingredients — a place, a job, a fear, one formal toy, one genre refusal. If the bank's starved (first sessions), seed it *now* from memory: five places you know cold, three jobs you've watched or lived, one fear you'd spend. The bank is the session's *you*-reservoir; the ladder without it is Chapter 5's distribution tour.
3. **Generation sprints (30–40 min).** PC-07 (or PC-10) runs, one seed per sprint, *selection silent* (loop truth #1). Between runs: file non-selected output immediately (parked file grows *during* the session so re-reading old rungs can't anchor the next sprint). Two sprints on one seed max — the third sprint's rungs are almost always recolored rungs 1 and 6; notice and switch seeds rather than push.
4. **Cool-down + shortlist (10 min).** Reread cold; circle two or three without justifying yet — the circle is itch, not argument. Justifications come *after* the circle, written as one line each ("rung 6: the maintained-house-as-argument detail is the story's engine").
5. **Stress-test leg (25–30 min).** PC-08 on each shortlisted candidate, separate fresh sessions, findings adjudicated row by row — this is where ninety minutes gets spent properly: testing is the lab's actual product. Verdicts filed (DEVELOP / PARK / PARK-AND-STEAL).
6. **Close (5 min).** Session-log block (Mode: ideation lab; Adjudications: N filed), parked file's new entries skimmed once, next lab's seed question written if today's wells ran dry.

Two calibration notes. Ninety minutes is a ceiling, not a quota — a session that reaches a DEVELOP verdict at fifty minutes *ends* (the temptation to keep laddering past a verdict is how strong premises get talked out of themselves by fatigue); a session that hits ninety with no circle should log "seed dry," bank three new seed questions for next time, and end without manufactured enthusiasm — *dry sessions are data about seeds, not about you*. And lab cadence: weekly for an active project (the parked file compounds fastest at weekly rhythm); monthly otherwise — more frequent than monthly and ideation starts cannibalizing development time (a premise chosen is a premise that should be outlining, Chapter 9's job), less frequent and the bank goes stale.

### When the well is dry: seeds that refuse to ladder

First sessions and winter sessions meet the same failure: rungs that restate the seed in costume ("woman sells house" → "woman considers selling shop" — a list of synonyms pretending to be a descent). Three wrenches, in order of force:

- **Wrench 1 — Change the axis, not the content.** If rungs keep rotating the same situation, ladder *in a different dimension*: era (the same conflict in 1987), scale (the house is one room of a condemned block), ontology (the "brother" is the executor who never met her) — sometimes the seed's *subject* is fine and its *coordinates* are boring. One axis per rerun; note which axis unlocked descent in the session log (that's bank metadata about *you*: your axis preferences are part of your ideation signature).
- **Wrench 2 — Import a stranger.** PC-10 with an element from an unrelated parked premise or a bank item you haven't spent ("fuse with the formal toy: told in receipts"). Fusions break dead wells because the *conflict* must be new before the components settle — the collision does the laddering for you.
- **Wrench 3 — Invert the want.** The universal solvent: keep the situation, flip who wants what ("*Theo* wants to sell; Mara wants to keep" — five seconds of inversion, and watch rung 6's pressure reappear mirrored). Most dry wells are wells where the *first* want you imagined got silently elected permanent; inversion re-opens the ballot.

What does *not* fix a dry well: more runs of the same prompt (you're sampling a distribution Chapter 5 already told you about), lowering constraints (removing friction *increases* center-drift — the opposite of what's wrong), or "one more sprint" past fatigue (rung quality collapses and files junk you'll re-read as anchoring junk). If all three wrenches fail twice on a seed: file it as `dry-2026-09` in the parked file and pick a seed from the starred bank instead — persistence at ideation is a different virtue than persistence at drafting, and confusing them burns lab time that testing would have spent better.

### The parked file as a long game

`parked-ideas.md` deserves the chapter's closing attention because it is the stage's only *compounding* artifact — everything else (selected premise, logline) is a point; the file is a curve. Three long-game habits:

**Ledger format:** `- [date] · one-line logline · verdict · bequest? · itches? (yes/no)`. The `itches` flag is the file's secret engine: monthly top-to-bottom reads (Ch 7's habit) re-rate itches — an idea that still itches after cooling has *survived its own novelty decay*, which is the cheapest quality signal available to a solo writer. Two or three itches on file at any time = your next projects pre-vetted; the itch that survives a *year* is the novel you owe yourself.

**Bequests make kills relational.** Changelog-style linking ("premise A parked; its notary became protagonist of premise C") turns the file into a graph where dead ideas quietly vote on living ones — and gives PC-10 its best raw material (fusions of *cooled* elements beat fusions of fresh ones: fresh ideas resist distortion because they're still defended by recency; cooled ideas bend). When Chapter 16's 90-day plan asks what to write *next*, the graph answers with evidence instead of vibes.

**What the file is *not*:** a second manuscript (one line per entry — the moment entries grow paragraphs, you've started developing parked premises and are avoiding the selected one), a place for *other people's* ideas (borrowed premises carry obligations you haven't examined — log the source if you must, prefer your bank), or a graveyard you visit *instead* of testing (itches must enter labs; an un-tested itch at month eleven is procrastination wearing curiosity's clothes). The success metric stands: entries added, verdicts filed, itches re-rated — growth only, judgment always, development reserved for exactly one survivor at a time.

### Constraint stacking with a personal bank — a transcript

To make the bank concrete before you build yours: here is one ideation run as it actually looks when the "you" material is loaded. Bank excerpt (author's, kept in `prompts/bank.md`):

```text
PLACES (cold knowledge): Atlantic fishing towns (winter); a municipal
  tide-gauge shed; the ferry maintenance depot my father managed.
JOBS WATCHED UP CLOSE: hydrographic survey crews; small-claims
  clerks; wedding planners (one season, as a temp).
FEARS TO SPEND: administrative permanent mistakes (a filing that
  can't be undone); being recognized in a place I've outgrown.
FORMAL TOYS: told in receipts / one location / no direct dialogue
  for 3 pages / countdown without clocks.
REFUSALS: haunted house; evil corporation; prodigy child; any
  villain whose motive is only money.
```

**Run (PC-07 seeded with constraint statement rather than plot seed — the bank *is* the seed):**

> Task: from the bank above, produce 5 premise seeds. Rules: each seed spends ≥1 place + ≥1 fear + ≥1 formal toy; no seed may use a refusal item; every seed's protagonist must *work* in the place (not just visit). Format: four-part premises, ≤60 words, then: which bank items you spent (list).

**Output (abridged, two of five):**

> 1. A small-clerks-office clerk (place: tide-gauge shed as de facto office) discovers a decade of misfiled storm-surge claims that all trace to her own early handwriting; she has one filing cycle before the archive locks (fear: permanent administrative mistake) — told in receipts, each one a claim she must decide whether to re-file honestly and cost herself the job. [spends: gauge shed, filing fear, receipts toy, clerk job] [...]
> 3. [...] the ferry-depot manager's daughter returns for the sale of the depot's last tool crib, recognizes her father's inventory codes in a stranger's ledger (place: depot; fear: recognition-in-place), no direct dialogue for 3 pages — every family negotiation conducted through parts-order slips. [...]

*Commentary:* seeds 1 and 3 are already four-part (the bank's specificity did the heavy lifting — the model's job was *assembly under rules*, which is work it does reliably when the parts list is yours). Note what's absent: no seed needed a "twist," because constraint-collision *is* the novelty engine — receipts-as-family-communication existed the moment "formal toy" met "depot," and the machine's contribution was noticing the collision faster than a blank page would. Seed 1's fictionality check is the author's to run (small-claims clerks: real jobs, invented office — verify any detail that reaches the page per Ch 5's type-split). Selection went to seed 1 with the survey-crew job swapped in (the bank's *first* place item was stronger) — that swap, made by hand in thirty seconds, is why the bank stays under your authorship even when generation is delegated.

### The why-me file: compulsion over competence

Check 3's author-proxy deserves a permanent home: `research/why-me.md`, a running document — not per-premise, per *writer*. Three sections, maintained quarterly: **Obsessions** (the questions you keep re-entering — inheritance and escape; whether maintenance is love; institutions that outlive their purpose — write yours plainly, without justifying them); **Authority** (what you've *done or watched at close range* — jobs, families, subcultures, griefs — competence inventory, no resume padding); **Refusals** (what you won't sentimentalize, won't fake, won't write for the market — the negative space of your voice: "no villains whose only motive is money," "no small towns as therapy," etc.). When PC-08's proxy line runs, you paste three bullets from this file, not an improvised paragraph — improvised proxies drift toward flattering self-summary under session pressure; filed obsessions survive it.

The file's second job is *surprise-keeping*: quarterly re-reads show you which obsessions are decaying (fine — cross them out; tastes live) and which are *urgent in a way you can't yet place* (the keepers). Two practical guards: it is **research-folder material, not bible material** — it informs premise selection and why-me answers, never becomes story canon about *characters*; and it stays **private** — paste *excerpts* into prompts (the proxy field's three bullets), never the file itself (Chapter 5's paste-discipline: the whole document is more personal than any single premise needs). Writers who maintain one find Chapters 8–16 repeatedly handing it back to them: premise tests, logline tone checks, Chapter 16's "what's next after this book" — all answer faster when *why-me* was already written down somewhere other than the mood of a Tuesday.

### Five questions the stage always raises

Collected from a decade of workshop versions of this chapter — answered in the book's voice, briefly, because premature answers are how premises die:

**1. "My seed is tiny — too small for a premise?"** Size at the seed stage is *uninformative*; what matters is *pressure-capacity* (can the four parts be forced without breaking your constraints?). A tiny seed — a woman who won't use elevators — ladders into a premise the moment a *want with a deadline* attaches (she must reach the 14th floor to sign something before the building's sale closes — the phobia now *costs* something on a clock). The ladder *manufactures* size from pressure; the banks' constraint material supplies the specifics. What tiny seeds genuinely *can't* survive: capacity checks for *novel-scale* ambitions with no relational cast to staff them — then the honest verdict is "long short story" or "park with a size-note," not a bloated premise apologetically inflated into a form it won't fill. Scale is chosen at the skeleton stage (Ch 2's length-honesty) — here, seeds get *pressure*, and pressure decides.

**2. "How is this different from just… having ideas?"** It isn't, at the bottom — the techniques are *organization for an ordinary faculty*, not a substitute for one. The difference the stage adds is **judgment infrastructure**: unconstrained ideation produces and *stops*; the lab produces, *selects against written criteria, tests adversarially, verdicts, and files* — the verdict-and-filing part is what "just having ideas" never does, and it's where the months get protected. An ideas-person without the lab forgets; the lab's parked file, why-me file, and decisions rows are the *institutional memory of taste* — which is exactly why Chapter 16's "what next" questions get answered from documents instead of mood.

**3. "The stress-test keeps saying PARK — am I broken?"** No: PARK is *the test working* — most premises should park (your future shelf is *supposed* to outnumber your active project; one survivor at a time is the design from the chapter's first paragraph). Distinguish two failure modes though: **healthy parking** (specific findings: predictable-version problem, capacity break — the verdict *teaches*; ladder-lessons accumulate into better seeds) versus **vacuous parking** ("just not feeling it" with no PC-08 rows attached — the test wasn't run, a mood signed its name; run the test *before* believing the mood — sometimes the findings actually *clear* a premise the mood had buried, and the verdict flips to DEVELOP-with-repairs in ways vibes never would). If healthy parking repeats five times *on structurally different premises*, the smell is upstream: your *author-proxy* may be too thin for the checker to defend anything (check 3's mark) — go maintain the why-me file before the next lab, because a premise-stage curse is usually a *fuel*-stage quiet.

**4. "Should the logline come before the stress-test?"** Order matters and the chapter's sequence is load-bearing: premise → stress-test → logline, because the *logline's compression inherits the test's repairs* (name the posting *first*, then compress — compress first and you'll polish a sentence encoding the premise's *pre-repair* defects, then feel oddly loyal to it afterward: **premature loglines become sunk-cost poetry**, defended for aesthetic reasons long after their underlying premises were flagged for exactly what the pretty sentence hides). Exception that proves it: when a premise's *natural form* arrives as a phrase ("the tide she never learned to read" class of image) — capture it in the parked file or the skeleton's field-10 margin as *raw material*, but the *working* logline (the elevator sentence you steer by) still gets built post-verdict from the repaired four-part. Capture fragments freely; *steer* only by post-test sentences.

**5. "Can I develop two premises at once?"** For *full labs* (stress-tests, skeletons, beta-bound projects): no — one DEVELOP verdict at a time is the whole selection architecture (two active labs *will* contaminate each other's constraint banks and, worse, *split your adjudication attention* until both get vibes instead of verdicts). For *light ideation* (bank-maintenance, laddering seeds that stay untested, parked-file mining): yes, indefinitely — the parked file *runs on parallel curiosity by design*. The distinguishing line: **verdicts are serial; wandering is parallel.** The moment a second premise asks for a *stress-test appointment*, it has *also* asked to park its rival — schedule the test for *next month* (cooled premises test better anyway — recency-fandom distorts check 3's why-me answers), and meanwhile keep only one skeleton at a time in `manuscript/`'s hands. Chapter 16's 90-day plan will re-derive this rule from the *calendar's* side: projects consume *finishing-capacity*, and finishing-capacity, for every writer not yet finished once, is the scarcest resource the book exists to multiply.

### The parking ritual (killing darlings, step by step)

The stage's coldest procedure, specified so it stays a *habit* rather than a mood-dependent mercy: **(1)** verdict recorded in the decisions file with its top finding quoted (*why* parked — in the test's own words, not yours — future rereads need the *checker's* evidence, not your summary of it); **(2)** the one-liner logline written *from the four-part as-is* (not as-is-plus-hope: the parked line describes *what was tested*, so later PC-10 fusions don't inherit unstressed material); **(3)** bequest named if the test's verdict included PARK-AND-STEAL-*X* (the stolen element logged *in the receiving file's header* — "notary came from parked premise #7" — chains of custody for ideas, the graph Ch 16 will mine); **(4)** itch-flag set to *no* (monthly rereads re-rate it consciously — an auto-yes flags would just be today's mood pre-writing future ones); **(5)** folder discipline: `parked-ideas.md` grows-only, entries never edited *after* the fact (if a parked premise gets *re-tested* later, the new verdict is a *new row with a new date* — the file is append-only like the claim register, because a parking record edited in retrospect is a parking record that can't be trusted to have existed); **(6)** the *ritual's* closing act, psychologically load-bearing: **delete the draft fragments** (any half-opened scenes, note-dump files, or chat-transcripts developing the parked premise move to `cuts/` with the date and one line — parked premises keep their *ledger rows* and their *bequests*, not their *manuscript debris*): fragments exert a gravity that loglines don't, and half-written openings are precisely how "parked" quietly becomes "two projects at once with one starving" — the cut file's address makes the murder *reversible if truly needed* (and the append-only row means the *idea* was never at risk anyway — you're filing the body, not destroying it). Run the six steps in one twenty-minute sitting per verdict — the ceremony matters *more* than the duration: writers who ritualize parking park *more*, park *better*, and carry visibly smaller grave-shoulders into the next lab — while writers who "get around to it" accumulate a draft-folder of unkillable darlings that will, with certainty, arrive at Chapter 16's planning stage demanding to be finished first. Serial verdicts, parallel wandering, twenty minutes per murder, append-only mercy: the stage's whole emotional economy, stated once.

### Logline variants: the family of forms

The single logline has relatives — writers get asked for the wrong shape all the time and lose minutes improvising under pressure; here's the family, each defined by *what it hides less of*, all derived from *your* PC-09-refined sentence (the master version never leaves the folder):

**The elevator (25–35 words, one breath):** protagonist + goal + obstacle + cost, tonal signal implied — the Ch 8 anatomy at speaking speed; use: parties, casual introductions, the *first* answer to "what's it about?" (its test: say it aloud without tripping — written-smooth and spoken-smooth are different crafts; if the breath fails, a clause is carrying two slots, split it). **The query logline (~40–50 words):** elevator + *stake-amplifier* (the stakes sentence's existential rung, compressed) + *comps-adjacent framing* where genre-standard allows ("*X* meets *Y*" patterns — used sparingly, honestly, from books you've actually read — the Ch 16 pre-flight's packet citizen); use: query letter's opening, cover emails, submission forms asking "describe in one line" with a word budget that actually means *two-and-a-half lines*. **The back-cover (60–90 words, *present tense, hook-final*):** NOT a logline stretched — a different species: *setup beat → inciting disruption → ticking condition → final-line question*, characters named as the cover's tradition demands (Mara-first for literary, situation-first for thriller — genre's cover-conventions are real and Ch 16's category-research tells you *which* yours expects); the discipline carrying over: **every sentence earns its clause** (back-covers die of adjectives, not plot — the style sheet's banned-list applies *verbatim* to your own blurb, the self-application test being its own reward: writers whose back-covers pass PC-26's four-minute rung *understand* the de-AI lesson as readers now, not just writers); use: production-side, Ch 16's metadata line's parent. **The synopsis first line (~20 words):** goal + stakes + *the story's engine* (what kind of trouble, structurally — "must decide whether to sell her dead father's house before the coast takes it" — engine = inheritance-versus-place on a clock); use: synopsis headers, fellowship/grant forms, the *first sentence of every professional packet* (readers triaging slush read line one *before* line forty — this variant's stakes-per-word is the packet's highest-leverage twenty). **The internal compass (~15 words, *no character names*):** the *shape* only — "inheritance-versus-place on a storm-clock, ending with a signature not a rescue"; use: *private* — outline headers, beat-list margins, the done-criteria document's header — its no-names rule the point: compass-lines survive *character renames and structural surgery* (the name-free version stays *true* when the named version's gone stale — the instrument's resilience trick: **what you check daily should be robust to what you change weekly**). Maintenance rule for the whole family: **regenerate variants from the master *after every mint that changes the stakes-sentence*** (the changelog line's small print — variants are *derived artifacts*, stale variants being how back-covers end up describing *draft-07's* story while draft-11 ships: derived-artifact staleness is a *sync* problem, and sync problems this book solves with a line-item, not a virtue: "variants regenerated: [date]" — one row, the family kept honest at cadence).

## Worked example: Example A's premise development, start to finish

Full loop on the running example — abridged transcript, annotated as ever. **Input from the author (hand-written first):** seed = "woman returns to sell family house in her fishing town"; constraint bank = *places: Atlantic fishing towns I've known; costs: inheritance fights; formal: one week, storm-bound; refuse: haunted-house version*.

**Leg 1 — ladder (PC-07, abridged):**

> [...] Rung 4: she wants the sale closed before her survey posting starts — visible, checkable, ticking. Rung 6: harder — her brother doesn't just refuse; he's been *maintaining* the house as their mother's project, so every repair is an argument. Rung 8: the cost lands — their mother's friend the neighbor carries messages neither will deliver directly. Rung 9 (banned: "gothic inheritance horror"): no ghosts — the only haunting is administrative. Rung 10 (banned: "reconciliation comedy"): nobody learns to love the town; [...]

*Commentary:* Rungs 4 and 6 are where the seed stopped being a situation (Chapter 2's idea/premise line now visible machine-side: a *checkable want with a deadline* appeared). Rung 8's neighbor (Dona Aurelia) was stolen *immediately* into the bible — the ladder's cost-landing rule manufactured a load-bearing minor character in one line. Rungs 9–10's bans are the author's refusals made *executable* — the haunted-house version never had a chance to be proposed, which is the entire difference from unconstrained ideation.

**Author selection (decisions file):** *"Develop rung 4+6 fusion. Rejected rung 7's custody-law subplot — off-scale for a short story. Stole Aurelia."*

**Leg 2 — four-part premise, hand-assembled (author, no machine):**

> Mara Solano, 36, returns to Porto Alvaro to sell her dead mother's cliff house in two weeks before her survey posting begins; her brother Theo refuses to sell and won't say why; she must force or forgo the sale within a storm-bound week — costing her either the sister she never was, or the life she built instead.

**Leg 3 — stress-test (PC-08, abridged with real findings):**

> 1. STAKES CHECK: personal rung concrete (posting vs. house); relational rung identified; existential rung thin — "life she built instead" risks abstraction. DAMAGE: med. REPAIR: make the posting *nameable* (the specific survey ship/route) so the loss has a proper noun. [...]
> 2. NOVELTY AUDIT: three predictable versions: (a) she softens, keeps house, small-town romance; (b) villain-buyer twist; (c) magical-realist ending where house chooses. MY DIFFERENTIATION: the buyer is human-scale, no villain; ending is administrative (keys, schedules) — *if drafted as written*. DAMAGE: low *under this draft* — but flag: version (a) is the center's gravity; first beta round should hunt for accidental romance beats. [...]
> 3. WHY-ME: author-proxy matches: inheritance disputes obsession ✓, refuse-sentimentality rule respected by rung 10 ✓. WHY-NOW: marked — urgency is private (estate deadlines), not cultural. Acceptable for literary short form; do not oversell urgency later.
> 4. CAPACITY: one-POV + one-week + storm = short-story-compatible; the tide-journal subplot is the expansion risk — cap entries quoted at two (rule added to bible §5). [...]
> 5. KILLER QUESTION (reader's words by p.10): *"Hasn't this exact sibling-fight-in-a-house story been told?"* — must be answered by specificity (the journal, the professional-symbolism) within first three pages. [...]
> Verdict: DEVELOP — with repairs: name the posting; two-journal-entry cap; romance-beat hunt scheduled for Ch 13 betas.

*Commentary (the whole method in five lines):* Finding 1's repair (a proper noun for the loss) is Chapter 2's stakes-ladder discipline running machine-side with your documents — accepted whole. Finding 2's differentiation was *asserted by the constraint bank* (the bans) and then *audited* against the distribution — note the check didn't say "this is novel," it said "not these three," which is all novelty claims can honestly be. Finding 5's reader-quote is the adversarial frame defeating sycophancy: the card *required* a hostile voice, so one arrived — the same prompt without the frame would have produced "a compelling exploration of grief and place." The verdict's three repairs all entered the bible and the skeleton *before a single outline beat existed* — stress-testing pays its bills in pre-emption.

**Leg 4 — logline (author's third attempt after PC-09's six, adjudicated):**

> A hydrographic surveyor who charts the sea for a living has one storm-bound week to sell the house her brother refuses to leave — and discovers that staying is the one tide she never learned to read.

*Commentary:* drafts 1–2 failed anatomy (draft 1 hid the obstacle; draft 2's cost was generic) — PC-09's diagnosis naming the missing slots produced the revisions; the author kept the final clause's image because *the profession-symbolism rhyme* (Chapter 2's skeleton annotation) is now doing double duty as tonal signal: literary, water-coded, non-genre. The "hidden card" noted: the mother's journal and the neighbor don't appear — correct omissions for this length; the logline's job is the elevator, not the index.

Stage complete: **premise selected, stress-tested, logged, parked-file grown, bible seeded** — the seed every remaining workflow chapter will plant itself in. Your versions of all five legs are this chapter's Stretch; the form is identical even when every word differs.

## Exercises

- **Try (15 min):** What-if ladder × 10 for one seed you actually care about (PC-07's rules enforced — especially the rung 7–8 cost-landing and rung 9–10 genre bans). No selection during generation; circle rungs *after*. If rung 9–10 feel forced, rerun with harder bans (ban the genre your seed *is*, not the adjacent one).

- **Stretch (1–2 h):** Stress-test your top three premises (write each as a four-part premise first — the card won't accept fog), run PC-08 on each *separately in fresh sessions*, adjudicate all three verdicts in writing (accept/override each finding with one-line reasons — overrides are taste reps), then pick ONE and write its logline through at least three of your own drafts before PC-09 touches it. Total honest cost: about ninety minutes; output: a survivor with a scar or two, which is exactly the kind of premise that survives outlining.

- **Project (ongoing):** Maintain `parked-ideas.md` — one-line loglines, verdict dates, bequests — as a *growth-only* file (this project's success metric is entries added, never removed). Habit: every ideation session ends by filing non-selected rungs/attempts there with their one-liners; monthly, read it top to bottom once — combinations (PC-10) form best between ideas that have *cooled*, and cooled ideas surprise you with what still itches.

## Pitfalls

> PITFALL — **Agreeable brainstorming.** *Symptom:* the assistant loves every direction ("excellent premise!"), suggests improvements that are really compliments, and the session ends with mood-boost but no decisions; your decisions file gains zero rows while the chat gains forty exchanges; alternative: the opposite overcorrection — you ask only "what's wrong with this?" and get a model that (without adversarial framing) softens the defects into "areas for development." *Why it happens:* Chapter 5's sycophancy in both configurations: unframed requests for opinions drift toward approval; unframed requests for critique drift toward *gentle* critique; and ideation sessions are the easiest place to forget that the machine's enthusiasm is *predicted text*, not assessment — it has read ten million "excellent premise" openers. *Fix:* adversarial frames as default: PC-08's ordered checks *require* findings with damage ratings; explicit bans ("no praise"; "phrase objections as the reader's exact words"); the author-proxy gives the model material to *check against* instead of you to flatter; and the standing loop rule — sessions end with verdicts (DEVELOP / PARK / PARK-AND-STEAL) written to the file, or the session didn't happen. *Prevention:* the adjudication-before-next-leg discipline — selection happens in silence, verdicts in writing, one seed per session (the three loop truths) — plus the weekly adjudication audit (Ch 7) which flags "forty exchanges, zero rows" as the sycophancy signature it is.

Related failure for Appendix C: **F-05, agreeable brainstorming with zero decisions** — this chapter's home entry; the fix stack (adversarial prompts, damage ratings, forced verdicts, one-seed sessions) is reused by Ch 9's outline critic and Ch 13's feedback triage.

## Checklist

> CHECKLIST — You're ready to move on when:
> - [ ] One premise selected — written as all four parts (protagonist, goal, obstacle, stakes) in your project's skeleton slot.
> - [ ] It survived the stress-test **in writing**: PC-08 findings each adjudicated (accepted/repaired/overridden with reasons), verdict filed.
> - [ ] Your hand-drafted logline exists (≥3 personal attempts before PC-09), with its hidden-omission noted.
> - [ ] `parked-ideas.md` holds every rejected rung/attempt/premise with one-line loglines and bequests.
> - [ ] Bible sections 1, 5, and any new names from this stage are synced (posting named; journal-entry cap entered; Aurelia cast).

## Key takeaways

- **Unconstrained ideation votes for the center; constrained ideation is authored.** Ladders (PC-07), fusions (PC-10), and a personal constraint bank turn "give me ideas" into narrowing work the model can labor at and only you can judge.
- **Stress-testing is cheap insurance with ordered checks:** stakes, novelty (against the three predictable versions), why-me/why-now (armed with your author-proxy so flattery has nothing to hold), capacity, and the killer question — each finding adjudicated by you, in writing, with damage ratings.
- **Kill at premise stage, not draft stage** — and make killing *constructive*: every park gets a logline, a date, and a named bequest; your graveyard's size is a taste metric, not a body count.
- **Loglines compress your own sentence, never the machine's:** anatomy diagnosis, six refinements, and *name what each hides* — you keep the compression reps and the honesty card.
- **The stage's outputs are the workflow's seed:** selected premise + tested logline + grown parked-file + bible syncs. Chapter 9 expands exactly this premise — nothing else — into an outline; if it's not selected yet, Chapter 9 has nothing to serve.


# Chapter 09 — Outlining & Structure with AI

## Opening hook

The outline looked *fine*. Forty-one beats, arrows between them, a color for each act — you remember the afternoon you made it, the satisfaction of a machine assembled. Then you read it aloud in order, the way you'd read a stranger's, and somewhere around beat 19 the air went out: three acts of *things happening* between your two good scenes, nothing anyone wants spilling over into anything else, no reason the middle shouldn't be skipped. The Act II hole — not a missing event (there are events; look at all the events) but a missing *pressure*: no rung movement, no map change, just a corridor your protagonist walks while the outline congratulates itself on arrows.

Here's the part that stings: the outline had *felt* fine for six weeks. Feeling is a terrible structure instrument — you cannot feel a pacing hole in a document you wrote in the dopamine of invention; you can only *test* for it. What found it in the end took twenty minutes: a structured critique pass where the machine was ordered to hunt exactly this failure — "where does pressure flatline for more than two beats? where does the protagonist stop *wanting on-page*?" — and then *you* adjudicating its findings against the skeleton's stakes sentence, keeping three, rejecting two, rebuilding beats 19–26 by hand from the kept ones.

This chapter installs that testing apparatus. Outlining with AI means three things, and only three: **granularity control** (acts → chapters → beats, at the resolution your story actually needs), **the machine as structure critic** (PC-11 asks for problems, never praise — you adjudicate everything in writing), and **pacing and subplot instruments** (PC-12, PC-13) that catch what eyes miss. Plus the escape hatch the chapter must keep honest: *when to abandon the outline*, because an outline serves the draft, not the other way around (the rule that also, quietly, keeps discovery writers in this book at all). By the end, Example A will have gone through the full v1 → critique → v3 loop in front of you, annotated, and your own beat outline will exist — frozen, with all later changes routed through the changelog.

## Learning objectives

By the end of this chapter, you will be able to:

- [ ] Expand a skeleton into a beat-level outline (acts → chapters → beats) at the right resolution.
- [ ] Use AI as a structure critic without surrendering decisions (PC-11, adjudicated).
- [ ] Check pacing and weave subplots deliberately (PC-12, PC-13).
- [ ] Revise an outline through versions (v1 → v3) and know when to abandon it instead.

## Outline granularity

**Granularity** is the outline's zoom level: too coarse and it can't guide scenes; too fine and it freezes accidents that wanted to be better. The three-level ladder, with beginner-sized guidance for each:

**Level 1 — Acts/skeleton-derived movements (3–6 entries).** You already own this: Chapter 2's skeleton *is* a level-1 outline (disturbance, point of no return, midpoint/crisis, climax preview, ending preview — five beats with causes). Never re-outline at this level from scratch; expand *from* the skeleton, which is why it lives in the bible. Rule of thumb: if a skeleton field is soft (Chapter 2's diagnosis guide), no amount of beat-level detail below it will compensate — fix upward first.

**Level 2 — Chapters/sequence beats (one line each, 12–40 for a novel; 5–12 for a long short story).** The workhorse level for Part III. Each entry: *what changes* in one line, stated as change, not activity — "Mara arrives; keys + notary letter; wants out by dark" (three changes compressed) beats "Mara's arrival scene" (an activity label with no opinion about pressure). The line-test for a level-2 beat: **does it contain a want, a cost, or a map-change?** If it contains none, it's scenery filed as structure — demote it to a scene detail inside a neighboring beat or cut it. Chapter granularity should also answer *ordering* questions: every beat states enough of its neighbors' relationship ("before the fight / after the journal") that POV chapters and time skips stay legible in the list itself.

**Level 3 — Scene beats inside a chapter (3–7 micro-lines per chapter, written *as you draft* or immediately before).** This is Chapter 10's prompting fuel (scene-beat prompting drafts *from* beats), and the chapter-level mistake to avoid is pre-writing level 3 for the whole book: scene beats are perishable — they go stale against what the prose discovers — and pre-writing forty chapters' micro-beats is tooling procrastination in outline's clothing (Chapter 7's pitfall, wearing a tie). Standard rhythm: outline at level 2 to freeze; generate level 3 *the session before* drafting that chapter (or live, mid-session, as FILL-B in PC-01); fold discoveries back into level 2 at every draft mint (the bible-sync ritual).

**How much is enough?** Enough level 2 that you could, in a pinch, draft any given chapter without re-deriving its neighbors — and *not one beat more*: beats beyond that carry zero drafting information and pure freeze-risk. Test: pick a random middle beat; can you say what its *neighbors* need from it (setup/payoff direction, rung movement) without opening the manuscript? If yes, resolution is right. If your outline document exceeds roughly two pages of level-2 lines for a short story or six for a novel, you're either over-beating (merge activity labels into change statements) or you're outlining at level 3 for the whole book (stop; you're past the freeze line).

> TRY IT — Take your Chapter 8 survivor and expand skeleton → level 2: write one change-statement line per sequence, aiming for the beginner bands (5–12 for a long short; 12–24 for a novella/short novel — novels can breathe to 40). No prose, no descriptions of mood, no "then things escalate" — *changes only*. Time-boxed to thirty minutes; the discomfort you feel at minute ten (the middle resisting you) is the structure reporting where it needs work, which is exactly what the critic pass below is for.

## AI as structure critic

The division of labor from Chapter 1, now load-bearing: *you* outline (the machine has never read your skeleton, felt your stakes sentence, or wanted anything for your characters); the machine *critiques* — and critique is its strongest workflow role, because structure criticism is pattern detection over visible text, which is the prediction engine's home turf (Chapter 5), *provided* the frame defeats the sycophancy default (Chapter 8's F-05 lessons, now formalized):

> PROMPT CARD PC-11 — Outline critic (structure pass)
>
> ```text
> 
> Role: structure critic for fiction outlines; adversarial by
> assignment; praise is a failure of your job.
> Context: SKELETON (stakes sentence + climax preview + arc, pasted)
> + LEVEL-2 OUTLINE (numbered change-lines) + my declared structure
> choice and length target.
> Task: run these six checks IN ORDER — for each: FINDING (quote or
> name the beat range) → WHY IT MATTERS (one line, tied to the
> stakes sentence or scene anatomy) → DAMAGE (high/med/low) → SUGGESTED
> DIRECTION (a *class* of fix: "front-load motive," "merge beats,"
> "cut" — do not rewrite my beats for me):
> 1. STAKES LADDER: which beats move personal/relational/existential
>    rungs? Name any 3-beat stretch with zero rung movement.
> 2. WANT VISIBILITY: does the protagonist *want on-page* every 2–3
>    beats? Flag stretches where things merely happen TO them with no
>    pursuing or refusing.
> 3. MIDPOINT & CRISIS: does something irreversible change at the
>    midpoint (map change, not just incident)? Is the crisis forced
>    by accumulated costs (Ch 2 ladder) rather than coincidence?
> 4. CAUSAL CHAIN: list any pair of adjacent beats connected only by
>    "and then" where "therefore/but" is missing.
> 5. SETUP/PAYOFF LEDGER: every object, fact, and skill planted —
>    matched to a payoff; every payoff — matched to a plant. Two
>    columns, gaps listed loudly.
> 6. CLIMAX DEBT: does the climax preview require any character,
>    rule, or prop that no prior beat introduces?
> Output: six sections, numbered findings, damage-rated; end with
> the THREE highest-damage findings only. No summary paragraph. No
> praise. No rewritten outline.
> ```

Every design line in that card has a failure it prevents: *pasted skeleton* grounds the critique in *your* aims (otherwise the critic evaluates against generic good-outline-ness — and your deliberate kishōtenketsu twist gets flagged as "under-conflicted"); *classes of fix, not rewrites* prevents the machine from becoming the outline's real author (and your adjudication collapsing into rubber-stamping — the authorship trap of Chapter 15's spirit, caught early); *damage ratings* force prioritization (twelve equal notes are noise — Chapter 13's triage lesson, pre-installed); *three-findings close* beats recency bias (the final three notes are the ones you'll remember — make them the worst three); *no summary paragraph* starves the agreeable wind-down ("overall, a strong outline with minor opportunities…") before it starts.

**The adjudication ritual — non-negotiable, the chapter's spine.** After every critic pass: open the decisions file (or an outline-notes file hanging off it) and log, *for every finding*: kept / rejected / deferred, one line of reason. Example real rows from Example A's first pass (you'll see these fully in the worked example): *"- PC-11 #3 midpoint was incident not map-change → KEPT: journal-reveal now changes Theo's leverage (stakes line tie-in)." / "- PC-11 #6 suggested adding a courtroom beat for climax prop → REJECTED: length target + we refuse legal-drama genre creep (bible §5)."* The ritual's mechanics matter: findings you can't find in your outline = you and the critic read different documents (go re-read before deciding — misfiring notes reveal outline ambiguities, which are *findings about findings* worth a v-next edit); findings that are right but expensive get DEFERRED with a revisit date (deferral with a date is intelligence; deferral without one is deletion with extra steps). Run the whole pass *before* drafting level 3 anywhere — structure corrections are free at level 2 and expensive at level 3, the Chapter 2 law at another altitude.

### When discovery writers meet outlines

Part of this book's readership drafts by discovering, and hears "beat outline" as a cage. The honest reconciliation, since the workflow needs a level-2 freeze to feed Chapters 10–12: **the outline you commit to can be written *after* a discovery draft, and many do it that way.** Path A (planners): skeleton → level 2 → critic loop → draft from beats. Path B (discoverers): skeleton (you already have one — even discovery writers keep a compass) → draft *one scene at a time*, checking only that the scene's goal/conflict/outcome serves the skeleton's five fields → *reverse-outline* (write the level-2 line for each scene as completed, in past tense: "Mara arrives; keys force her to find Theo first") → run the critic pass on the *reverse* outline before revising (Chapter 12) rather than before drafting. Both paths converge on the same artifact — a level-2 outline that has survived one adversarial pass — before any structural revision begins; they differ only in *when the lines were written*. The pitfall below (outline worship) applies with equal force to both: a Path-B writer who *refuses* to look at their reverse outline before revision is not discovering; they're postponing the diagnosis.

## Pacing and subplots

Two instruments beyond the six checks, each with a card, each earning its keep at specific moments (critic pass #1 for the six checks; these two when the six checks come back *clean* but the read still feels off — pacing and weave are the finer gauges).

> PROMPT CARD PC-12 — Pacing checker
>
> ```text
> 
> Role: pacing analyst; you measure tempo, you don't judge ideas.
> Input: LEVEL-2 OUTLINE with each beat's approximate word-share
> estimate (my numbers, my responsibility) OR chapter word-targets
> if drafting has begun.
> Task: (1) Build a tempo table: beat | space allotted | pressure type
> (pursuit/refusal/revelation/reaction/transition) | rung moved
> (P/R/E/—). (2) Flag: any 3+ consecutive 'transition' or '—' rows;
> any revelation cluster within 2 beats (informational indigestion);
> any rung-movement gaps longer than 4 rows. (3) Compare my
> midpoint's share of total space to my structure's teaching
> default (three-act ≈ 40–50%; note deviations without correcting
> them). Output: table, then flags, then stop — no recommendations
> beyond flag classes.
> ```

Pacing, operationalized: **space + pressure-type distribution + rung rhythm**, not "feels slow." The tempo table turns the felt problem into a visible one (the Act II hole is three transition rows and two "—"s in a row — you can *see* the corridor the hook's reader walked), and the flags-without-recommendations rule keeps PC-12 an instrument rather than a second critic (recommendation authority stays consolidated in PC-11's adjudication — two cards prescribing fixes will disagree, and split prescriptions are how outlines fork into paralysis). Space estimates are *yours* by design ("my numbers, my responsibility") — estimates forced by you are honest approximations; estimates the machine invents are fiction about fiction.

> PROMPT CARD PC-13 — Subplot weaver
>
> ```text
> 
> Role: subplot engineer; every subplot must pay rent in the main
> line or leave the document.
> Context: LEVEL-2 OUTLINE (main line) + my subplot inventory below
> (name · thematic echo of main want · structural function).
> Task: for EACH subplot: (1) ENTRY: name the earliest beat where
> its absence would be noticed — if none, mark VESTIGIAL; (2) TOUCH
> BEATS: every main-line beat where the subplot must surface (min
> every 4–5 beats — flag longer silences); (3) PAYOFF: the exact
> beat where it changes stakes or meaning of the main line — if you
> can't point at one, mark RENT-FREE and recommend parking; (4)
> COLLISION: the beat where subplot and main line *physically share
> a scene* (thematically adjacent subplots with no shared scene are
> two stories mailing each other). Output: per-subplot cards +
> inventory of VESTIGIAL / RENT-FREE items. I decide what to park.
> ```

The four marks (entry / touch / payoff / collision) are the whole weaving discipline compressed: subplots die of *neglect* (forgotten for twelve beats — the reader forgets too, and re-entry costs a scene of re-introduction) and of *delusion* (thematic resonance mistaken for structural function — "it echoes the inheritance theme" is not rent; a beat that *changes a main-line decision* is rent). The inventory preamble forces *you* to have named functions before the machine audits them — again the pattern: you propose aims, it tests structure against them. Budget guidance for beginners: **one subplot per ~15 level-2 beats in a novel, zero subplots in a long short story** unless the form is braided on purpose (a short story with a vestigial subplot is almost always a novella that won't admit it — the capacity check from Chapter 8 catching a scale error from the other side).

**When to abandon the outline.** The escape hatch, stated without romance: you abandon — not "bend," *abandon and rebuild* — when (1) the critic loop can't converge: three passes producing *new* high-damage findings rather than converging (the F-07 signature at outline stage — usually means the skeleton's fields 4/5/10 were soft all along: go *up* a level, fix the stakes or obstacle, regenerate level 2 wholesale rather than patching); (2) drafting has *disproved* a load-bearing beat (your hands know something your outline didn't — the discovery-draft or mid-draft disproof is information, not betrayal); (3) the book you're actually writing has different *aims* than the one you outlined (premise drift — return to Chapter 8's logline first: if the logline is still true, the outline betrayed it; if the logline isn't true anymore, the premise stage reopens). The mechanics of abandonment are boring and vital: **mint nothing mid-panic** — write `outline-v2 ABANDONED · reason` in the changelog, re-derive from the skeleton (+ bible syncs), re-run PC-11 on the fresh v-next. What you never do: silently rewrite outline beats inside the doc (un-changelog'd structure edits are how "felt fine until Act II" holes get *re-seeded* — you'll fight this same hole at draft stage with no memory of changing the conditions). And the counter-hatchet: **an outline is never abandoned for the crime of being followed** — if the draft proceeds as planned, that's not rigidity, that's an outline working; discovery-culture's suspicion of plans and planner-culture's worship of them are the same error mirrored — the document serves the story, and the changelog is how you keep *that* relationship honest too.

### Level-2 line clinic: change-lines before and after

The granularity rule ("changes, not activity") is easiest to install by *editing* — specimen lines, diagnosed in the order their failures usually appear:

| ❌ Activity label | ❌ Scene description | ✅ Change-line |
|---|---|---|
| "Mara's arrival scene" | "Mara drives up in the rain and enters the house" | "Mara arrives expecting an empty house — finds Theo's boxes; the sale is now a standoff" |
| "The storm hits" | "Dramatic storm sequence with power outage" | "Storm cuts power *and* both exit routes — the clock stops being calendar and becomes physical" |
| "Mara and Theo talk" | "A long conversation about their mother" | "Theo names the promise he made; Mara's exit plan loses its moral cover" |

Read down the right column aloud: every line contains a *before → after* the reader can name (standoff discovered; routes gone; moral cover lost) — the Chapter 4 scene-anatomy contract lifted to structure level: beats *promise* scenes that change something, and the promise is testable before drafting. The left column's sins, named: **activity label** (no opinion about what's at stake — a table of contents for your own scenes, useless under pressure), **scene description** (previews prose mood instead of committing to change — the outline starts *writing for you*, which is how beat-lists ossify into must-imitate scripts), and — the subtle third — **backstory recap** ("Mara remembers the argument of '09" filed as a beat: memory is *material inside* a beat; it only earns a line when the *remembering changes a present decision*).

Editing drill (feeds the Try exercise): take five activity lines from any outline you've written and convert them right-column-style — the test for done is the *neighbor test*: with only your five lines visible, can you say how each change *pressures* the next? ("standoff discovered" → wants-a-clean-exit impossible → notary-clock *matters*) — activity lines don't pressurize neighbors; change-lines do, and the pressure *is* the outline's real content. When PC-11's check #4 flags "and then" pairs, the cure is almost always this clinic: beats that don't *change* anything can't be linked by "therefore/but," because there's no state for the conjunction to bite on.

### Critique-pass hygiene: contexts, batching, fresh sessions

Three operational rules that keep PC-11 (and its siblings PC-20…22 in Chapter 12) producing signal:

**Fresh session per pass, always.** A critic run inherits every prior exchange's gravity — the model has been *your drafting partner* for forty messages; its "critique" will now negotiate with the tone it learned from you (sycophancy's most sophisticated form: not flattery, but *continuity of agreeableness*). Passes are verdicts, not conversations: new session, full context paste (skeleton + outline + card), zero warm-up. The mild cost of re-pasting is the price of an unanchored reader.

**Context packs, assembled from the hub.** Never paste from memory's idea of the documents; paste from files: bible §1 + stakes + the frozen outline + length/structure declarations — a standard "context pack" of 300–600 words you can re-assemble in a minute *because the bible's sections already have the headings*. Chapter 7's hub-and-spoke paying off at altitude: passes that used to require twenty minutes of rummaging become a copy-ritual, which is why the pass actually gets run instead of skipped when tired (compliance with your own process is a design problem — design for tired-you).

**Batch by judgment type, not by anxiety.** The temptation: run a new pass every time a doubt surfaces mid-draft ("is it the midpoint? is it pacing? — let me ask"). Doomed: doubts compound into overlapping passes whose findings interleave, and adjudication rows start contradicting each other (you kept #3 from pass A, rejected #2 from pass B, and can't now tell which outline version either read). The discipline: **one pass type per outline version, ordered** (six-check structure first → pacing table second → weaving third — each *after* the previous adjudications have been applied and re-minted), doubts collected in the session log's blockers column between passes ("note: pacing suspicion — queued for PC-12 after structure v-next"). Findings against *stale* documents are worse than no findings: they send you editing beats a kept row already moved. Version discipline for passes: every critic prompt's paste carries the outline's version header (`outline-v3`), and adjudication rows open with the same version tag — if a row can't name its target, the row is invalid and the pass gets re-run against current state. It reads bureaucratic; it is the difference between a loop that converges in two passes and one that *vibrates* (F-07's outline-stage sibling — the convergence signature you'll meet formally in Chapter 12).

### The tempo table, run on Example A (PC-12 live)

Instruments teach best with readings taken, so here is PC-12's output on the v1 outline from the worked example — flags first, adjudications after (the card *forbids* recommendations, which is exactly why the rows below are *yours* to write):

```text
beat | space(est %) | pressure type | rung
1 arrival/keys      | 6  | orientation→refusal      | P?
2 notary+Casi       | 8  | revelation (clock starts)| P
3 porch standoff    | 9  | refusal/refusal          | — ←flag r1
4 storm warning     | 5  | pursuit (self-set deadline)| P
5 kitchen fight     | 11 | revelation + refusal     | R ✓
6 journal found     | 8  | revelation               | R
7 Aurelia coffee    | 7  | revelation               | E ←flag r2
   (transition-ish entry — see adjudication)
8 power+Theo reveal | 10 | revelation + revelation  | E? ←flag r3
9 Casi contract     | 9  | pursuit                  | — ←flag r4
10 night journal    | 12 | revelation               | E
11 dawn signing     | 14 | refusal/decision         | P/R
midpoint share: beat 5–8 ≈ 39% vs. teaching default 40–50% ←flag r5
```

*Flags, ranked:* **r3 (revelation cluster, beats 6–8):** three information-beats in a row — informational indigestion *in the outline itself* (readers of the draft would receive the journal, the neighbor, and the promise as a *lecture series* unless something between them *presses*); **r5 (midpoint share low):** corroborates r3's diagnosis from the other instrument — the middle isn't just *dense with news*, it's *under-weighted in space*, which is how "felt fine until Act II" holes get built from *too much plot in too little room* dressed as the opposite problem; **r1 + r4 (rung gaps around refusal/pursuit beats):** beats 3 and 9 are *behaviorally right* (standoff, escalation) but *structurally silent* on rung movement — they *enact* conflict without *advancing stakes*, which at level 2 means their change-lines under-specified (the Ch 9 clinic's conversion will fix them: beat 3's line must *state* what the standoff costs Theo's keeping-plan, not just note mutual ignoring); **r2 (Aurelia as transition):** likely *healthy* literary breathing (the adjudication from the worked example already treated this adjacency as mood-chained by design) — flagged-but-kept, consistent with the v1 ritual's rejected-with-craft-substitute row.

*The adjudications' shape* (writing them out is the exercise's real content): r3+r5 → **KEPT together** — the fix that serves both: a *press-beat* inserted between 6 and 7 (Mara attempts the notary call — refused for information she now can't use — the pursuit flicker *and* a revelation-breaker in one line — note this is the *same* micro-pursuit the PC-11 ritual already queued as the 6b insert: **two independent instruments recommending the same fix class = the chapter's corroboration pattern working again**); r1/r4 → **DEFERRED to level-3 drafting** with dates ("when beat 3's scene is written, its change-line must name the cost — verify at mint"), the noted-typo altitude rule applied to *outline quality itself*; r2 → **WAIVED-with-reason** citing the literary-register note (the waiver row *then* gets pasted into any future PC-12 re-run's context — "known waivers: beat 7 adjacency" — so the instrument stops re-spending its flag-budget on settled weather). The tempo table's deeper lesson in one line: **flags are cheap, verdicts are the product** — an instrument that flags and *stops* leaves you with a prettier spreadsheet; an instrument whose flags pass the adjudication ritual leaves you with *an outline that has been measured, argued with, and versioned* — which is the only kind of outline that deserves the word "frozen" in its changelog entry.

### Outline maintenance during drafting: the living document

"The outline serves the draft" needs a *mechanics* section, or it decays into good intentions — so here is the maintenance regime that keeps `outline-vN` and `draft-current` speaking to each other without either dominating, written as the week-by-week habits it actually is:
