AI's role on this rung: **PC-27's analysis half** — paste the original page and ask for a *feature inventory* ("list the techniques this page uses: sentence-length pattern (numbers), image sources, hedge frequency, balance usage, paragraph architecture — features only, no quality judgments") — the inventory is a *checklist of what to copy*, making the hand-copy targeted (you're now practicing *features*, not vibes); the *copying itself stays in your hands* (the whole neurological argument of the rung would evaporate if the machine did it — analysis may be delegated; **acquisition may not**, it's rep-based skill like the taste-gate reps of Ch 1, just aimed at syntax).

**Rung 2 — Variation (the controlled mutation).** Take *your* imitation-page (now semi-installed) and produce **three variations that break exactly one axis each**: (a) same techniques, *your* subject matter (their clause-architecture describing *your* fishing town — the transfer test: do the moves survive without their content?); (b) same content, *opposite* their tendencies (their balanced clauses → all your ragged ones; their long meditations → cut every reflection-tax sentence — running the diagnostic *on their style* and fixing it reveals *which of their habits you actually wanted* vs. which you were admiring on authority); (c) same features at *half-length* (compression exposes the load-bearing 40% — if the imitation needs every ornament to work, you loved the ornament, not the method). PC-27's generation half *proposes* variation (b) and (c) drafts *labeled as exercises* — machine text permitted here *explicitly* because this rung's product is *muscle memory of contrast*, not pages: you read the machine's mutated versions *against yours* and articulate the difference ("its half-length cut my favorite image — because the image was *decoration in the variation*, load-bearing in mine" — that sentence is voice-knowledge, worth more than either draft). The rung's completion test: write 300 fresh words on *your* material using *one chosen axis* of the inventory — read them beside your baseline: **different from your old self, recognizable as no one else** — imitation that *moved* you without *replacing* you = one rung climbed.

**Rung 3 — Voice (the deliberate signature).** Voice, defined operationally for this book: **the set of stylistic choices you make *consistently under pressure*** — not your vocabulary on a good day (that's mood) but your habits when tired, when drafting fast, when the machine's suggestions look *good enough* (the pressure is the definers: anyone can be stylish at leisure; voice is what you do at 11 p.m. with a deadline). The rung's work is therefore *cataloging* rather than inventing: from your style sheet (below), your runs-2 deviations, and your baseline-vs-current gaps, you extract **three to five named features that are *yours* and that you'll defend** — maybe: *clipped fragments after questions* (your rhythm tell), *diction pitched one class below the scene's emotion* (your register choice — feeling delivered in plain words because ornament would lie), *reflections performed only through objects* (your answer to the reflection-tax — never appended, always enacted: the soup pot *is* the grief). Naming them does three jobs: they become **locks you protect** (the sheet's KEEP list — PC-26 preserves them by name, PC-14's anchors *are* them, PC-18's fingerprint-logic runs on them — your features are your characters' anti-flattening cousin: *author-level* fingerprints); they become **the diagnostic's baseline** (symptoms are *deviations from your named features* — the checklist stops being other-people's taste); and they become **checkable claims about originality** — which is where this chapter's last理论 lands.

**Originality as recombination + lived specificity.** The anxious age's question — *"is originality even possible when the machine remixes everything?"* — dissolves under this book's definitions twice over. *Recombination:* every writer you admire is already a recombination (their influences *documented*, their originality lying in *which* combinations, under *what* pressures, executed with *what consistency* — your voice-features × your obsessions from Ch 8's why-me file × your constraint bank's material = a combination whose *specific instance* no training set contains as a *choice*, because choices-with-names-under-pressure are exactly what the distribution doesn't have (it has their *outputs*, not their *defended commitments*). And *lived specificity* — the second engine — is structurally immune: the machine completes *representations* of experiences; it does not have *yours* (the Tuesday your father's depot closed and the particular smell of its parts crib — Chapter 1's "true details" and Chapter 4's "owned concrete" at maximum stakes: prose that could only exist because *you* were there is original the way a fingerprint is original — not novel-with-a-capital-N (the machine's problem, Ch 1's inventory) but *specific*, which was always the operable kind). The ladder's honest claim, then: **style can be practiced into consistency (rungs 1–2), originality can only be *kept specific* (rung 3 + the why-me file + the sheets)** — the machine touches the first and *demonstrates the absence* of the second, which is why a voice-owning writer in 2026 has *less* competition at the level that matters, not more: most generated text never gets specific enough to collide with anyone's particular life — your whole advantage is doing *that*, on purpose, with a sheet.

## Your style sheet

The chapter's central artifact (template — copy to `bible/style-sheet.md`, v1 today, revised at every line pass):

```text
STYLE SHEET — [project or personal]            v1 · 2026-09-XX

1. DICTION CLASS (one line — narrator's register, stated as a
   range): "plain, physical words; abstract nouns only when they're
   also weather/measurements; one Latinate flourish per chapter,
   chosen."
2. RHYTHM NOTE (your signature imbalance — from baseline's
   length-column): "medium default, but every 4th paragraph needs
   a runt (<7w) and periodic sentences are mine — max one 'lovely'
   30-w per page."
3. BANNED CONSTRUCTIONS (growing list — machine-default +
   self-tics caught by diagnostics; each entry dated):
   - hedge-strings >1 per 3 sentences (2026-09)
   - "palpable/ethereal/unwavering" class (upgrade-itis exemplars)
   - emotion-as-weather ("guilt rose like tide") (from PC-14 v2)
   - "not just X, but Y" beyond one per page
   - reflection-tax closers after performed beats
   - "she couldn't help but" (your own Ch-1 tic — baseline-
     harvested: tics-to-kill from the *unaided* record)
4. KEEPS (deliberate oddities PC-26 must preserve — [!mine] list):
   - fragments after questions (feature 1)
   - emotion-in-plain-words (feature 2)
   - objects-performed-feelings only (feature 3)
   - typos of voice? NO — dialect spelled as spoken only in
     [X]'s speech, noted per-character in bible §2
5. VERB POLICY: active by default; three passive slots allowed
   per chapter where agency *should* blur (reporting, dread).
6. FIGURE BUDGET: one simile per ~1,000w; sources restricted to
   [your domains: weather, mechanics, tide]; no metaphor for
   emotions directly — objects only (ties to feature 3).
7. REVISION-DATE + NEXT-REVIEW (line-pass trigger — sheets live
   at pass 4, v-bump when a ban graduates from a diagnostic).
```

Sheet-writing craft notes: **items 3 and 4 are the engine** (bans *from evidence* — every entry cites the diagnostic or baseline-tic that caught it, so the sheet's authority is *your own record*, immune to "whose taste is this?" — and keeps *equally* evidenced, or the sheet becomes a purity-spiral that sanded your weirdness off); **item 1's range-wording** (a single-word class declaration like "plain" gets violated by every tool reading it — ranges with *inclusions and one budgeted exception* ("one Latinate flourish per chapter") are what machine-passes can actually *execute against* — the sheet is a contract *with instruments*, which is why its format looks half-essay, half-spec); **the growth path** (v1: from baseline-tics + this chapter's diagnostics, ~15 lines; v2 at your first line pass: real hits added; v3: PC-26's keep-flags revealing *features you didn't know you had* — the sheet's most valuable entries are the ones *discovered in your own defended revisions* — decisions-file mining for style rows: every "kept my wording over the machine's, because ___" is a feature *caught in the act*; the sheet is that column of your ledger, systematized). Maintenance cadence: **read at line-pass entry, bump at line-pass exit, full re-read quarterly** (the bans that *stopped firing* graduate to a "cold" subsection — a ban you haven't triggered in three passes was either internalized (feature now automatic — move it to KEEP-side pride) or *wrong* for the register (delete with a note — the sheet may *correct* itself, versioned like everything else this book has touched: a style sheet that never revises itself is the static-outline disease wearing academic costume).

### The diagnosis-to-surgery handoff (one page, both halves)

The chapter's two instruments (4-minute diagnostic, PC-27/PC-26 surgery) are *adjacent*; the handoff between them is where writers lose the thread — so the seam, specified: **the diagnostic's output *becomes the surgery's input, structurally*** — not "you found symptoms, now fix them" (advice) but a *literal transfer protocol*: the four counts-table row for each family routes to its PC-26 handle (H1-count>threshold → hedge-strip pass; H2-bans-hits → diction-demote with *your* class's substitutes; H3-band-narrow → rhythm-break marked stretches; H4-tax-events → reflection-tax cut with keep-flags) — **and each routed handle carries the diagnostic's *numbers* as its exit-conditions** (the surgery doesn't end when the passage "reads better"; it ends when H1-count ≤ threshold on the *same* counting method — instrument-meets-instrument with *shared units*, the seam's whole trick: diagnosis without routing produces *anxious awareness* (you now know something's wrong, you fix what *annoys you*, the worst-offenders survive their invisibility); routing without diagnosis produces *theater surgery* (opening PC-26 with no counts = no exit-condition = you stop when *bored* — F-07's emotional cousin at micro-scale). The transfer artifact (sixty seconds to build, the handoff made physical): the diagnostic's table *pasted at the top of PC-26's context block*, above the style sheet, with one line prepended: **"exit: counts below thresholds per this table; keep-flags listed; `[!mine]` markers set."** That one paste-line chain — *diagnostic numbers → handle routing → exit-conditions → adjudication rows* — is Ch 14's entire workflow in its smallest possible form, and the version worth memorizing when the chapters close: **measure, route, cap, adjudicate** — four verbs, fifteen minutes, the voice-layer's immune response, ready for the next Friday the mode-log marks fatigue and the next quarterly baseline that needs *not* to embarrass the last.

Real surgery — a co-drafted paragraph from Example A's storm-night stretch (machine-heaviest region per its mode-log), diagnosis first, then PC-26's table, then the adjudication:

**Before (diagnostic run — counts in margin):** *"It was, in a way, a kind of quiet that seemed to settle over the house as the power failed — a palpable silence, not just empty but somehow waiting, as if the building itself were holding its breath in anticipation of what might happen next. Mara felt a sense of something like recognition at this moment, and she couldn't help but think that the darkness had always been there, latent within these rooms, patient and unspoken."*

**Diagnostic counts:** hedges/fillers: *"in a way," "a kind of," "seemed," "somehow," "as if," "something like"* = 6 (threshold blown); upgrade-itis: *"palpable," "latent," "unspoken"* (class: MFA-brochure — narrator's class is plain-physical); rhythm: 34w / 26w / 28w — narrow-band corridor, zero runts; reflection-tax: sentence 2's "recognition" + sentence 3's entire meditation appended *after* the performed beat (power failed = the scene *showed* waiting — the building-holding-its-breath line *comments* on it symmetrically — "not just empty but somehow waiting" is the X-but-Y pair, doubled with "patient and unspoken"); worst-offender quote (feeds PC-26's context): *"a palpable silence, not just empty but somehow waiting."*

**PC-26's output (table abridged; sheet pasted: banned §3 entries all cited):**

```text
| ORIGINAL | EDITED | tag |
| "It was, in a way, a kind of quiet that seemed to settle" | "Quiet settled" | H1×3 hedge-strip + H3 split (runt installed) |
| "a palpable silence, not just empty but somehow waiting, as if the building itself were holding its breath" | "a silence with the house's weight in it — holding, like a held breath" | H2 demote (palpable → house-class: weight/held = mechanic's diction) + H4 symmetry: "not-X-but-Y" side killed, breath-image kept ONE side |
| "Mara felt a sense of something like recognition at this moment" | [CUT — scene's performed beat stands alone] | H4 reflection-tax (recognition already *in* her stopping at the breaker) |
| "and she couldn't help but think that the darkness had always been there, latent within these rooms, patient and unspoken" | "Darkness had been in these rooms all along. Patient." | H1 + KEEP-partial: meditation *reduced* not deleted (flag: load-bearing? — LEAVE-flag worked: the darkness-was-always-there thought carries ch10's journal payoff-sign — kept core, cut wrappers "latent/unspoken/couldn't-help") |
counts: hedges −6, class-changes 3, rhythm-breaks 2 (runt + 14w close), reflections −1, symmetry −1 pair
```

**After (clean passage):** *"Quiet settled when the power failed. A silence with the house's weight in it — holding, like a held breath. Mara stopped at the breaker. Darkness had been in these rooms all along. Patient."* — and **the adjudication** (rows you'd write): *"KEEP the cut's core per flag (journal-payoff sign) — machine correctly identified load-bearing and left the skeleton; 'held breath' image: MY KEEP-side (sheet §4 objects-performed-feelings — the house breathes = object-feeling ✓); 'Quiet settled' runt: rhythm-break accepted — my rhythm note says every 4th paragraph needs a runt; one REJECT: its 'like a held breath' kept my doubled 'holding/held' — the repetition is [!mine] (marking habit paid: the preserve-rule caught its own near-miss)."*

*Commentary — the five moves that matter:* the paragraph went from 62 words of generic weather to 24 of *house-class plain physics* **with the architecture unchanged** (the performed beat still lands where the draft's decisions put it — de-AI ≠ re-outline); every cut *cited its sheet entry* (the sheet-as-contract, executing — and note the pass got *better* as the sheet got real: PC-26 without §3's specific bans would have produced "different but still someone else's" prose); the **leave-flag saved the meditation's core** (the conservative bias *working* — the machine couldn't know "darkness-always-there" was structural, so it *asked* instead of killing — and your adjudication, citing *ch10's payoff-sign*, answered with the outline open: style-surgery consulting structure, the pipeline's layers *talking*); the **[!mine] near-miss** (its cleaned version *smoothed your deliberate holding-held echo* — caught because the marker was in the paste — the preserve-list's value demonstrated in the pass's own first run: unmarked, your echo would have died politely, and *no diagnostic would have flagged a deletion* — the asymmetry (symptoms find additions, markers protect *intentional* oddities) is why the full pipeline needs *all* its layers: diagnosis misses what it can't see was deliberate, only *you* can mark deliberate); and the count-trend (**6 hedges → 0, corridor broken, tax paid down**) — the pass *converged on the sheet's thresholds* and stopped (bounded — no vibe of "maybe prettier still?" — that itch is F-07's ghost, and the count-arithmetic keeps it exorcised).

Apply the same six-move reading to *your* worst paragraph: diagnose with counts → sheet pasted → flags respected → adjudicate with your outline open → recount. The paragraph-level version of this chapter, repeated as a habit, *is* the voice program: maintenance by instruments, growth by ladder, originality by specificity — three systems, one name on the cover.

## Exercises

- **Try (15 min):** Run the four-minute diagnostic on one page of your current draft — margin counts for all four families + the existential test ("anyone's narrator?") + worst-offender quoted. Then read your `baseline` page with the *same* pen: which symptoms *predated the machine* (your original tics — style-sheet seed from the unaided record, honestly claimed), which are *new to the assisted era* (the drift column — PC-26's first targets). Two columns of evidence, zero self-flagellation: the baseline's problems were craft-addressable, the new drift is *process*-addressable, and you now hold both lists in one page's margins.

- **Stretch (1–2 h):** The style imitation ladder, rungs 1→2 in sequence on *one* chosen writer: (1) feature inventory via PC-27's analysis prompt on one of their pages → hand-copy the page → memory-copy → note drift (3 places you "misremembered" = their habits vs. yours — write one line each); (2) pick *one* axis from the inventory and produce variation (a) 300 words on your material using it (hand-written — acquisition stays yours), and variation (b) deliberately *break* that axis on the same material (PC-27 may propose one draft per variation, labeled exercise-only) → read all four texts aloud in sequence (their page, your copy, your variation, your break) → five lines: which single technique you're *keeping* as a new KEEP candidate, and the evidence (the rung-2 transfer test's verdict — did the move survive without their content?). Promotion decision goes to the sheet as a *candidate with evidence line*, v-bump at next line pass — the ladder feeds the sheet; the sheet feeds every pass thereafter.

- **Project (ongoing):** Style sheet v1 → v3 over the next drafting stretch (v1 today from Try's two columns; v2 at the first line pass with real ban-hits; v3 after PC-26's keep-flags have *discovered* two features you didn't know you had — the sheet's growth curve is the voice-program's progress bar). Every line pass reads the sheet at entry and bumps it at exit (the maintenance cadence — bans graduating to "cold," keeps accruing with evidence lines). And the comparison this whole chapter opened: **re-run the baseline test quarterly** — fresh 200 unaided words + 200 current-draft words, cold, side by side: you are looking for the gap *changing shape* (raw-to-eroded was the hook; the goal-state is raw-craft *fixed* at the baseline's roots while the drift column empties — when your current pages read *both* smooth *and* unmistakably like the person who wrote the baseline, name three features and all three appear on inspection: the checklist passes, the sheet exists, the ladders have run — Chapter done, voice in progress, as it will be forever).

## Pitfalls

> PITFALL — **Voice erosion (F-09).** *Symptom:* the draft converges on AI-default *gradually enough to be invisible session-by-session* — quarterly, your pages read like a competent stranger's; beta residue-images (Ch 13's Q8) come back *generic* where you designed specific; the Ch 12 voice-audit's shuffle-test drops below your done-criteria's threshold; you catch yourself *unable to produce a paragraph unaided* that matches your own sheet (the deepest symptom: the voice now lives in the tool, not in you — anchors feel like scaffolding for a building that forgot its foundation). *Why it happens:* **the pull never turns off** (Ch 5's center-of-mass is a permanent force — erosion is the *default direction* of assisted drafting under incomplete layers; each skipped layer (anchor → marking → blind-test → diagnosis → de-AI) *raises the leak-rate* slightly — the compound-interest structure of F-09: no single skipped session ruins anything; thirty skipped sessions *rebrand your narrator*); **house-register seduction under deadline** (the machine's smooth paragraphs are *faster to accept at 11 p.m.* — the acceptance curve and the fatigue curve are the same curve — mode-log cross-refs will show your drift clustering on your tired days, exactly like Ch 11's Sunday-smiles); **sheet-staleness** (a sheet not read at pass entry is decorative — bans you've stopped enforcing *silently stop binding* — the tool reads whatever's in the paste, and stale pastes license drift); and **the identity dodge** (symptom families are easier to spot in *others'* prose than admit in yours — "generic" is a criticism writers fear applying to *their own darlings*, so the diagnostic gets run on chapters you *suspect* rather than the ones mode-logs *name* — comfort-diagnosis, the evasion that keeps the drift column looking empty while chapter 9 is colonized). *Fix (layered — matching the pipeline, in drift-detected order):* (1) **stop the leak *upstream* tonight:** re-verify the anchor discipline's functional test (Ch 10's "would generation lose its hinges without this paragraph?") on your next scene — if anchors were decorative, regenerate the worst affected stretch *with beats + locks* (the Ch 10 fallback machinery — drift is *recoverable at drafting-precision* before it's expensive at de-AI-precision); (2) **measure before mood:** re-run the four-minute diagnostic on the mode-log's *machine-heaviest* chapter and on a *self-suspected* chapter (the comfort-diagnosis antidote is *mandated sampling* — the two columns you can't dodge), symptom counts recorded as the *audit baseline* (Ch 7's monthly voice-check now has numbers, not vibes); (3) **surgery with the full kit:** PC-26 per worst paragraph — sheet pasted, leave-flags honored, `[!mine]` markers *written before the paste* (the near-miss lesson: mark *first*, pass *second*), adjudication rows with your outline open (structure-suspects cited, not guessed), counts re-run to thresholds (bounded exit — two runs, done, next paragraph: the pass version of F-07-prevention applied to voice); (4) **rebuild the *generative* voice, not just the cosmetic one:** if the unaided-paragraph test (Try's deepest probe) now fails *badly* — you can't write sheet-compliant prose without the tool — run the ladder *rung 1 hard* for two weeks (hand-copy + memory-copy daily, one feature at a time): imitation-at-daily-rep is how acquisition repairs *sedimented-out* habits — the model's smoothness un-learned by your hands' unevenness, the oldest technology for this exact problem still the best; (5) **close the loop where it entered:** draft-log the drift's *cause-session* (which mode, which fatigue, which missing layer — one honest row per colonized stretch: "co-draft, day 14 streak-tired, anchors skipped" — the *pattern* of those rows is your personal erosion-rhythm — schedule *hand-first sessions* proactively at those slots next month (drift-prevention as *calendar design*, the deepest form of the fix: you cannot skip the layer on the day you *planned* for the layer because you know yourself). *Prevention (the steady state — the five layers as one habit-stack, each cheaper than the last, total cost ≈ 15 minutes per scene):* anchor functionally → locks stated → mark incrementally → blind-test dialogue-heavy chapters → diagnose one page per drafting week → de-AI only on *counts-over-threshold* → sheet bump at every pass exit → quarterly baseline comparison as the *scheduling mechanism* for the whole program. The stack reads like a lot until you price its alternative: **a finished book whose voice you can no longer produce, defend, or recognize** — the book's whole argument (Ch 1's golden rule at maximum stakes) says that outcome isn't a style problem; it's an *authorship* problem, and authorship, once more: **you decide — every sentence that keeps your name.**

Related failure for Appendix C: **F-09 (voice erosion)** — full entry here with the layered fix-stack (upstream-stop → mandated-sampling audit → kit-surgery → ladder-rebuild → cause-row calendar-design); cross-refs: F-01/F-04 (caught upstream at Ch 10's locks/marking — F-09 is the *cumulative* failure when they leak), F-02 (character-voice cousin, Ch 11's stack), F-07 (the de-AI pass's bounded-count exit prevents voice-surgery non-convergence), Ch 13's Q8-residue (external early-warning: *readers* report your erosion in residue-images before your own ear does — betas as drift-instruments, feed round 2's questions accordingly).

## Checklist

> CHECKLIST — You're ready to move on when:
> - [ ] The four-minute diagnostic has run on: your machine-heaviest chapter (mode-log named), one self-suspected chapter (comfort-diagnosis antidote), and your Chapter 1 baseline — counts recorded in two columns: predates-the-machine vs. assisted-drift.
> - [ ] Your draft passed the generic-prose diagnostic *at your sheet's thresholds* for the sampled pages — OR the failures are *scheduled* (worst paragraphs queued for PC-26 rows, not left as atmospheric guilt).
> - [ ] Style sheet v1 exists (today — from Try's two columns + the template), already carrying: diction-class *range*, rhythm note, ≥5 dated bans with evidence-lines, ≥3 KEEP features with provenance, figure budget — and it has *lived through* one bump (any bump: a line-pass read, a diagnostic harvest, a [!mine] promotion — a sheet that has never revised itself isn't yet a sheet).
> - [ ] The ladder has run at least one full sequence (inventory → hand/memory copy → drift-notes → one-axis variation on your material) with its keep-candidate promoted or explicitly declined — acquisition attempted *by hand*, the rung rule honored.
> - [ ] You can name three voice features that are *yours* — with evidence lines from the sheet — and, from the baseline comparison, one sentence on how your unaided writing changed since Chapter 1 (the honest-craft column: what the *practice*, independent of tools, repaired — because this book's voice program was never only about the machine; it was about knowing, on purpose, what your sentences do).

## Key takeaways

- **"Generic AI prose" is a symptom cluster, not provenance:** hedges (confidence-laundering — strip-and-retest), thesaurus weather (class-mismatch is the sharpest detector), rhythmic monotony (the 14–22 corridor — absence of *designed* irregularity), reflection-tax and symmetry-by-default — each with a *countable* test; diagnosis is quantitative, tool-neutral, and yours the moment the sheet exists to set thresholds.
- **The de-AI pass is re-voicing, not re-deciding:** PC-26 runs on your pasted sheet + diagnosis quote, leaves-if-unsure with flags, breaks symmetry at *your* chosen side, preserves `[!mine]` (mark *before* pasting — the near-miss is the rule's proof), counts to thresholds (bounded), and never touches adjudicated structure — without a sheet, it just installs *different* generic prose; the sheet is the instrument, the card is only its executor.
- **Voice is built on the three-rung ladder:** imitation (feature inventories delegated, *copying hand-held* — acquisition never outsourced), variation (one-axis mutations — transfer, inversion, compression — where machine drafts are labeled exercises and your articulations of the difference are the real product), voice (your choices *consistently under pressure* — cataloged as three-to-five defended, named features that become KEEP-locks, diagnostic baselines, and fingerprint logic).
- **Originality survives the machine as recombination *plus lived specificity*:** defended commitments under named pressure aren't in any distribution, and your Tuesdays are structurally uncompletable — specific beats novel-with-a-capitol-N, and *most* generated prose never gets specific enough to collide with yours.
- **Erosion is default-direction and layered-fixed:** anchors → locks → marking → blind-tests → weekly diagnosis → count-bounded surgery → sheet-bumps → quarterly baseline comparisons — each layer cheap, each skip compounding, cause-rows designing the calendar that prevents the skip — because at F-09's endgame, the question was never stylistic: a voice you can no longer produce unaided is a name you can no longer honestly sign. Sheets, ladders, and counts exist so you always can.


# Chapter 15 — Ethics, Disclosure & the Law

> **Scope warning (printed, not hidden):** every legal and regulatory statement in this chapter is *information, not legal advice* — sourced to official material where a source exists, date-stamped, and flagged as perishable. Rules in this area change; policies change faster. This chapter's job is to make you *fluent enough to check* — with the check itself taught as a skill — not to substitute for a lawyer's advice on your specific situation. Re-verify every row against `research/claim-register.md` (Appendix E prints the consumer version) before publication or before relying on any statement here.

## Opening hook

The rejection email used three sentences on the story and one on the *form*. A contest finalist's slot, withdrawn: "Applicants must disclose the use of generative AI in the creation of submitted work; no disclosure was present on your entry." Your story used AI exactly as this book teaches — brainstorming, continuity passes, one line-edited chapter — and you *knew* that, in the way you know your own birthday. You did not know that *this* contest's entry form, in 8-point type below the upload button, wanted that knowledge **in writing before submission**, and that "I figured it was obvious from the drafts" is not a disclosure.

Here's the quieter horror underneath the anecdote: nothing about the *story* was dishonest. The failure was **provenance hygiene** — the habit of knowing *where your assistance came from, what it touched, and what each venue needs to be told* — a skill adjacent to everything this book already trains (session logs, decisions files, pass records: you have *been* keeping provenance; this chapter teaches you to *surface* it, to whom, when, and in what words). The stakes are concrete and asymmetric: get it wrong and you can lose a slot, a contract clause's protection, a reader's trust, or — at the sharp end — a copyright registration's accuracy. Get it right and the paperwork takes ten minutes, because your logs were always there.

This chapter teaches three things in order: **authorship lines** (what plagiarism is, what AI-assisted authorship is, and why disclosure is hygiene rather than confession), **venue fluency** (finding and following any publisher's, contest's, or platform's current AI policy in under ten minutes — because they differ, and difference is the whole game), and **the legal floor** (copyright's human-authorship baseline with jurisdiction boxes, contracts with AI clauses, real people as material, and academic-integrity parallels). By the end: you can find any venue's policy fast, your disclosure statement exists in three lengths, and you know which jurisdiction box applies to you.

## Learning objectives

By the end of this chapter, you will be able to:

- [ ] Distinguish plagiarism from legitimate AI-assisted authorship.
- [ ] Find and follow any venue's AI-disclosure policy.
- [ ] State the copyright basics that affect AI-assisted fiction (US baseline + UK/EU/BR boxes).
- [ ] Handle real people, privacy, and contracts with AI clauses — and say what this chapter is not.

## Plagiarism vs. authorship with AI in the loop

Draw the lines cleanly, because public discussion blurs them into one anxious smear. **Plagiarism** is *presenting someone else's expression as yours* — unattributed copying of a human's prose, ideas credited nowhere, a text lifted and signed. **Provenance fraud** is the new neighbor: presenting *machine-generated material as human-written where the venue's rules required disclosure* — the contest anecdote's actual species (note what it is *not*: not copying, not theft from a person — a *process* violation against a rule, which is why the sanction was withdrawal rather than plagiarism's moral register; mixing the categories rhetorically serves nobody and terrifies beginners into either silence-about-assistance or shame-about-use, both wrong). **AI-assisted authorship** is this book's entire practice: a human's aims, selections, revisions, and final responsibility with machine *labor* in the loop — and its authorship-claim (your name, alone, on your book) is *legitimate* under this book's working definition and under the copyright baseline you'll meet below — **provided the expressive choices that matter are demonstrably yours**: the voice-anchor, decisions-file, pass-log, and criteria machinery from Parts II–III isn't just productivity system — *it is the evidence trail of human authorship* (Chapter 7's row: "authorship evidence, keep factual" — the payoff arriving now: when a venue, a publisher's rights-and-warranties paragraph, or a future registration asks *what you did*, the answer is a folder, not a story you compose under pressure).

**Disclosure is hygiene, not confession.** The word "disclose" spooks writers because it *sounds like* an admission seeking forgiveness. Reframe it with precision: disclosure is a **provenance statement** — *this work was made with X assistance in Y areas, reviewed and revised by me* — the same species as "photos: archive of the author" or "typeset in Garamond": logistics of making, stated because the recipient's decisions depend on them (a contest's judging model, a publisher's risk assessment, a reader's expectations, *your own* registration accuracy). The stance this book takes, stated once and enforced throughout: **when a rule asks, answer fully; when a rule is silent, follow the venue's culture and your logs; when a rule forbids, don't submit — route around, don't rationalize.** Two supporting principles that make the stance *usable* rather than preachy: **never let disclosure be discovered for you** (the withdrawal-email's asymmetry — discovering *you* under-disclosed is infinitely worse than any honest disclosure would have been; over-disclosure costs a sentence, under-disclosure costs a slot), and **disclose at the granularity the rule asks** (some venues want a checkbox; some want a paragraph; some contract riders want *categories* of assistance itemized — the three-length statement you'll draft in the Stretch scales to all three; asking for *more* than the form's granularity wastes the reader's time and buries the answer).

> ETHICS NOTE — The mirror-image rule matters equally: **never claim assistance you didn't use as a badge.** "Written with AI" *brags* circulating as marketing are provenance fraud's backwards cousin — false statements about process corrode the disclosure channel *everyone* relies on (Ch 13's sim-vs-human warning at culture scale: if "AI-assisted" ever means *vibes* rather than *fact*, the term stops protecting the honest and starts indicting everyone). State what happened; let it be boring; boring accuracy is the whole ethic.

## Disclosure norms: verify each venue

Now the operational heart — because *rules differ, and the difference is the game*. **Claim C003 (register): major publishers, contests, magazines, and platforms have *differing* AI-disclosure policies, they change, and authors must check each venue's *current* rules.** The claim's evidence, dated: the policies demonstrably diverge — during the 2023 wave of consumer-model adoption, **Clarkesworld**, a leading SFF magazine, publicly *did not accept* AI-generated or AI-assisted writing per its then-current submission guidelines and, flooded with machine-submissions, *temporarily closed submissions* in February 2023 after over 500 account bans that month (reported Feb 2023 — Washington Post, Vice/Motherboard coverage; *and* the same month, other markets stated the opposite posture — e.g., **Metastellar** publicly announced it did *not* care what tools created a story, only whether it was good (their published position, Feb 2023)). Two reputable venues, *opposite rules, same week* — that's C003's thesis in one exhibit: **there is no industry default to default to.** (Perishable flag: every specific policy quoted here is *historical evidence of divergence*, not current permission — Clarkesworld's and any other venue's present rules must be read *at their guidelines page, dated by you, before submission*; the register's row carries the re-verify date.)

**The ten-minute policy hunt — taught as a repeatable procedure** (this is the actual skill; any example ages, the procedure doesn't):

1. **Find the *guidelines* page, not the marketing page.** Submission portal footer → "submission guidelines" / "author information" / "terms"; for publishers: the imprint's *authors* or *submissions* page; for contests: "rules" or "official rules" (the legally-operative document is usually the word *rules*, and it's where disclosure clauses hide). Screenshot or save-PDF *before* you draft your statement (policies change *while your story is in the queue* — dated evidence of *which* rules you filed under matters if a mid-queue rule-change email arrives).
2. **Ctrl-F the dialect:** "AI," "artificial intelligence," "generative," "machine learning," "ChatGPT"-as-verb (venues name tools when nervous), "disclose," "originality warranty." Read the *whole paragraph* around each hit — disclosure clauses live in *originality warranties* ("the work is your original creation…") more often than in labeled AI-sections; a silent AI-section with a strict originality warranty still *binds* — interpret silences against wishful readings (a venue that says nothing about AI but requires "entirely your original work" has *told you something*; whether assisted drafting breaches *that* wording is exactly the ambiguity you resolve by *asking*, next step).
3. **Check the *form* separately from the *guidelines*** (the hook's lesson: the 8-point clause under the upload button is a *fourth* document — rules page, guidelines page, portal checkboxes, and confirm-email template can each carry disclosure language; four surfaces, four reads).
4. **When silent or ambiguous: *ask in writing* — one email, specific, timestamped.** "Do you require disclosure of AI-assisted revision where all final prose was human-written? [your process in three clauses]" — *their reply is your policy* (file it in `research/venues/` with the date; a written answer beats any reading you could've done — and if they don't answer before the deadline, *disclose conservatively* under the broadest reasonable reading: over-disclosure's cost is one sentence).
5. **Re-check at submission *and* at contract/acceptance** (the mid-queue rule-change reality; also acceptance-time is when *contract riders* arrive — next section).

**The three-venue walkthrough** (the worked example compressed to its findings — one story, three simultaneous subs, the procedure run): *Venue A (magazine):* guidelines page — Ctrl-F "AI" → explicit prohibition *plus* form-checkbox requiring affirmation of no-gen-AI-use → **your process doesn't fit their rule → don't submit (route around: withdraw from consideration list, note in venues-file: "prohibitive — as-of date")** — the stance's third clause enacted without drama; *Venue B (press):* guidelines silent on AI; originality warranty in the contract draft says "your original work product" → **ask-in-writing** → editor's dated reply: "disclose assistance in a cover note; warranty covers final expression" → **cover-note sentence filed (your mid-length statement), proceed**; *Venue C (contest):* rules-page has a *required disclosure field* (character-limited) + portal checkbox + guidelines restatement → **three surfaces agree; fill field with short-form statement; screenshot everything pre-submission; save confirm-email.** Three venues, three behaviors, zero moral complexity — because *each was read at its own sources, dated, in writing* — versus the single behavior the under-checked writer defaults to: silence everywhere, discovered once, catastrophically.

## Copyright basics (with jurisdiction boxes)

The legal floor, kept to **official-source statements only** per the research protocol — with jurisdiction boxes as the outline's structure demands. **The framing everything else hangs on: copyright law is territorial** — rules below are *country-shaped*; your manuscript crosses borders, the *rights* don't travel with it automatically; when money, contracts, or registration enter, the lawyer-trigger (stated plainly in every box) is part of the advice, not an evasion of it.

**United States — the human-authorship baseline (C001).** The U.S. Copyright Office's registration guidance and its subsequent report on AI (Part 2, released January 29, 2025) state the operative rules: **copyright requires human authorship — purely AI-generated material is not copyrightable in the US**, and where a machine produced a work's traditional elements of authorship without sufficient human creative control, that material is excluded from protection; works *containing* AI-assisted material can be registered where the human contributions are sufficient, with **AI-generated portions disclosed and disclaimed** on the application (applicants must identify AI-generated content; the Office's guidance says prompts alone generally do not establish the necessary control over expression); human-authored elements remain protected, and selections/arrangements of AI outputs can qualify as human authorship of the compilation, *not* the underlying generated text itself. Appellate confirmation exists (the D.C. Circuit's 2025 decision in *Thaler v. Perlmutter* affirmed the human-authorship requirement on facts before it — full opinion at the court's official site). **Practical translation for your novel:** (1) *your* copyrightability rests on *your* human expression — the Parts II–III evidence trail is the point; (2) if you ever *register* (Ch 16's checklists touch registration timing), **disclose and disclaim AI-generated material as the guidance instructs** — accurate applications are the registration-side twin of venue-disclosure hygiene; (3) "the model wrote paragraph 3 unedited" = paragraph 3 unprotectable *as such* (your surrounding work unaffected); "the model proposed, I rewrote to my aims" = your paragraph, disclose per guidance as needed. *Confidence: high (official USCO guidance + report + appellate decision); perishable: guidance pages can be superseded — re-check USCO's AI-registration guidance page at use.* **This is information, not legal advice; for registration strategy on a mixed work, consult a US copyright attorney.**

> **Jurisdiction box — United Kingdom.** The UK's Copyright, Designs and Patents Act 1988 contains (in its original provisions) a *computer-generated works* regime — ss.9(3)/12(7) attributing authorship of qualifying computer-generated works to the *maker of the arrangements* for their generation — a *statutory* structure without direct US-equivalence, whose *interaction with modern generative tools in practice* (what counts as "the arrangements," how it meets publishing reality) sits in professional-practice territory this box doesn't adjudicate. **Operative advice:** UK-facing publication or contracts → read the statute's current text at legislation.gov.uk yourself *and* have counsel sort your fact-pattern; disclose per venue rules regardless (disclosure duty and copyrightability are *different questions* — a work can be non-infringing, venue-compliant, *and* still raise authorship-attribution questions the statute words carefully). *Confidence: statute existence/text high; practical application: explicitly out of scope.*

> **Jurisdiction box — European Union.** No single "AI-copyright article" to quote: copyright authorship doctrine lives substantially in member-state law (EU-harmonization reaches *exceptions* and *infringement*, not a uniform authorship code), while the **EU AI Act** (Regulation (EU) 2024/1689) imposes *transparency* obligations in other gears — notably marking/identification duties for certain AI-generated or manipulated content (deepfake-class disclosures) as the Act's rules phase in, with the details and application dates at EUR-Lex. **Operative advice:** EU distribution → (a) check the AI Act's transparency obligations *as currently in force* for your content class (official text + the Commission's guidance pages), (b) check the *member-state* your contract is centered in for authorship questions, (c) venue-disclosure as always. *Confidence: Regulation number/text high; obligation-scope-per-content-class: re-verify at official sources at use (phased application dates make single-sentence summaries dangerous).*

> **Jurisdiction box — Brazil.** For this book's purposes the verified floor: **LGPD — Lei nº 13.709/2018** (Lei Geral de Proteção de Dados Pessoais), in force since 2020 after the 2019 sanctions trigger, governs *personal-data processing* by natural and legal persons (official text: Planalto; regulator: ANPD) — which binds Brazilian writers *directly* where your process handles **dados pessoais de pessoas identificáveis**: pasting real individuals' private correspondence, health details, or identifying letters into AI tools is a *data-processing act* with LGPD duties attached (purpose, good faith, necessity — Art. 6 principles) layered on top of the privacy hygiene of Chapter 5 and the fiction-ethics of this chapter's next section. For *copyright/authorship* questions under Brazilian law and for any *national AI-regulation* status (legislative files evolve — check current status at Planalto/Câmara/Senado official trackers rather than trusting any summary *including this one*), consult a Brazilian attorney; this box deliberately states **no rule it hasn't sourced.** *Confidence: LGPD text/force high (official); other BR AI/copyright specifics: unverified by design — pointer-only, per protocol.*

**The lawyer sentence, finally earned:** across all four boxes, the honest professional advice is identical in shape — *the official text beats every summary (including this chapter's), your specific facts beat every general rule, and counsel exists for where those two meet.* Use this chapter to arrive at the lawyer's door *informed* (your process described, your venues listed, your questions specific) instead of *alarmed* — the fee buys the part that *cannot* be sourced generically: your case.

## People, privacy, contracts (and classrooms)

Four adjacent domains, kept practical:

**Real people as characters/input — the fiction-ethics core.** *Publishing* a novel shaped by a real person is lawful in ordinary circumstances (fictionalization is a *craft* tradition — courts and customs protect expressive work built from life); *feeding a real, identifiable person's private material into processing tools* engages the Chapter 5 privacy floor *and* — for identifiable individuals' personal data — data-protection law where applicable (LGPD box above; GDPR-class regimes elsewhere: same pointer-discipline). The operational rules this book enforces: **public facts and your lived observations = normal novelistic material; private records, medical/financial/sexual details, and correspondence = don't paste into tools (fictionalize the load-bearing specifics beyond recognition first, per Ch 5's rule, or don't use the material at all)**; **the living and *identifiable* in *thinly-veiled* form, in a small community, with private details in the text** = defamation-adjacent territory where *accuracy of malice-sensitive inventions* becomes counsel's question (the classic "everyone will know it's X" fact-pattern: talk to a lawyer *before* publication, not after the letters arrive); and **the stranger's dignity test** as the craft-floor: if the portrayal's *distinctiveness* relies on a real group's stereotypes (Ch 11's specificity audit), the fix is craft first — three individual traits or the marker comes off. None of this is new morality; it's Ch 5's paste-rules and Ch 11's representation-rules *pointed at named humans* — the place where they stop being style.

**Contracts and AI clauses.** Rights-and-warranties sections in publishing and agency contracts increasingly *address* AI-assisted works in some of these forms (the clause families to recognize): *warranties that the work is original / free of infringing material* (ask how your disclosed assistance interacts — a disclosure letter *precedes and informs* the warranty reading), *affirmative **representations about AI use*** (categories disclosed, thresholds stated — answer with your Appendix-F-style log's categories, not adjectives), *indemnities tied to provenance claims* (the clause that makes *inaccurate* disclosure expensive — hence: accurate process knowledge before signing), and *positive **AI-use permissions/restrictions*** (some contracts now *grant* limited assistance use or *restrict* it per format/territory — check against your actual process). **The procedure is the same ten-minute discipline as venue policies:** read the *whole* clause family, map it against your logged process (the three-length statement's *long form* is your contract-conversation script), request revisions or clarifications *in writing where the map mismatches*, and — above a material contract's value — **counsel reviews the rider** (the lawyer-sentence's contract edition: you bring the *process map*, they bring the *risk reading*). Never sign a representation about process you can't *substantiate from your logs*; never let a *sales* conversation's casualness ("we don't care about tools") overwrite the *signed* document's carefulness — the rider wins, the email trail is evidence, the hallway is nothing.

**Academic-integrity parallels** (why this paragraph exists: many readers' *first* disclosure habits formed at school, and transfer poorly). Universities, journals, and conferences run the same spectrum as venues — prohibition, disclosure-with-assessment, tool-neutral — with *their own* current policies (institutional offices of research integrity, publisher after-publisher policies for journals); the *transferable skills* from the procedure above: find the *operative rules document* (course syllabus = contract; submission system checkbox = portal-form layer; journal's author-guidelines = guidelines layer — the *four surfaces* pattern again), *ask when silent*, *disclose at the granularity asked*, *keep the record*. The parallel's deeper lesson for fiction writers: academic integrity culture treats *process provenance* as constitutive of the work's validity — fiction's publishing world is arriving at the same place through contracts and contest forms; writers who built provenance habits *before* the pressure arrived (Parts II–III, exactly) experience this shift as *paperwork*, and everyone else experiences it as the hook's email.

### Contracts 101: the clause families a working writer actually meets

The section on AI clauses needs its plain-language foundation — the *rest* of the contract anatomy around which those clauses orbit — because a rights-and-warranties paragraph read *alone* is how smart people sign bad deals (the rider's power is contextual: each clause *buys* something from the other side, and only the *whole map* shows the exchange-rates). The five families, at the literacy level this book can honestly provide (your counsel provides the rest, per the lawyer-sentence):

**Family 1 — Grant of rights (what you give, for how long, where).** The paragraph that *defines the deal*: which rights (print, audio, ebook, film, translation, serial), in which territories (world, or language-by-language), for what term (fixed years, "life of copyright," *reversion triggers* — the out-of-print/sales-threshold conditions under which rights *come home* — the family Ch 16 flagged as small-press contract-craft 101, now at reading-depth: **a grant without a clear reversion path is a permanent donation with paperwork** — the literacy-move: read the grant *backwards* first (find reversion, then read forward — the sequence that keeps the shiny advance-figure from anchoring your read of everything after it); AI-clause adjacency: none directly, but *everything* in Family 4-5 interprets *through* the grant's scope (a warranty about AI-assistance means different risk at "life of copyright, world" versus "five-year English-only" — the map's first rule: **rights first, or rights never.**

**Family 2 — Advance & royalties (the money mechanics).** Advance = pre-paid future royalties (earned-against — your royalties *don't flow* until the advance "earns out" — the mechanism behind every "modest advance" shape in Ch 16, stated mechanically now: *the advance IS your royalty until it isn't*); royalty rates differ by format (print's *percentage-of-list* convention vs. ebook's *percentage-of-net* convention — the two denominators' gap being why "30% ebook royalty" and "8% print royalty" aren't comparable numbers *until you know what they're percentages OF* — the literacy move: **for every rate, ask "percent of WHAT?"** — list price, net receipts, something else — the one question that collapses most royalty-confusion into arithmetic); escalations (rates rising at thresholds), reserves against returns (a withheld slice held back for bookstore returns' eventual accounting — the *quiet* subfamily: how much withheld, how long, reported-when — the clause where "why does my statement show sales I can't be paid for yet" gets answered *on paper or not at all*); AI-clause adjacency: none — but the **statements** (royalty reports) are where any AI-related *audit rights* in Family 5 would eventually *bite* — read the reporting clause while you're in the neighborhood.

**Family 3 — Editorial & production control (whose call).** Cover design, title, schedule, edits' *approval semantics* (consultation vs. approval vs. publisher-final — three *different* contracts wearing one friendly word: "your input matters" maps to any of them, and only the *document's* words count — the Ch 15 sales-conversation rule's structural home: **consultation = they decide after hearing you; approval = you can refuse once, with reasons; publisher-final = the paragraph you accepted by signing it** — the literacy move: find which of the three each key decision *actually* got — title and cover being the two where writers *believe* approval and *signed* consultation, the gap between belief and paper living exactly one clause-family over from the smile in the room); reversion-adjacent: manuscripts *rejected after acceptance* (yes, that's a thing — "we reserve the right not to publish" clauses — where the grant's scope meets *their* delivery obligations — check what happens to your rights if they *take the book and shelve it*: the Family-1 reversion triggers reading differently for *never-published* than *out-of-print* — two *distinct* homecomings, two *distinct* clauses, both worth finding *before* signing, not after the silence).

**Family 4 — Warranties & representations (what you *swear*).** The AI-clause *native ground* (already covered in depth above): originality, non-infringement, *now frequently*: assistance-disclosure representations — read here only to add its literacy-reframe: **warranties are *forward-looking law*** (they convert *your process history* into *contractual liability* — which is precisely why this book's provenance logs and the three-length statement aren't *bureaucracy*: they're the *evidence base* a warranty-reading depends on — the person who can *substantiate* "human-revised, machine-labor disclosed as per Rider C" from session-logs and AI-use rows *answers warranty questions like a professional*; the person *without* the trail negotiates the same paragraph *on vibes* — the contract-family's whole argument for Part II's ledger-discipline arriving four chapters late, right on schedule); indemnities (family-cousin: *you pay their costs* if the warranty proves false — the clause that makes *inaccurate* disclosure expensive in *their* currency — hence: warranty-reads always run *against your actual logs*, never against your *memory of* your process).

**Family 5 — The operational fine-print (assignments, audits, termination, reversion-mechanics).** The family nobody reads until they need: *assignment* (can they sell the contract to someone else — your reversion-terms travel with *whichever company now holds it* — check the assignee-obligations language), *audit rights* (whose books you may inspect to verify royalties — the Family-2 statement's enforcement mirror), *termination* (what breaches end *what*, and what happens to *rights already granted* — the asymmetry-tells: termination that ends *their* obligations but leaves your grant intact is a door built for one direction), and *reversion-mechanics* (how homecoming *actually executes*: notice periods, file-formats-of-return, non-compete-survival clauses — the fine print *around* Family 1's trigger, where "your rights revert" becomes either *two clean emails* or *eighteen months of defined process* — the literacy move: **reversion-mechanics read aloud at signing**, because this paragraph is where future-you's *relationship with the whole deal* gets conducted, and doing it *then*, alone, with a contract's full emotional weight, is exactly the situation the lawyer-sentence's *scheduled* version exists to prevent). The five families' closing pattern — *rights first, percent-of-what second, whose-call third, what-you-swear fourth, the fine print fifth* — a reading order, not a priority order (all five *bind*; the sequence exists because **each family's terms *interpret* the next** — a warranty's risk reads through the grant's scope; a royalty's meaning reads through its denominator; a control-clause's *consultation* reads through the termination-clause's *consequences* — contracts are *arguments*, and arguments have *reading orders*: yours is now the paragraph above, the AI-clause section you've already run, and — through the door marked on every page of this chapter — counsel, arriving *informed*, with the map folded open to the page that needs their eyes.)

### The quarterly provenance review (scheduling the stance)

The stance's instruments (dossier, statements, logs) were built; this section *schedules* them, because provenance rot is *time-shaped*: policies expire, statements drift from evolved processes, screenshots age past relevance — and every rot-pattern this book has catalogued (stale style sheets, un-versioned cards, un-audited instrument-rhythms) says the same thing: **without a calendar slot, maintenance becomes a thing you *remember at the worst moment*.** The quarterly review, specified at Ch 7's audit-rhythm granularity (45 minutes, same Sunday slot family as the monthly audits — the *quarterly* cadence chosen because venue policies and contract-templates update on *roughly* that weather-pattern while your *own* process evolves slower — monthly would waste the slots, annual would let rot outrun detection — and the review inherits the audit's standing format: **instrument rows first, narrative never**):

**Rows (the checklist that IS the review):** **(1) Dossier sweep** — `research/venues/` rows older than ~12 months: re-open each guidelines page *now, dated*, update posture-or-tick the re-verified box (the row's `as-of` field *is* the alarm; the sweep *is* the response — ten venues ≈ fifteen minutes at Try's practiced pace — *this row alone* justifies the quarter); **(2) Statement-to-process drift** — your three-length statements read *against* your current actual process (new mode adopted? new tool category? pass-system changed? — a statement claiming "final prose human-revised" while your practice evolved some new category of machine-touch = the representation *quietly aging into inaccuracy* — the fix's shape: **one paragraph updated per drifted clause, version-dated, old version archived** (append-only, like everything with a shelf-life worth respecting — the statement's *version history* itself becoming provenance-evidence of your attention to accuracy); **(3) Contract-map refresh** — Family-1–5's literacy notes re-read *if* any submission's advancing (active-negotiation quarters run this row *per-opportunity* instead: the review's flexible child — no pending contracts = row ticks "none active" in ten seconds, guilt-free; the row exists so the *first* contract-notice doesn't *become* the review, panicked and 1 a.m.-shaped); **(4) Log-integrity spot-check** — one random week of session-logs + decisions rows *audited for the appendix's own standard* (would these rows *substantiate* a warranty answer? — the self-as-subject discipline: **your provenance trail gets the same quarterly inspection you'd give any instrument claiming to be evidence** — three rows read, gaps noted, cause-rows filed if the trail's been thinning: the FAQ from Ch 7's neighboring territory — *logs kept for capacity* double as *logs kept for accountability*, and this row keeps the double-duty *honest*); **(5) Perishables-bridge** — Appendix E's table *consulted* (not re-run: that's pre-publication's job — this row just checks: any *new* claim-rows added since the last review that lack source+dates? — the register's **append-hygiene** caught quarterly rather than at the worst possible deadline; **(6) Calendaring the next one** — the review's *own* re-appearance scheduled before you close the laptop (the calendar-closes-the-loop rule from Ch 16, applied to *this* system: a provenance review that doesn't book itself becomes a *nice intention with a folder* — the review's deepest pattern is its *boringness*: six rows, forty-five minutes, one dated-tick per instrument — the stance from the chapter's opening *operationalized into maintenance*, which is what "hygiene" meant all along: **not a virtue, a schedule — not confessed, performed — quarterly, dated, boring, and the reason the hook's withdrawal-email finds *someone else* in its inbox for the rest of your working life.**

## Worked example: checking three venues for one story

The procedure at full resolution (Example A's "Low Tide" going out — the *walkthrough structure* you'll copy; findings abridged to what teaches):

**Day 1, Venue A (the prohibitive magazine).** Guidelines page saved PDF (`venues/A-guidelines-2026-09-2X.pdf`); Ctrl-F "AI" → hit in originality section: *explicit non-acceptance of AI-generated or AI-assisted writing* + portal shows the affirmation checkbox (surface 3 — found *because* the hunt checked the form separately) + confirm-email template read (surface 4, via last year's confirmation saved in `venues/`): restates affirmation. **Verdict: prohibitive → do not submit → `venues/` row: "A · prohibitive · as-of 2026-09-2X · screenshot ✔".** The route-around logged *without* hand-wringing (the stance's third clause — and a *data point for C003's appendix note*: divergence documented with a dated artifact, the register's evidence-chain satisfied by *your* hunt, not a remembered blog post).

**Day 1, Venue B (the silent press + the written ask).** Submissions page: no "AI" hits anywhere (Ctrl-F across guidelines + FAQ); contract *sample* (linked for authors — good sign: press publishes its template) carries originality warranty: "Work is your original work product." → **ask-in-writing sent** (the exact email saved): subject "Process question — submission of assisted-revised work"; body: three-clause process description (brainstorming assistance logged; final prose human-revised with voice anchors; continuity checks machine-assisted) + one question (disclosure desired? does warranty contemplate this process?). **Day 4, reply received (filed):** "Please include a short cover-note disclosure as you've described; warranty concerns final expression — your description is fine." → **verdict: proceed-with-disclosure → mid-length statement locked from their reply's language** (the reply *becomes* your statement's frame — venues tell you their vocabulary; use theirs back). Row: "B · disclosure-via-cover-note · reply 2026-09-2X filed ✔".

**Day 2, Venue C (the three-surface contest).** Rules page (PDF'd): §7 requires "describe any generative-AI assistance" in the entry form's *character-limited field* + guidelines §3 restates + portal checkbox "I have disclosed all assistance per §7" (found mid-form — the hook's 8-point-type species, now *surfaced by procedure*) → **short statement drafted to the field's limit** (~500 chars: categories of assistance + human-revision + no-gen-prose-in-submission claim — *accurate to the actual process*, no padding), **screenshots of all three surfaces taken pre-submission, confirmation email auto-filed.** Verdict: proceed-with-field-disclosure. Row: "C · field+checkbox · 3 surfaces ✔ · screenshots dated." **Three verdicts, one afternoon's procedure, zero improvisation** — and the `venues/` folder now *is* C003's personal dossier: divergence *documented*, each venue's rule *dated*, each answer *sourced to the venue itself* (the research protocol's official-source rule aimed at *terms of art* instead of statistics — the same discipline, another domain; Appendix E's source-priority table already had a row for "publisher/contest policy → the publisher's own current page (dated access)" — this walkthrough is that row *executed*).

*The last beat belongs to the authorship trail:* when B's reply asked nothing further and C's field accepted the statement, *no new writing was needed* — the three-length statement (next section) had been drafted from **the logs that Parts II–III kept regardless of any venue** (session modes, decisions rows, pass logs, the Appendix-F categories). Disclosure, executed well, is *retrieval* — which is precisely why the golden rule's paperwork half ("you decide, AI proposes" — and *you keep the ledger*) was always going to end this chapter instead of starting a panic in it.

## Exercises

- **Try (15 min):** Find the AI policy of *one* contest, magazine, or platform you might realistically use within the year — run the four-surface hunt (guidelines page → rules word → portal form → confirm-email template) and produce the row: *venue · posture (prohibitive / disclosure-required / written-ask-needed / silent-with-warranty) · as-of date · artifact saved or link dated*. Fifteen minutes is the *target pace* of a skill you'll use forever — if it took longer, note which surface hid (the hiding surface teaches the next hunt). Save it in `research/venues/` — you've just begun your C003 dossier.

- **Stretch (1–2 h):** Draft your personal disclosure statement in **three lengths** — *short* (~500 characters: checkbox/field — categories + human-final-responsibility only), *mid* (~150 words: cover-note — the three-clause process + invitation to ask), *long* (~400 words: contract/rider conversations — categories itemized against your logs' actual data: modes used, passes run, what was machine-drafted vs. human-final, pointer to your AI-use log's existence) — each length **factual to *your* real process** (no inflation, no minimization — write them *from* your session-log/decisions rows so every clause is *substantiatable*, the contract-warranty rule applied to your own sentence), each fact-checked against one venue reply or policy *for tone-matching* (B's vocabulary style: theirs-back); then *run one full venue check* (the Try's row — extended: draft the actual statement *that venue's way* and file it as `venues/X-statement-ready` so your next submission's disclosure is *retrieval*, not composition). Two hours well spent converts an ethics chapter from *anxiety* into *templates*.

- **Project (ongoing):** Maintain `research/venues/` as a living policy file — one row per venue ever considered (posture + date + artifact), statements drafted stored *per venue's granularity*, re-check dates noted (annual review of rows marked "checked >12 months ago" — policies rot; your dossier rots with them unless the review is *scheduled*: add it to the quarterly review Ch 7's audits already own — one more instrument, same rhythm); contracts arriving → clause-family mapping against your long statement *before* signing conversations (the process-map preparedness rule); any *real-person* character question in your current draft → run the people-rules paragraph against it *now* (identification risk + private-material paste audit — the one review in this book best done *before* rather than after); and the standing posture: when a rule surprises you, the *first* action is the written ask (B's email template saved for reuse), the *second* is submission — never the third thing (silent hope). The dossier's endgame is boring in the best way: by Ch 16's checklists, disclosure appears as a *pre-filled line item* — because your provenance was always in your logs, and this chapter only taught you where to *point* them.

## Pitfalls

> PITFALL — **Assuming the rules ("everyone does it" ≠ allowed).** *Symptom:* submission decisions made from forum vibes, writers'-group anecdotes, or a *different* venue's permissiveness ("Clarkesworld-*style* markets don't care anymore" — venues aren't styles, they're *documents with dates*); disclosure skipped because the process "felt normal"; contracts signed because the call was friendly; the deep version: *policy-surfaced-by-panic* — discovering a rule only at the rejection/withdrawal/claim letter, then interpreting it in the worst possible light at 1 a.m. with no artifacts. *Why it happens:* **normalization drift** (assistance became habitual in your *studio* — you measurably forgot which parts were even *assistance-worthy* of disclosure without your logs — the forgetting is invisible until a form *asks*, which is why the logs were never bureaucracy); **ambiguity-optimism** (silence read as permission when silence actually means *ask* — the motivated reading of an ambiguous warranty is *always* the permissive one, by definition of motivation — hence the written-ask rule *mechanizing* what intuition will always distort); **category collapse** (plagiarism-panic generalizes to disclosure-panic and vice versa — the smear from the chapter's opening: writers either hide *routine* assistance as if it were theft, or *rationalize* wholesale unattributed generation as if venues owed them assumptions — both are the *same* error: no working vocabulary for the actual distinctions (plagiarism / provenance fraud / assistance) the chapter installed); and **artifact-absence** ("I didn't screenshot the rules page" — mid-queue policy changes then become *their word against your memory*, and memory always loses contractually). *Fix (the procedure, one more time as a checklist — it *is* the fix):* (1) **posture-before-submit, every time:** four-surface hunt → dated row in the dossier → verdict from the *document's own words* (prohibitive / disclosure-required / ask / silent-warranty) — fifteen minutes, Try's target pace; (2) **silence → written ask → conservative answer if silence persists** (B's template; over-disclosure's one sentence beats under-discovery's withdrawal); (3) **three-length statement maintained from *actual logs*** (the Stretch's drafts, refreshed at each quarterly review — a statement that can't be substantiated row-by-row isn't a disclosure, it's a hope); (4) **artifacts at every layer** (PDFs/screenshots dated at *submission*, saved at *acceptance* — the dossier's evidence-chain feeding C003's own re-verification the way Appendix E prescribes: your hunts *are* the counter-evidence-gathering the register asks for); (5) **vocabulary discipline in your own communications** (say which of the three species you mean — "disclosed assistance" ≠ "plagiarized," and *both* directions of the conflation get *corrected once, politely, in writing* — public discourse will keep smearing the categories; your records and your sentences *won't*); (6) **contract/warranty map before signing** (long statement × clause family × counsel where value warrants — the lawyer-sentence *scheduled*, not summoned); (7) **the quarterly dossier review** (policies rot — dates expire — the review lives alongside Ch 7's audits because *staleness is an instrument-maintenance problem, not a morality problem*). *Prevention (steady state):* the whole chapter collapses into four habits once internalized — **posture-hunt before every submission; ask-on-silence; statements-from-logs; artifacts-dated-everywhere** — plus the identity-level anchor the ETHICS NOTE set: *say what happened, exactly, always — boredom as the target emotional register for every disclosure you'll ever file.* The golden rule's final office: you decided, the machine proposed, **and the ledger proves it in any room that asks.**

Related failure for Appendix C: no F-number (policy failures are *process* failures caught by the dossier's instruments, not prose-failures) — cross-refs: Ch 5's privacy hygiene (paste-rules feed this chapter's people-rules), Ch 7's audit rhythm (quarterly review slot), Ch 13's ETHICS NOTE (sim-vs-human provenance — the same disclosure channel protected at content scale), register rows C001/C003/C007 (this chapter's *own* perishable set — re-verified at pre-publication per Appendix E's schedule).

## Checklist

> CHECKLIST — You're ready to move on when:
> - [ ] You can find any venue's policy in under 10 minutes using the four-surface hunt — *and you've done it once for real* (Try's dated row in `research/venues/`, artifact saved).
> - [ ] Your disclosure statement exists in three lengths (short/mid/long), each factually mapped to your logs' actual data, tone-matched to at least one real venue's reply — retrieval-ready.
> - [ ] You can state the three-species distinction (plagiarism / provenance fraud / AI-assisted authorship) in one breath — and name which discipline handles each (craft+attribution norms / venue rules / copyright baseline + your authorship trail).
> - [ ] You know which jurisdiction box applies to you (US / UK / EU / BR — and "your contract's center of gravity" if you publish across lines) — with each box's *official-source pointer* saved, not memorized, and the lawyer-sentence's trigger list identified for *your* upcoming situation (registration? material contract? thin-veil character?).
> - [ ] People-rules and contract-clause-family have both been *run against your current project* (real-person audit done; any warranties you'd sign are mapped to your long statement) — and you can say in one sentence what this chapter is *not* (information, not legal advice — the scope warning earned, not appended).

## Key takeaways

- **Three species, one vocabulary:** plagiarism (someone else's expression, unattributed), provenance fraud (process violations against a venue's disclosure rule), AI-assisted authorship (your aims + machine labor + your responsibility, *evidenced* by the Parts II–III trail) — confusion between them is the public smear *and* the private panic; precise words dissolve both.
- **Disclosure is retrieval, not confession:** statements drafted from logs at three lengths, submitted at whatever granularity each rule asks, never discovered for you — over-disclosure costs a sentence; under-disclosure costs the slot (and mid-queue rule-changes cost the *screenshot*).
- **C003 is the operational thesis: venues differ, policies rot, the four-surface hunt with dated artifacts is the only reliable method** — rules pages, guidelines, portal forms, and confirm-emails each *bind separately*; silence means *ask in writing* (their reply becomes your policy), prohibitions mean *route around*, and your dossier *is* the dated evidence-chain the claim register wants.
- **The copyright floor is territorial and human-authorship-shaped:** US baseline (official USCO guidance/report + appellate confirmation: purely AI-generated material uncopyrightable; disclose/disclaim on registration; prompts alone ≠ control; consult counsel for your facts), with honest jurisdiction boxes for UK (statutory computer-generated-works regime — text at legislation.gov.uk + counsel), EU (member-state authorship + AI Act transparency as-in-force at EUR-Lex), and BR (LGPD floor verified at Planalto — personal-data processing binds your *process*; everything else pointer-only) — **official text beats every summary including this chapter's.**
- **People, contracts, and classrooms run the same drill at higher stakes:** private material about identifiable humans never pasted (fictionalize or don't), thin-veils before publication get counsel, contract clause-families get mapped against your substantiable logs before signing — provenance habits formed *before* the pressure arrive as paperwork; everyone else meets them as the hook's email. Four habits close the chapter: **posture-hunt, ask-on-silence, statements-from-logs, artifacts-dated** — boredom as the target feeling of every disclosure you'll ever file.


# Chapter 16 — Publishing Paths & Your Writing Practice

## Opening hook

Two writers, identical manuscripts — same polish, same genre fit, same honest quality: a literary short-story collection, one of them (we'll let Example A's "Low Tide" stand in for both). **Writer A** queries agents for eleven months: forty letters, four full-request rejections with encouraging tails, one partial that dies in an R&R-that-never-arrives, then a smaller press's open-window answer — an offer on a modest advance, eighteen months after Query One, with the publisher controlling cover, schedule, and price while A gets industry distribution and the prestige ladder's first rung. **Writer B** files an LLC-light, buys a commissioned cover, pays an editor for one pass, assembles an interior, publishes on the platforms in eight weeks — and now spends evenings doing *everything*: mailing-list hygiene, ad budgets that eat the modest royalties, price experiments, review-gathering that lives inside each platform's rules (Ch 15's four-surface hunt aimed at *algorithms*), earning slowly, compounding slowly, owning every lever.

Neither story is the true one. That's the point of showing them side by side: **the path doesn't change the manuscript — it changes *time, control, capital, and who bears the risk* — and those are *goals* questions, not quality questions.** The rejection culture tells beginners quality is the gate; the hustle culture tells them speed is the edge; both are selling something (validation, courses, comfort) and both conveniently skip the actual decision: *what do you want this writing life to optimize for, in the next three years specifically?* This final chapter of the book gives you three things and then lets you go: an honest map of the four main paths (with the trade-offs as *arithmetic you run yourself* — no stale market statistics will appear here; anything numeric gets dated or generalized, per the book's standing rule), two checklists (querying, self-publishing) you can actually execute, and — the chapter's real payload — **the 90-day plan** that converts everything you've built (voice, process, finished pages, provenance) into a practice that outlives this book. By the end: paths scored against *your* goals, week 1 scheduled, one community step chosen — and a closing letter from a book that has been talking to you for sixteen chapters about its favorite subject: your next sentence.

## Learning objectives

By the end of this chapter, you will be able to:

- [ ] Compare traditional, indie, serial, and small-press paths honestly — against your goals, not someone else's career.
- [ ] Run a querying or self-publishing checklist without outsourcing the decisions.
- [ ] Build a sustainable writing practice with a concrete 90-day plan.
- [ ] Measure progress beyond word counts — and find your next community.

## The paths, honestly
